Friday, November 13, 2026

arXiv:2611.15957 §

The Curious Case of Anapole Dark Matter at LZ: Minimal, Elastic and Testable

Sourav Bera et al.

A Majorana fermion whose only electromagnetic coupling is a hypercharge anapole moment, \(a_\chi\,\bar\chi\gamma^\mu\gamma^5\chi\,\partial^\nu B_{\mu\nu}\), fits LZ230616 with purely elastic scattering. The interaction reduces to the NREFT operators \(\mathcal{O}_8\) and \(\mathcal{O}_9\). Their momentum dependence keeps the spectrum falling slowly at high recoil, which compensates for the halo-tail suppression. The fit is a binned Poisson likelihood over LZ’s public 2.84 t·yr data, with three S1c windows of 32 \(\delta\) bins each. The background is fixed to LZ’s post-fit model, and the signal templates come from rescaling LZ’s \(\mathcal{L}_{10}\) templates with NEST. Local significance from toys rises from 1.8σ at 100 GeV to 2.9σ at 300 GeV and saturates near 3.1σ above 500 GeV. The thermal-relic contour crosses the best-fit line at \(a_\chi \simeq 1.05\times10^{-6}\) GeV\(^{-2}\) and \(m_\chi \simeq 300\) GeV, or 215–422 GeV within the 68% band. That point is allowed by an ATLAS 139 fb\(^{-1}\) monojet recast and by white-dwarf heating limits.

Solar neutrinos are not computed here. The authors cite a celestial-probes study that included them and state that the white-dwarf limits are the most stringent in this mass range.

The prediction is sharp. At the full LZ exposure of 15.3 t·yr, the benchmarks reach 2.6–3.2σ (Asimov), mostly from the 250–500 phd window, which is nearly background-free and does not contain the observed event. XLZD would reach ≳9σ (11.7σ at 300 GeV).

Caveats: - Low predicted count where the event sits. At current exposure the model predicts only 0.064 events in the 500–600 phd window, against a background of 0.0097. - EFT validity. The EFT sits near its unitarity limit at LHC energies. - UV completions are strained. A tree-level vector completion needs \(\epsilon g_\chi \sim \mathcal{O}(1)\), which implies a strongly coupled dark sector. A loop completion needs charged states below about 100 GeV.

arXiv:2611.16689 §

Dark matter evolution in stellar objects: A Boltzmann transport approach

Yen-Hsun Lin et al.

The classical isothermal treatment of solar capture fails badly when nuclear scattering is rare. The method orbit-averages the Boltzmann equation into a master equation on an \((E, L)\) grid of 192×48 cells. It evolves capture, orbit-to-orbit scattering, evaporation and s-wave annihilation together, and is valid because there are far fewer than one collision per orbit.

For SD scattering at 100 GeV and \(\sigma_{\chi p}=10^{-50}\) cm², captured DM never thermalizes. Its central density ends up more than six orders of magnitude below the isothermal value, because freshly captured particles sit on wide orbits, often reaching beyond the solar surface. The classical treatment overestimates the annihilation rate by more than 2× below about \(10^{-46}\) cm² (SD) or \(10^{-49}\) cm² (SI). SD is suppressed much more than SI because hydrogen transfers energy poorly to heavy DM. The evaporation mass matches the classical value down to about \(10^{-47}\) cm² and falls below it at smaller cross sections. Orbit anisotropy inherited from the halo changes the annihilation rate by less than 4%.

For the inelastic Higgsino at 1.1 TeV, the result goes the other way. The full evolution confirms the equilibrium relation \(2\Gamma_A \simeq C_c\). Including the thermal motion of solar nuclei opens capture channels that are closed for nuclei at rest. With the IceCube 10-year \(W^+W^-\) limit (\(\Gamma_A < 2.9\times10^{19}\) s\(^{-1}\)), the bound becomes \(\delta \gtrsim 592\) keV, compared with 552 keV for nuclei at rest. The heavier elements earlier studies added (Zn, Ge, Pb, U) shift this by only about 0.1 keV.

Caveats: - Conservative channel choice. The neutrino signal uses only \(W^+W^-\), which has a branching ratio of 0.54. - Simplified Sun and grid. The Sun is static, planets are ignored, and grid choices move \(\Gamma_A\) by \(\mathcal{O}(1)\). - Limited scope. Cross sections are taken as velocity-independent SI/SD. The suppression results therefore cannot be carried over directly to momentum-dependent operators such as the anapole above.

arXiv:2611.16347 §

Multi-approach SMEFT analyses of top-quark associated production in leptonic final states with ATLAS

ATLAS Collaboration

Abstract only. ATLAS fits 33 dimension-six Wilson coefficients simultaneously across nine processes using 140 fb\(^{-1}\) of Run 2 data: - \(t\bar t\gamma\), \(t\bar tZ\), \(t\bar tW\), \(t\bar tH\), \(t\bar tt\bar t\) - \(tZq\), \(tWZ\), \(tHq\), \(tHW\)

The operators cover top–boson, four-quark and top–lepton interactions, including complex coefficients. Object definitions are harmonized across processes, and detector-level SMEFT effects are treated consistently. No significant deviation from the SM is found, and 95% intervals are set.

The methodological result is the comparison of two event categorizations: generic object counting versus orthogonal regions targeted at individual processes. The targeted design gives the best sensitivity for most coefficients. The abstract does not give the size of the gain, the coefficients for which the generic categories do as well or better, or any numerical limits.

Wednesday, November 11, 2026

arXiv:2611.13629 §

Where does the neutrino Mass Ordering preference come from?

Pedro A. N. Machado et al.

The combined reactor + \(\nu_\mu\)-disappearance fit disfavors IO at 1.9σ (CL\(_s\), pseudo-experiments), but the pieces disagree. JUNO’s 207 days alone favor NO by \(\Delta\chi^2=1.84\). Forcing JUNO from its low global minimum (\(1.96\times10^{-3}\) eV² in NO) onto Daya Bay’s branch near \(2.5\times10^{-3}\) costs 5.6 units in NO and 4.0 in IO, so reactors alone give \(T_{\rm MO}=0.21\), which is no preference. Each \(\nu_\mu\) experiment pulls a different way. Reactor+NOvA disfavors IO at 1.4σ, while reactor+T2K disfavors NO at 0.8σ. The T2K–NOvA gap in \(|\Delta m^2_{31}|\) is about 3.5% (~1σ), comparable to the 1.6–3.2% \(\Delta m^2_{ee}\)–\(\Delta m^2_{\mu\mu}\) separation that distinguishes the orderings. In a stress test, sliding T2K onto NOvA’s best fit gives a 1.8σ IO rejection, while the reverse gives a 1.7σ NO rejection. The \(\cos\delta_{\rm CP}\) term contributes at most 0.4%, so it cannot explain the gap. Asimov projections stall the reactor contribution: 1500 more JUNO days yield 2.8σ, and a perfect reactor measurement asymptotes at 2.9σ.

Why it matters. Past ~3σ this channel is limited by \(\nu_\mu\) energy reconstruction at the percent level. That is the target for today’s interaction-modeling papers, and the same requirement applies to HK. DUNE’s matter-effect route sidesteps it.

Caveats. The shift exercise simply sums \(\chi^2\) curves and ignores the experiments’ shared \(\delta_{\rm CP}/\theta_{23}\) dependence. The known appearance-channel disagreement may leak into the \(\Delta m^2\) values. The statistics also matter: naive \(\sqrt T\) would claim a 3.1σ IO tension where toys give 1.8σ.

arXiv:2611.14473 §

Running out of Field Range: Nonlinear Sourcing of Light Scalars

Abhishek Banerjee et al.

For a pNGB with periodic potential and coupling, matter induces \(m_\rho^2\propto dQ\rho/f\) and drives \(\theta\to\pi/2\). This caps the sourced field at \(\pi f/2\) and suppresses the local linear coupling as \(\cos\theta\). For the photon coupling the Earth saturates at \(d_e\simeq2.2\times10^{-9}(f/10\,{\rm MeV})\), about \(10^4\) below the MICROSCOPE linear limit. The two become comparable only near \(f\sim100\) GeV, so existing bounds sit outside their own validity regime. At \(f=10\) MeV the recast results are:

  • MICROSCOPE: at orbit \(\cos\theta\simeq0.16\), and the limit relaxes to \(d_e\lesssim2.8\), a factor \(7\times10^4\) weaker. It closes again near \(3\times10^9\) as the satellite walls screen the field.
  • Electron and nucleon couplings: \(d_{m_e}\lesssim170\) and \(d_{m_N}\lesssim6\), weakened by \(2\times10^5\) and \(2\times10^6\).
  • Eöt-Wash uranium test: develops a vertical wall below \(m_0=3\times10^{-11}\) eV once the air is sourced. MICROSCOPE loses reach above a few×\(10^{-12}\) eV, leaving an unconstrained mass window between the two.
  • Stellar bounds: linear cooling bounds vanish because cores also saturate. Only quadratic pair emission survives, e.g. SN 1987A gives \(d_e\lesssim4\times10^9\).

A ground–ISS clock comparison measures field values rather than gradients. With ACES Cs/H-maser it would reach \(d_e\gtrsim4\times10^4\) above \(1/h_{\rm ISS}\), with a ceiling near \(10^6\) set by the ISS’s own sourcing. A \(^{229}\)Th clock would reach \(\sim3\times10^{-2}\).

Caveats. The upper edges depend on geometry. The lab rock opens a notch in the uranium exclusion, the satellite is a toy model, and LAGEOS–Lunar is ambiguous at this \(f\). Naturalness also needs a \(\mathbb Z_N\) to avoid vacuum sourcing.

arXiv:2611.13617 §

Self-interacting dark matter and neutrino masses from MeV-scale lepton-number breaking

Manoranjan Dutta

One MeV-scale L-breaking vev generates \(\mu_S\) for the inverse seesaw and a pseudo-Dirac splitting for DM. A structural result follows: any central coupling satisfies \(|g|\simeq\Delta m_\chi/(2v_\phi)\), so a vev-generated splitting cannot support strong inelastic SIDM. In the viable symmetric regime, \(\Delta m_\chi\simeq2g_{11}v_\phi\) is tens of MeV and scattering is elastic.

With \(\alpha_{11}\) fixed by the relic density (decoupled, hotter dark sector) and exact partial waves including Majorana statistics, the fit is:

  • Main band: \(m_s\simeq1.9\,{\rm MeV}\times(m_\chi/{\rm GeV})\), with best fit \(m_\chi=7.6\) GeV, \(m_s=14.6\) MeV and \(\chi^2/{\rm dof}=2.04\). The data’s own floor is ~1.9, since a free power law gets 1.92.
  • Resonant islands: appear at higher mass.
  • Overall range: 3–70 GeV at 95% CL, with \(m_s\) = 6–28 MeV.

The mediator decays to \(\nu\nu\) in roughly 40 ms at a rate fixed by \(\sum m_\nu^2\). That lifetime and SN 1987A confine \(v_\phi\) to roughly 130–390 MeV. NO is preferred because its \(\nu_3\)-dominated couplings weaken the SN bound by \(F=5.7\), while IO (\(F\simeq1\)) forces \(m_s\lesssim6\) MeV, against \(N_{\rm eff}\).

Why it matters. It is a concrete NO-predicting model, landing as Machado shows how fragile the current oscillation NO hint is. CMB-S4-level \(N_{\rm eff}\) would probe \(m_s\lesssim11\) MeV.

Caveats. The 70 GeV cap depends on thermal history; it falls to ~60 GeV at a common temperature. The SN treatment is one-zone and ignores flavor conversion in the envelope.

(Full texts for arXiv:2611.13833 and arXiv:2611.13184 were not supplied, so they are not expanded here.)

Tuesday, November 10, 2026

arXiv:2611.12265 §

Beam-beam effects on measurements at electron-positron colliders: LEP neutrino counting and Higgs factories

Brendon Madison

The bias is real but small in \(N_\nu\); the error-bar argument is the real claim. FeeBL, a Guinea-Pig++ descendant, reproduces the analytic field-integration Bhabha deflections at LEP: inner 13.12±0.08 vs 13.09 μrad, outer 11.49±0.06 vs 11.49 μrad. The published Guinea-Pig values sit 2.2±0.5% and 2.6±0.6% low. This moves the acceptance correction from −0.10425% to −0.10671% and shifts \(N_\nu\) by only +0.0002. The author cannot identify the origin of the deficit without the original settings.

The error-bar change is a separate, statistical argument. The four LEP determinations scatter less than their quoted errors allow, with a χ² lower-tail probability of 2.05%. Rescaling only the experiment-specific uncertainties by a common factor gives σ = 0.00401, with a 68% interval of [0.00391, 0.00485]. The paper explicitly says this needs each experiment’s uncertainty budget to be redone before anyone can defend quoting it. The suspected culprit is the \(\sigma^0_{\rm had}\)–\(R_\ell\) correlation.

The paper also does two other things: - Beam-pair fixes. It corrects implementation defects in Guinea-Pig++ v1.2.2 pair production. The Born normalization is 1.319% low, small-angle sampling is truncated, and virtual-virtual Landau–Lifshitz collisions are dropped. - Monochromatization. Opposite horizontal dispersion narrows the ILC/LCF and HALHF peaks about 2× at constant luminosity. It reaches sub-MeV FWHM, but only at about 0.07% of nominal luminosity. FCC-ee at |D| = 175 mm is still at 57 MeV FWHM, roughly 14× wider than 4 MeV.

The ~10 m figure for FCC-ee is an extrapolation from a rigid-Gaussian model. That model already misses the FCC-ee simulation by 55% in luminosity and 22% in width. Convergence is explicitly not claimed anywhere in the paper.

arXiv:2611.11328 §

Observation of a strange J^P=1⁻ resonance K^*_1(1870) in χ_c1→K⁺K⁻η^' decays

BESIII Collaboration et al.

A new \(1^-\) state appears in \(K\eta'\) in BESIII’s ψ(2S)→γχ\(_{c1}\) data. The sample is 2.71×10⁹ ψ(2S), with η′ reconstructed in both ππγ and ππη. The state is required beyond the PDG-based amplitude set, with M = 1869.0±14.6±18.6 MeV and Γ = 167.3±34.0±24.2 MeV. Its significance is 11.0σ (statistical) for \(1^-\), against 3.2σ for \(0^+\) and 3.7σ for \(2^+\). The product branching fraction is (1.48±0.30±0.33)×10⁻⁵, and B(χ\(_{c1}\)→K⁺K⁻η′) = (7.90±0.48)×10⁻⁴ supersedes the earlier BESIII value.

Why it matters: this is the strangeness slot of the \(1^{-(+)}\) nonet that the π₁(1600) and η₁(1855) candidates point to. One scenario predicts a strange hybrid at 1.83–1.90 GeV that couples to \(K\eta'\). Quark-model \(3^3S_1\) expectations sit near 1.95 GeV with \(K\eta'\) suppressed, or else are implausibly broad.

Caveats: - The quoted significance is statistical only. - Several neighbours are fixed to PDG values, including K(1680) at 1718/320 MeV, so correlations with the new \(1^-\) are only partly probed. - \(J^P=1^-\) alone cannot separate a hybrid from a conventional excited K. The collaboration says further study is needed.

arXiv:2611.12774 §

Search for Dark Photon Dark Matter at 74–159 μeV/c² Using Broadband Cryogenic Receivers

Hiroki Takeuchi et al.

No signal is seen, giving χ < (3.1–17)×10⁻¹² at 95% CL over 99.8% of 18–38.5 GHz. The setup is a metal plate viewed by cryogenic K- and Ka-band horns, with system temperatures of about 40–120 K. The readout is dSpec, a 4 GHz, 15.6 kHz-bin spectrometer that gives 2000× more integration per bin than the collaboration’s previous spectrum analyzer, for 4.8×10⁵ s per bin. The largest local excess is 4.96σ at 37.585 GHz, which becomes a global p = 0.38 once the look-elsewhere effect is included (\(N_{\rm eff}\) = 1.94×10⁶).

Why it matters: the result improves the collaboration’s own limits more than 10× over most of 74–110 μeV. It also gives the first direct limits over most of 110–159 μeV, about two orders below cosmological bounds. That is the mass range where inflationary DPDM points, and resonant searches have to tune step by step to cover it.

Caveats: - The limits assume DPDM is all of the local dark matter (ρ = 0.39 GeV/cm³) with randomly oriented polarization in a single-polarization receiver. - Coherent conversion from other metal surfaces has an unknown phase. \(A_{\rm eff}\) is therefore set to its minimum over that phase, which costs up to 44% in area. - Window geometry contributes up to about 25% systematic uncertainty on χ. - Clock-harmonic spurs are excluded. In those excluded intervals the conservative limit degrades to 3.8×10⁻¹⁰.

Monday, November 9, 2026

arXiv:2611.09299 §

Spin Correlations in tt̅ Production and Decay at the LHC in Perturbative QCD

Emanuele Re

Bound-state effects near \(2m_t\) can be computed in ordinary perturbative QCD, provided the observable integrates over \(M_{t\bar t}\) up to a cut at least ~5 GeV above threshold. The argument rests on the integrated spectral density. Bound-state poles enter at relative \(\mathcal{O}(a_1^3)\), i.e. N³LO, and combine with a continuum term into an analytic result. The consequence is concrete: once the full expansion is kept, the naive toponium contribution is cut by a factor of two. Adding an \(\eta_t\) term on top of a perturbative continuum therefore double-counts. Re keeps the first few \(\alpha_s/v\) terms of the Sommerfeld expansion, with a Coulomb scale of several tens of GeV set by the cut rather than by \(v\to0\), and matches them to NLO+PS and NNLO+PS. This pushes NLO+PS predictions for the spin observable \(D\) toward the ATLAS/CMS data. The shift is smaller on top of NNLO, which already contains the leading enhanced terms. For CMS’s 2018 \(d\sigma/dM_{t\bar t}\), the \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s^3)\) terms are “useful, but not crucial” once NNLO is included.

These are proceedings. The companion paper builds three POWHEG generators: hvq‑thr1 (narrow width), hvq‑thr2 (off-shell), and an upgraded bb4l. Their spread gives an estimate of the narrow-width-approximation uncertainty. In the thr2 comparison, the threshold increment is 1.62 pb against 2.73±0.61 pb from Garzelli et al. at \(M_{\rm cut}=345\) GeV. The two converge by about 350 GeV. Caveats: the claim fails for measurements fine enough to resolve the peak, and the author flags the newer, more exclusive CMS/ATLAS \(M_{t\bar t}\) analyses as possibly changing the conclusion.

arXiv:2611.09788 §

Possible Solution to the Gallium Anomaly? Nuclear Transition Densities

D. Castillo et al.

A recent PRL claimed that dropping lepton–nuclear factorization resolves the gallium anomaly. This paper finds that the claim does not survive realistic nuclear structure. The authors run nine shell-model calculations of the Gamow–Teller radial transition density for \(^{71}\)Ga↔\(^{71}\)Ge. These span four \(pf g_{9/2}\) Hamiltonians, bare and effective GT operators, and an LNPS space extended to \(0f_{7/2}\). All are normalized to the \(^{71}\)Ge EC half-life, \(11.465\pm0.003\) d, and convolved with Dirac–Hartree–Fock–Slater electron wavefunctions. The densities form a narrow band. The unfactorized cross sections are \(5.26\pm0.01\) (\(^{51}\)Cr) and \(6.30\pm0.01\) (\(^{37}\)Ar) ×10⁻⁴⁵ cm², against measured values of \(4.44\pm0.33\) and \(5.21\pm0.72\). That gives experiment/theory ≈ 0.83, and the factorized and unfactorized results agree to ~1%. This is expected, since the Fermi function varies by only ~3% across the nucleus.

The PRL’s fitted densities (mDG, mTG) have overall scales 100× and 1000× the shell-model ones. Their nodes cancel so strongly that the net integral is only 2.5% and 0.25% of the integrated absolute value. Refitting with a shell-model radial ansatz requires coefficients ~3000× the calculated ones, again with percent-level cancellation. Caveats: a harmonic-oscillator radial basis is used, and \(q_{\rm EC}\) absorbs two-body currents, weak magnetism and radiative corrections. The authors argue these are subleading but call for ab initio (VS-IMSRG) cross-checks. The result is consistent with the solar-source analysis, which shows no deficit.

arXiv:2611.08586 §

Magic Is in the Eye of the Measure

Zhi-Heng Shen et al.

For two qubits, “maximal magic” depends on the measure, and so does the minimal-magic determination of \(\sin^2\theta_W\). The paper proves global maxima exactly for four measures: - \(M_2\): \(\log_2(16/7)\) on 480 states, certified by a sum-of-squares argument. - \(\mathfrak{D}_{\min}\) and \(\mathfrak{D}_{\max}\): a shared maximum \(\log_2[6/(2+\sqrt3)]\approx0.685\) on 640 states, certified by branch-and-bound over ~4.4×10⁶ boxes. - LR: \(\log_2[(1+\sqrt5)/2]\) on a continuum of 192 planes, certified by LP vertex enumeration over 626,626 vertices.

The three maximizer sets are mutually disjoint.

In tree-level Møller scattering, \(M_2\) is minimized at \(s_W^2=0.2317\) at \(m_Z\) (measured value 0.23122) and tracks the \(\overline{\rm MS}\) running to ~1 TeV. \(\mathfrak{D}_{\min}\) has a shallow minimum 7% low at \(m_Z\). \(\mathfrak{D}_{\max}\) and LR instead give a cusp at exactly \(1/4\) at every energy. The cause is structural. These two measures are maxima over witnesses, so their kinks point upward, and they grow linearly in \(|g_V|\) near stabilizer states. At \(g_V=0\) the singlet scatters into itself, which pins the minimum there. One of the authors (Yin) co-authored the original Møller minimal-magic paper, so this is an internal audit. The authors stress that both the \(1/4\) pinning and the \(\mathfrak{D}_{\min}\) kinks rest on exact tree-level properties and may not survive higher orders.

Full texts for arXiv:2611.09404 (Zakharov) and arXiv:2611.09150 (Ding et al.) were not supplied, so they have no deep-dive entries.

Friday, November 6, 2026

arXiv:2611.07578 §

Can cosmic neutrinos induce a cosmic birefringence signal?

Mario Reig

The Kalb–Ramond completion of the neutrino-current operator \(j_\mu A_\nu\tilde F^{\mu\nu}\) is exactly dual to a massless axion. In that dual theory the signal fixes \(g_{a\gamma}/f_\ell=1/\Lambda_*^2\) independently of the 2-form normalisation. The Yin–Komatsu fit (\(44^{+12}_{-7}\) TeV\(\sqrt{\xi_{\nu_e}}\)) gives \(\Lambda_*\simeq10\) TeV. Because \(j^\mu\) must be an \(SU(2)_L\) singlet, the axion picks up an electron coupling with \(g_{a\gamma}g_{ae}=(1-c_R)m_e/\Lambda_*^2\simeq5.3\times10^{-12}\) GeV\(^{-1}\) for \(\xi_{\nu_e}=0.05\). The bounds then close every branch:

  • Left-handed current. ALPS II requires \(g_{ae}\gtrsim1.8\times10^{-3}\). That shifts \(a_e\) by \(-2\times10^{-8}\), against agreement at \(\sim10^{-12}\). The product also exceeds the CAST helioscope bound by eleven orders of magnitude. Stellar trapping does not rescue it: trapped axions boost solar conductivity, and protection requires \(g_{ae}\gtrsim40\).
  • Electron lepton number (\(c_R=1\)). This current is anomalous. Since ALPS II caps \(f_\ell\lesssim0.3\) GeV, it predicts BR\((Z\to\gamma\varphi)\gtrsim0.1\), against L3’s \(10^{-6}\).
  • Higgs-dressed currents. These give excessive \(W\) and \(h\) decays to \(\varphi\).
  • Anomaly-free currents (e.g. \(B-L\)). These leave a contact term \(\gtrsim5\times10^5G_F\). LEP and neutrino scattering exclude it, and it would also give \(N_{\rm eff}\approx12\). Alternatively the axion velocity is set once at asymmetry generation, and relic neutrinos play no role in the rotation.

Why it matters: any confirmed isotropic rotation should be read as a light photon-coupled axion, not as a measurement of the relic-neutrino asymmetry.

Caveats: the Stückelberg alternative is dismissed on EFT-validity grounds (breakdown at ~keV, below BBN) rather than excluded by data. The stellar-trapping treatment uses order-of-magnitude solar-core estimates.

arXiv:2611.08327 §

Exploring the limit of partonic collectivity in small collision systems at the LHC

ALICE Collaboration

ALICE extracts identified-hadron \(v_2(p_T)\) in Run 2 p–Pb (198M events) and pp (226M events) in multiplicity bins. The method combines TPC–FMD long-range correlations, a template fit, and a three-subevent construction. Baryon–meson splitting over \(3<p_T<5\) GeV/\(c\):

\(N_{\rm ch}\) bin p–Pb pp
\(\geq50\) 6.13σ —
25–50 5.28σ 4.78σ
15–25 1.95σ 0.35σ

Mass ordering and the ~2.2 GeV/\(c\) crossing persist in every bin. In low-multiplicity p–Pb the baryon/meson ratios stay above unity past the crossing but return to unity at 4–6 GeV/\(c\). In low-multiplicity pp, \(v_2(\Lambda)/v_2(K)\) sits below unity.

Why it matters: this is the first scan of where coalescence-type signatures fade. The ≥25 bins in pp now look like high-multiplicity p–Pb. In the 15–25 bin the authors explicitly decline to claim presence or absence of splitting, because uncertainties dominate. It complements last week’s O–O φ-flow result from the opposite direction: the floor rather than the reach.

Caveats: - The non-flow template baseline is \(N_{\rm ch}<15\), directly adjacent to the lowest signal bin. Baseline variation is already the dominant systematic (4% p–Pb, 7% pp). - Equal \(N_{\rm ch}\) in pp and p–Pb need not mean equivalent physics. - Run 3 statistics are needed to settle the low bins.

arXiv:2611.07067 §

QED corrections to e⁺e⁻→π⁺π⁻⁽γ) with amplitude-level resummation

St\'ephane Delorme et al.

Amplitude-level YFS resummation for \(\pi^+\pi^-(\gamma)\) is implemented independently in KKMC (CEEX) and SHERPA (ACRAIC). Both include exact one- and two-photon trees, the full one-loop correction with initial–final boxes, and the exact one-loop correction to single-photon emission. First-order fiducial cross sections agree to ≲0.04% across all five STRONG2020 scenarios. The second-order theory-nuisance bands are:

  • ~0.005% at CMD
  • ~0.1% at KLOE-I
  • ~0.02% at KLOE-II
  • ~0.4% at BESIII
  • ~0.9% in the 10 GeV B scenario, the setup matching BaBar’s ISR measurement

The new radiative-return parameter handles the regime where the first-order correction \(r_1\) is O(1), up to order −1 at BESIII and B. It uses the last relative correction as its template and normalises it by the YFS exponent \(\gamma=2(\alpha/\pi)(L-1)\). At O(α) it dominates the bands (~6% at BESIII, ~9% at B) and covers the −5% and −10% second-order steps. The exception is low \(M_{\pi\pi}\), where a second hard photon enters at leading order. There an explicit \(\pi\pi\gamma\) NLO calculation is needed; it agrees with BabaYaga@NLO to ~1%, and to ~0.1% around the ρ.

At KLOE-I both generators follow BabaYaga@NLO, not PHOKHARA, in the pion polar-angle shape. GVMD loops lower the CMD-3 charge asymmetry by up to \(1.15\times10^{-2}\) near 0.78 GeV, so form-factor modelling dominates that observable.

Caveats: - Pions are point-like scalars with a factorised form factor, and vacuum polarisation is off throughout. - Two-loop boxes, the genuine two-loop pion vertex and O(α³) are missing. - The KLOE-I second-order steps differ by ~0.06%, several times the statistical error. - The radiative-return parameter underestimates the missing higher orders wherever real and virtual corrections cancel.

Thursday, November 5, 2026

arXiv:2611.05051 §

A Note On Lattice Definition of Gauge Theories With Chiral Fermions

Edward Witten

Witten defines a lattice chiral gauge theory without lattice fermions. The link variables are restricted to “admissible” fields, meaning every plaquette satisfies \(|U_P-1|<\epsilon\). Each admissible configuration is interpolated to a continuum gauge field, and continuum chiral fermions are coupled to that field. The fermion weight is \(|\det\not{D}_W^+|\exp(-i\pi\eta(\mathcal{D}_Y))\), where \(\eta\) is the APS eta-invariant on a five-manifold \(Y\) bounding spacetime. By the Dai–Freed gluing law, this weight is independent of the choice of \(Y\) exactly when the representation is free of both perturbative and global anomalies. Instanton sectors are handled with fiducial cobordism generators. Their phases are the renormalized theta-angles. Infinite-order generators give continuous thetas, and torsion generators give discrete ones.

The construction works for any compact gauge group, any anomaly-free representation, any dimension, and any compact spin manifold, not just \(T^4\). Witten justifies the general-manifold case by the requirement that chiral theories couple consistently to gravity. He notes that on \(T^4\) the cluster-decomposition shortcut fails for discrete thetas and for fractional instanton number. He also argues that numerically evaluating the continuum determinant does not reintroduce doubling.

The caveats are explicit: - Sign problem. The chiral fermion weight is not positive, so the scheme cannot be run as Monte Carlo. - Admissibility. Irrelevance of the admissibility constraint in the continuum limit rests on the asymptotic-freedom expectation, not a proof. - Novelty. Witten describes the main novelty as “possibly” a sharper treatment of the fermion path integral and its anomalies. The same variant appears in Hsu, and earlier proposals ran along similar lines.

arXiv:2611.05464 §

Flavour Chains at the TeV Edge

Admir Greljo et al.

Vector-like-fermion chains generate Yukawas as \(y_F z^N\) with \(z\simeq0.02\), and the Minimal Flavour Protection group \(SU(2)_q\times U(1)_X\) protects them. The same Higgs link generates \(\mathcal{O}_{H\ell,Hq}^{(1,3)}\) at tree level with \(C\sim|y_F|^2/M_F^2\) and no spurion suppression.

Current bounds - A six-class fit to LEP/SLD data, cross-checked with three public codes, gives \(M_F/|y_F|\gtrsim3\)–7 TeV. The indicative tuning is \(1/\Delta_{\rm total}\simeq0.3\%\). - \(B_s\to\mu\mu\) is currently stronger for the down-type classes, at about 9 TeV. Projected flavour reach stays at about 9 TeV or below.

FCC-ee projections - The reach is about 100 TeV unprofiled and about 50 TeV profiled. - The profiled tuning reaches \(\mathcal{O}(10^{-3})\) in scenario S1 and \(\mathcal{O}(10^{-4})\) in S2. - In the experiment-only scenario, the profiled bounds collapse to a nearly universal 52–57 TeV. This reflects an approximate flat direction from the \(R_f\) ratios, which better \(\Gamma_Z\) precision could lift.

Why rearranging the chain doesn’t escape. Moving the Higgs link shifts the effect between left- and right-handed currents but fixes their product. The floor becomes \(M_F\gtrsim(|y_F|\,y\,\Lambda_L\Lambda_R)^{1/2}\), which puts the \(b\) and \(\tau\) chains above 15 and 10 TeV at Tera-\(Z\). That configuration is about 100× less tuned than the minimal chain but still heavily tuned. This estimate assumes \(\Lambda_R\approx\Lambda_L\), which the authors did not compute.

Caveats - The tuning scales as \(|y_F|^4\), and \(|y_F|\sim0.2\) removes today’s tuning entirely. - The Higgs-criticality motivation needs \(|y_F|\gtrsim0.7\). With 18 Higgs-coupled states, it lowers \(h_{\rm crit}\) from \(3\times10^{11}\) GeV to 7.3 TeV at the 5 TeV benchmark. - That mechanism still needs stabilizing bosons below about 100 TeV and leaves a \(3\times10^{-3}\) residual hierarchy.

arXiv:2611.05432 §

Parton wave functions, density matrices and entropy in DGLAP evolution

S. Bondarenko et al.

Entropy from densities. The one-parton matrix built from Lipatov’s wave functions supports two distinct entropies: - Sampled-parton entropy: \(S_1\), computed from the trace-normalized matrix. - Mode entropy: \(S_{\rm mode}=\sum_\lambda g_B(n_\lambda)\), which bounds any state with the same one-body data, \(S_{\rm vN}\le S_{\rm mode}\). Equality holds only for the thermal Gaussian state.

The pure hadron has zero entropy regardless of its occupations. The Kharzeev–Levin geometric entropy is \(g_B(\bar n-1)\), the one-mode special case. Its large-\(\bar n\) form \(1+\ln\bar n\) is 47% high at \(\bar n=1.5\).

Rapidity cells. Summing isolated unit-rapidity cell entropies, rather than merging them, overcounts by \(\Delta_Y=\tfrac12\omega Y^2\). At \(\omega=0.4\) this is 11.6 at \(Y=8\), a factor of 3.8. The gap depends on the chosen resolution.

Evolution. Averaging over unrecorded emission histories, assumed incoherent across scales, gives a Lindblad equation in \(\ln Q^2\) with \(\hat\Sigma=\hat{\mathcal R}^\dagger\hat{\mathcal R}/2\). Its occupations reproduce LO DGLAP, including virtual terms, at leading-log accuracy. This holds for strong \(k_\perp\) ordering in light-cone gauge with dilute occupations.

Caveats - The thermal-Gaussian closure supplies Bose/Pauli factors, but its own error estimate is unfavourable. Its initial rate derivative deviates from the exact one by a relative \(4n\) and has the wrong sign for \(1/6<n<1/2\). - The Lamb-shift-like term is set to zero by assumption. - No recombination is derived. - The proposed HERA/LHC test compares mode numbers inferred from hadron factorial moments with transverse mode counts. The extracted text cuts off before the test is specified.

arXiv:2611.04596 §

A New Precise Measurement of the e⁺e⁻→π⁺π⁻⁽γ) Cross Section with the ISR Method at BaBar

BaBar Collaboration

The new analysis drops particle ID and separates \(\pi\pi\gamma\) from \(\mu\mu\gamma\) with template fits to \(|\cos\theta^*|\) in bins of two-track mass. Pions follow \(\sin^2\theta^*\); muons go from flat to \(1+\cos^2\theta^*\). This allows a \(p_T>0.1\) GeV track cut instead of the 2009 analysis’s 1 GeV/\(c\), which retains the asymmetric topologies at large \(|\cos\theta^*|\) where the two shapes differ most. The fit uses 7.6M events from the full 460 fb⁻¹. As a validation, the muon spectrum is compared absolutely to QED and gives \(R_{\mu\mu}=0.9920\) with 0.69% total precision; the analysis was unblinded only after this check.

The result is \(a_\mu^{\pi\pi}(0.3\)–\(1.4\,\mathrm{GeV})=525.9(3.5)(3.8)\), against 513.3(2.2)(3.1) for 2009. The gap is 12.6×10⁻¹⁰, and the new cross section sits ~2% higher across the ρ peak. Compatibility is assessed in two ways: - Local scan: in 50 MeV intervals the differences are mostly <2σ in the central region, and conservative systematic-correlation scenarios move these by ≤0.2σ. - Integrated window: over 0.6–0.8832 GeV the 2009 result is −1.4σ from 2026.

The 517.8(2.4)(3.3) combination inflates uncertainties wherever local χ²>1. In the same window, 2026 is +0.9σ from CMD-3, −4.2σ from KLOE and −3.0σ from BESIII. BaBar calls it consistent with τ-based estimates and recent lattice results.

Caveats: - Below 0.5 GeV and above 1 GeV the \(\mu\mu\) background overwhelms the signal, and the new result is not competitive with 2009. The 0.3–0.5 GeV piece reads 60.7 vs 57.6. - The combination therefore mixes two methods whose relative weight depends on energy.

arXiv:2611.03782 §

Toponium hadroproduction at next-to-next-to-leading order in NRQCD

Sven Yannick Klein et al.

The new ingredient is the NNLO QCD short-distance coefficient for \(gg\to\eta_t\). It uses nested soft-collinear subtraction, two-loop amplitudes and a soft-improved matching scheme. The cross section also includes: - NLO EW corrections, - \(\Theta_t\) channels, - \(v^2\) and P-wave terms, - associated production, - NNLO+RG-improved perturbative LDMEs summed over radial states.

At 13 TeV the result is \(\sigma=4.87^{+0.63}_{-0.65}\) pb. NLO raises LO by 28% and NNLO lowers NLO by 5%. The NLO EW term is a flat −7%, driven by \(y_t^2\) Higgs exchange. All other pieces are each below about 1%; only \(q\bar q\to\gamma^*/Z^*\to\Theta_t\) reaches ~1% below 10 TeV. The seven-point scale band narrows about fourfold relative to NLO, to \(^{+0.5\%}_{-3.0\%}\). The paper warns that the small NNLO shift at \(\mu=m_t\) is unrepresentative, since the correction grows above \(2m_t\).

The error budget is now dominated by non-hard sources: - Coulomb scale of the LDMEs: ±12%, nearly independent of order and \(\sqrt{s}\). - \(\alpha_s(m_Z)\): ±5%, also mainly through the LDMEs. - \(m_t\): ±0.8%.

The authors point to N³LO S-wave LDMEs as the remedy; these already exist for the spin-triplet state.

Caveat: this is a zero-width result, counting only \(M_{t\bar t}<2m_t\). It is not yet the threshold spectrum ATLAS/CMS measure; that comparison awaits the promised finite-width Green’s-function extension.

arXiv:2611.03904 §

Constraints on a Long-Lived X17 Vector Boson with FASER

FASER Collaboration et al.

FASER statistically combines two existing searches: - Dark-photon search: 27 fb⁻¹, 0 events observed on a background of 2.3×10⁻³. - ALP search: 57.7 fb⁻¹, 1 event on 0.44±0.39. It uses the timing scintillator as a veto, so it covers decays after the decay volume and is orthogonal to the dark-photon search.

Together they use the full magnetized region as decay volume. The reinterpretation is for a strictly protophobic vector with \(\epsilon_n=10^{-2}\).

Production is dominated by proton bremsstrahlung, which is not suppressed despite \(\epsilon_p=0\). The form factors are evaluated at \(t=m_X^2\), where both \(F_1\) and \(F_2\) are nonzero even though \(F_1(0)=0\). \(\eta\to X\gamma\) and neutron bremsstrahlung at the TAN are subleading.

At 17 MeV the 90% CL CLs exclusion is \(2\times10^{-8}<\epsilon_e<3\times10^{-4}\). That fully covers the low-coupling window \(6\times10^{-5}\)–\(1.2\times10^{-4}\), which had only recently reopened after an E141 re-analysis. The high-coupling window (\(6\times10^{-4}\)–\(1.5\times10^{-3}\)) survives because the X decays before reaching FASER. Under an alternative model with \(\epsilon_n=\epsilon_e\), the excluded range is \(2\times10^{-5}<\epsilon<1.5\times10^{-4}\).

Caveats: - The bremsstrahlung off-shell damping factor alone carries ~40% signal uncertainty. - The model is simplified: the small proton and axial couplings required by pion and kaon decay bounds are neglected. - Results are stated to be insensitive to \(\epsilon_n\) within the Be-favoured range.

(Full texts for arXiv:2611.04865 and arXiv:2611.03794 were not supplied, so no deep dive is given for them here.)

arXiv:2611.00230 §

Evidence for the decay B⁰→μ⁺μ⁻ and improved measurements of the decay B⁰_s→μ⁺μ⁻

LHCb collaboration et al.

With 18 fb⁻¹ of 2024–25 Upgrade I data, LHCb sees a \(B^0\to\mu^+\mu^-\) excess at 3.0σ, with \(\mathcal{B}=(1.26^{+0.51}_{-0.47}{}^{+0.06}_{-0.05})\times10^{-10}\). Combined with Runs 1–2, this becomes \((1.14^{+0.42}_{-0.39}{}^{+0.06}_{-0.05})\times10^{-10}\) at 3.2σ. The SM predicts \((1.03\pm0.05)\times10^{-10}\), and the prior limit was \(<1.6\times10^{-10}\) at 90% CL.

For \(B_s^0\), Run 3 alone gives \((3.31^{+0.31+0.15}_{-0.30-0.13})\times10^{-9}\). The combined fit gives \((3.25^{+0.26+0.13}_{-0.25-0.12})\times10^{-9}\), sitting somewhat below the SM’s \(3.66\pm0.14\).

On the digest’s question: the ratio is now measured rather than bounded, but loosely. The combined result is \(\mathcal{R}_{\mu\mu}=0.035^{+0.014}_{-0.012}\), against \(0.0280\pm0.0016\) in the SM and in any MFV theory. At ~40% precision, that is not yet a meaningful test of non-CKM flavour structure.

The effective lifetime is \(1.42\pm0.18\pm0.02\) ps from Run 3 and \(1.60\pm0.16\pm0.01\) ps combined. Both are still statistically dominated, and \(\mathcal{A}_{\Delta\Gamma_s}^{\mu\mu}=0.91\pm1.92\) is unconstrained within its physical range.

Caveats visible in the paper: - The \(B_s\) branching fraction assumes \(\mathcal{A}_{\Delta\Gamma_s}^{\mu\mu}=+1\). Using −1 instead raises it by 9.2%. - The dominant \(B_s\) systematic (3%) is \(f_s/f_d=0.247\pm0.008\) at 13.6 TeV, taken from an LHCb paper still in preparation. - The \(B^0\) systematic (4%) comes from misID and semileptonic background modelling. - The \(B_s\) yields from the lifetime and branching-fraction fits agree only at the 1.7σ level.

arXiv:2611.01158 §

Exothermic Higgs portal: from LUX-ZEPLIN recoil to Gamma Rays

Subhojit Roy et al.

(Authors are Roy and Zupan.) The model is a complex singlet split into \(\phi_{1,2}\) by a few hundred keV, coupling only through an off-diagonal Higgs portal \(g\,\phi_1\phi_2 H^\dagger H\). The relic abundance fixes \(g\) and leaves \(r_2\simeq0.5\) of the dark matter in the excited state today.

At the benchmark (\(m_{\phi_1}=100\) GeV, \(\Delta m=600\) keV, \(g\simeq0.0515\)), the predictions are: - LZ rate: \(\phi_2 A\to\phi_1 A\) down-scattering gives \(N_\epsilon\simeq1.63\) efficiency-weighted events in the 2.84 t·yr exposure. This uses the Standard Halo Model and needs no velocity tail. - Diphoton decay: \(\phi_2\to\phi_1\gamma\gamma\) has \(\tau\simeq4.5\times10^{23}\) s, about 40× above a recast INTEGRAL/SPI bound. That recast neglects inter-bin correlations and template systematics. About 83% of the photons fall above COSI threshold. - Dwarf annihilation: \(\langle\sigma v\rangle_{\rm equiv}=2.26\times10^{-26}\) vs a Fermi-LAT limit of \(3.34\times10^{-26}\) cm³/s. That is a thin margin, and the dwarfs exclude \(m_{\phi_1}\lesssim72\) GeV. - Solar capture: capture leaves the Sun’s population mostly in \(\phi_1\). The resulting WW rate is ~\(10^{-13}\) of the IceCube limit. The captured-\(\phi_1\) × halo-\(\phi_2\) channel was not evaluated.

The main caveat is spectral. A xenon diffraction minimum near 270 keV pushes the predicted peak to ~180 keV. The model expects 1.18 events in 100–200 keV but only 0.22 in 215–270 keV, where the candidate sits. The total yield also depends strongly on nuclear modelling: 1.63 events with the default response, 4.70 with a Helm form factor, and 1.61 with chiral EFT. No event-level likelihood is performed.

arXiv:2611.00086 §

SCRAMJet: An Ultra Low Latency JetClass-Scale Transformer for the HL-LHC Level-1 Trigger

Aadeesh Sharma et al.

The model is a single-block transformer with rank-2 Linformer attention, \(D=16\), and 64 constituents, trained with per-parameter learned fixed-point quantization. It reaches 71.43±0.62% accuracy on ten-class JetClass with 0.157M MACs. That is 6.1 points above PHAT-JeT at one-eighth the MACs. On a VU13P it meets timing at 147 ns (21 cycles) using 25.7% of LUTs, and the RTL is bit-exact over 1.3M outputs.

The transferable result is the “virtual channel” mechanism. The quantizer masks zero-width channels only at inference, so training sees them while the circuit does not. One checkpoint scores 72.32% in training mode but 65.2% at inference, and 72.35% with the mask disabled; the mask explains the whole gap. All 7,015 sub-zero-width parameters sit inside attention.

The fix is a floor on the activation-quantizer bit-widths; the inherited constraint only covered weight quantizers. At EBOPs matched to within 0.12%, the floor lifts accuracy from 66.38% to 69.48%.

Caveats: - That same floored design compiles to 42% more adder-graph units, so EBOPs can rank designs opposite to their real cost. Width cuts are the reliable lever: halving \(D\) removed 38.8% of compiled cost at no accuracy loss. - Macro AUC (0.9289) still trails PHAT-JeT by 0.010. - Results come from a 2M-jet training subset with three seeds.

(arXiv:2611.00141 and arXiv:2611.01285 were not supplied and are omitted.)

arXiv:2610.40766 §

Hints of self-interacting dark matter from strong gravitational lensing

Daniel Gilman et al.

What they did. This is a recast, not new data. It takes the G26 likelihood on median core-collapse times in two halo-mass bins, \(10^6\)–\(10^8\) and \(10^8\)–\(10^{10.7}\,M_\odot\), from 29 JWST quads. G26 disfavors CDM with Bayes factors of 8:1 to 24:1. The authors map that likelihood onto elastic Yukawa SIDM using the Gurian et al. gravothermal \(t_c\), which is valid across Knudsen regimes. They then multiply in cluster (\(<0.1\) cm²/g at 1500 km/s), satellite-evaporation (\(<10\) cm²/g at 250 km/s) and relic-density likelihoods.

The likelihood peaks at \(\sigma_{10}\approx1000\) or \(200\) cm²/g, depending on the subhalo-modeling weights. Both peaks sit at \(\alpha_\chi\sim10^{-3.5}\)–\(10^{-3}\) and \(m_\chi/m_\phi>10^{3.5}\). At \(\alpha_\chi=10^{-3}\) the viable region is \(m_\chi\sim20\)–80 GeV with \(m_\phi<20\) MeV, much of it near the \(s\)-wave resonances. To survive mediator bounds, they adopt Dirac DM with a complex scalar dilepton that decays to \(\nu_R\nu_R\); lepton charge forbids tree-level Higgs mixing. Kinetic decoupling at \(\sim0.14\) MeV clears the \(\gtrsim7\) keV requirement. \(\Delta N_{\rm eff}\) can stay below 0.107, but only with extra entropy release or a tuned Higgs-portal history.

Why it matters. The paper turns the lensing thread into a concrete particle-physics target with \(N_{\rm eff}\) and direct-detection handles. It also shows that elastic scattering cannot collapse standard field halos faster than 2.0 Gyr and 2.9 Gyr in the two mass bins. Shorter collapse times would point to inelastic or dissipative physics instead.

Caveats. CDM is not formally excluded. The result depends on the assumed tidal acceleration (3–5×), subhalo abundance, and collapsed-profile slope. The relic relation carries a factor-of-5 allowance because the bound-state-formation calculation does not yet exist. If mergers delay collapse, the inferred long-mean-free-path cross sections are only lower bounds.

arXiv:2610.40821 §

The Yottascale at Tera-Z: Petascale SUSY?

Lukas Allwicher et al.

What they did. Gauge couplings are run from \(m_Z\) to the GUT scale at successive loop orders and compared against current and FCC-ee input errors; \(\delta\alpha_s\) drops from \(9\times10^{-4}\) to \(10^{-4}\). In the SM, 2-loop running is already only marginally adequate, and FCC-ee precision would resolve 4-loop effects. In the CMSSM, the 2-loop SUSY threshold shift to \(\alpha_3\) makes unification worse at three loops: \(\bar\xi\) goes from 0.1–5% to about 2.4%.

In minimal SUSY SU(5), the 1-loop GUT matching fixes \(M_{H_c}\) independently of \(\alpha_5\). The reason is group-theoretic: \(X\) and \(\Sigma\) together fill a complete \(\mathbf{24}\). The same holds in Georgi–Glashow, but fails for multi-field breaking sectors.

This makes \(M_{H_c}\) purely IR-determined and loop-order sensitive. With \(\tau/\tau_{\rm SK}\simeq1.55\times10^{-4}(M_{H_c}/{\rm YeV})^2(m_S/{\rm PeV})^2\), the two loop orders give opposite answers: - Two loops: \(M_{H_c}\) grows with \(m_S\), so the Super-K \(K^+\bar\nu\) bound is evaded near PeV. - Three loops: \(M_{H_c}\) falls with \(m_S\) and stays excluded at every SUSY scale, including against Hyper-K projections.

Why it matters. It is a clean example of perturbative order, rather than data, flipping a model’s viability. It also gives a concrete theory requirement for Tera-\(Z\).

Caveats. The 2-loop GUT thresholds are unknown, and the authors admit they could cancel the effect if comparable in size. Results above \(m_S\sim100\) TeV are extrapolated with analytic degenerate-spectrum thresholds. The analysis does not cover split SUSY or gravity-induced operators.

arXiv:2610.41894 §

Calorimetry as inference: the information content of particle showers

Wonyong Chung

What they did. The paper casts calorimetry as Fisher/Bayes estimation on a lattice of records. It proves monotonicity under refinement, exact information scaling under Poisson thinning, and a \(1/(1+{\rm cv}^2)\) clustering cost. It also shows that the \(a/\sqrt E\oplus b\) budget is the Gaussian-equality case of a two-sided bound.

It then measures a resolution ladder with trained estimators (validated to \(\le0.34\%\) against exact posteriors on solvable cascades). The data are Geant4 11.4.2 FTFP_BERT \(\pi^-\) events in a large Cu block, with the neutron tracking cut removed. At 20 GeV the ladder reads:

Record \(\sigma/E\)
Sum 4.07%
+ \(S,C\) 2.16%
+ longitudinal shape 1.60%
+ time 1.32%
+ counts 1.29%

The full record is about \(5.5\%/\sqrt E\oplus0.3\%\) over 20–100 GeV. That is a factor 1.7–1.9 below the Lee–Livan–Wigmans one-functional construction evaluated on the same events (2.14%; 2.7% as published).

What the readout dimensions are worth: - Shape: radial information is worth only 2–3%; depth sampling saturates at about \(1\,\lambda_I\). - Time: a 10 ns gate costs about a third in resolution. - Floor: in-volume neutrinos from stopped-\(\mu\) chains leave an estimated 0.99% floor, barely reduced from their 1.12% marginal. This argues for muon-stop tagging.

Caveats. All records are truth-level, single-particle and pileup-free. The count-based record elements have no hardware counterpart, so 1.60% is the hardware-relevant number. Cross-run spreads reach 0.19% at 20 GeV. Model dependence is real: - Transferring FTFP_BERT-trained estimators to QGSP_BERT events shifts the full record by +19% (no retraining was done for QGSP_BERT). - Retraining with high-precision neutron transport leaves the top value nearly unchanged (1.30%) but cuts the time increment from 0.28% to 0.17%.

arXiv:2610.36237 §

Evidence of partonic collectivity in OO collisions through the measurement of φ, K⁰_S, and Λ elliptic flow

ALICE Collaboration

What they did. ALICE measured identified-hadron \(v_2\) in ~3×10⁹ O–O events at 5.36 TeV, in 0–10% and 10–40% centrality, using a \(|\Delta\eta|>1.2\) gap and a low-multiplicity template fit with a 65–100% reference. Signal \(v_2\) comes from simultaneous fits to the invariant mass and \(V_{2\Delta}(m)\).

  • Low \(p_T\) (<2.2 GeV). The ordering is \(v_2(K^0_S) > v_2(\Lambda) \approx v_2(\phi)\). K⁰_S–Λ separates at >10σ and K⁰_S–φ at >5σ.
  • Above 3 GeV. The pattern flips. Λ exceeds K⁰_S at >10σ and exceeds φ at >5σ. The φ is compatible with the K⁰_S despite its Λ-like mass.
  • Scaling. Constituent-quark scaling holds approximately, with deviations comparable to those seen in Pb–Pb.

Why it matters. The φ separates mass from quark content. That makes it a sharper test than baryon-to-meson yield ratios such as CMS’s O–O enhancement. Mass ordering at low \(p_T\) combined with type grouping at intermediate \(p_T\) is the full heavy-ion pattern, now seen in a system with well-constrained geometry.

Caveats. - The 10σ figure is the Λ–K⁰_S splitting. The comparison that actually discriminates mass from type, Λ–φ, is “>5σ”. With the forward FT0C centrality estimator it drops to about 4.5σ in 0–10%. - Non-flow subtraction is the largest systematic, up to 9% for the φ, and it grows with \(p_T\), exactly where the splitting is claimed. This is why the nonflow validation work (Mallick et al.) matters. - The interpretation is explicitly “qualitative”. No quantitative model comparison is shown, and hydro-plus-fragmentation is acknowledged to generate some species splitting.

arXiv:2610.37318 §

High significance measurement of reactor CEνNS on germanium by CONUS+ (N. Ackermann (CONUS+ Collaboration) et al.)

What they did. Run 2 used two new 2.33 kg active-mass point-contact HPGe diodes plus the original 0.94 kg detector, at 20.7 m from the 3.6 GW_th Leibstadt core. Thresholds were 140–170 eV_ee and the exposure was 1296 kg·d reactor-on. The blind likelihood (spectra hidden below 400 eV_ee) gives:

  • \(N_s = 1555 \pm 116\,(\text{stat}) \pm 98\,(\text{syst})\), a 10.2σ detection against an expected 10.4σ.
  • \(N_s/N_{\rm SM} = 0.91 \pm 0.14\), compared with \(N_{\rm SM} = 1701 \pm 198\).
  • \(\sin^2\theta_W = 0.225 \pm 0.022\) at \(Q \approx 10\) MeV, against the SM value of 0.2387. This is the lowest-\(Q\) CEνNS determination to date.

Why it matters. This is the largest CEνNS sample so far, with precision comparable to COHERENT’s germanium result. It also disfavors the Dresden-II rate and the non-Lindhard quenching behind it. The ratio, however, is systematics-dominated. The 11.6% prediction uncertainty comes mainly from the energy scale near threshold (8.0%) and the quenching factor (7.5%). Until those improve, the \(\sin^2\theta_W\) point is not competitive with the combined CEνNS+ν–e value.

Caveats. - The combined fit has χ²/ndf = 1.37 over 381 dof (\(p = 7\times10^{-6}\)), driven by detector C3 and by C6’s reactor-off data. Inflating the variances to restore \(p>0.1\) lowers the significance to 9.3σ. - The background-shape-independent on-minus-off fit gives only 1332 ± 420 events (3.1σ). - Per-detector yields straddle the prediction: C6 gives 731 against 604 expected, and C7 gives 714 against 903. - Four analysis changes were made during unblinding. The authors state these were motivated by checks independent of the signal.

arXiv:2610.37137 §

What the Primary Lund Plane Omits: A Model-Stitching Diagnosis

Saurabh Rai et al.

What they did. The authors built Lund-ParT, a network architecturally identical to ParT but fed Lund declusterings. They then stitched frozen front and back halves of the two networks together through a single trained 128×128 linear map at the class token. All results use 50% of the top-tagging reference dataset.

  • The gap is representational. Standalone rejection at \(\varepsilon_S = 0.5\) is 291 for ParT and 136 for the primary-branch Lund-ParT. ParT’s downstream reading the Lund representation reaches 128–136, while Lund-ParT’s own downstream reaches only 120–134. A stronger reader does not recover the missing performance.
  • AUC hides it. Retained AUC stays within 0.977–1.007 throughout.
  • It is localized. The deficit sits in about 0.6% of ParT-rejected background jets. These have a mean mass of 180 GeV and about 67 constituents, yet fewer primary splittings than typical background (11.3 against 12.6).
  • The full tree repairs most of it. Rebuilding on the full C/A tree raises rejection to 248, closing 72% of the gap.
  • Masking. Dropping 12 secondary nodes costs little (230.6 → 186.5). Dropping 12 primary nodes costs almost everything (230.6 → 9.2). This suggests the secondary nodes aid learnability rather than inference.

Why it matters. The method is transferable for locating where a tagger loses information. It also warns against a “primary branch plus a few secondaries” shortcut. On quark/gluon tagging the primary branch already retains 77.6% of ParT’s rejection, and the full tree reaches about 85% of ParT on both tasks.

Caveats. - Absolute rejections sit far below full-scale values (~2000–5000). - The consumer-asymmetry result has \(p = 0.062\) with five seeds. - The hypergraph check (116 against 112) uses a single seed on 10% of the data. - The “learnability” reading is an interpretation of the masking results, not a direct test.

Full texts for arXiv:2610.35927 and arXiv:2610.36065 were not supplied, so they are not covered here.

arXiv:2610.35619 §

Indication of Electron Antineutrinos from the Diffuse Supernova Neutrino Background

Kamiokande Collaboration et al.

Super-Kamiokande fits 5003.0 days (Oct 2008–Mar 2026, phases SK-IV through VIII, including the Gd-loaded phases) and finds a DSNB flux of \(3.6^{+1.6}_{-1.5}\) cm⁻²s⁻¹ (\(4.8^{+2.2}_{-2.0}\) events/yr) for \(E_\nu>13.3\) MeV, assuming the Horiuchi+09 6 MeV spectral shape. The background-only hypothesis is disfavored at 2.6σ (one-sided). The Kresse+21 and Ashida+23 shapes give 2.7σ and 2.6σ. The Gd phases alone give 2.2σ and the pure-water phases 1.4σ. The simpler model-independent count over 13.3–31.3 MeV gives only 1.8σ (p = 0.036). The best-fit flux sits at the upper end of the Horiuchi+09 range of 2.1–3.9 cm⁻²s⁻¹.

On the digest’s question of where the gain comes from: - Background rejection. An ML NC classifier raises IBD efficiency from 79% to 89% and halves the residual NCQE fraction from 10% to 5%. - Systematics. A T2K beam sample cuts the NC rate uncertainty from 70% to 45%. The neutron-multiplicity uncertainty falls from 30–40% to 10–30%, and the ⁹Li remaining-rate error from 50% to 30%. - Thresholds. The \(N_n=1\) threshold drops from 15.5 to 11.5 MeV.

The paper says only that the combined effect more than doubles the median discovery potential. The provided text does not split that gain by component.

The key caveat is in Appendix B. With the old \(N_n\neq1\) threshold, the pure-water phases show an extra contribution at 15.5–21.5 MeV, comparable to the expected signal. Including it shifts the fitted DSNB rate by about 1σ, which is more than statistics should allow. The collaboration attributes this to mismodeled spallation and raises that threshold to 21.5 MeV, at a 13.9% cost in expected signal. That is conservative, but it also shows the spallation model is not yet under control at the sensitivity now being reached.

arXiv:2610.35239 §

Observation of the T_cc̅1(4430)⁻ state in B⁰→D^*-D⁰K⁺ decays

LHCb collaboration et al.

LHCb’s amplitude analysis uses 6912 candidates from 9 fb⁻¹, with about 5% background. A model containing only \(D^0K^+\) resonances describes the \(D^0K^+\) and \(D^{*-}K^+\) spectra but misses a dip near 4.4 GeV in \(m(D^{*-}D^0)\) (\(\chi^2=82.1\) over 64 Dalitz bins).

Adding the \(T_{c\bar c1}(4430)^-\) with its mass and width fixed gives \(2\Delta\)NLL \(=-43.0\), which is 6.2σ statistical and 5.3σ after systematics. The state appears through destructive interference rather than as a peak. Its own fit fraction is \((1.3\pm0.4)\%\), while its interference with the QMI S-wave is \((-6.6\pm1.3)\%\). \(J^P=1^+\) is preferred over the alternatives at more than 5σ.

Other results: - With mass and width free, the fit gives \(4424\pm21^{+12}_{-6}\) MeV and \(145\pm39\pm17\) MeV. These sit 1.8σ and 0.7σ from the \(\psi(2S)\pi\) values, so “consistent” carries a mild mass pull. - Substituting the \(T_{c\bar c1}(4200)\) gives only 3.6σ. With both states included, the 4200 drops to 1.6σ. - The \(T_{c\bar c1}(3900)\) and \(T^*_{\bar c\bar s1}(2900)\) are not significant (fit fractions below 0.24% and 0.32% at 95% CL).

This is the first open-charm decay of the state. The measured width ratio \(\Gamma(D^{*-}D^0)/\Gamma(\psi(2S)\pi^-)=0.96\pm0.29^{+0.22}_{-0.48}{}^{+0.57}_{-0.30}\) disfavors dominance of either channel. The authors note that theory predictions for this ratio are lacking.

The main caveat is that the dominant systematic is amplitude modeling. The overlapping \(D^0K^+\) \(1^-\) wave, which carries about 75% of the fit fractions, is described by splines, with a K-matrix alternative used as the systematic variation. The two diverge in phase above 3 GeV.

arXiv:2610.33152 §

A Low Scale for Quark–Lepton Unification

Lawrence J. Hall et al.

Running the Standard Model Yukawas at two loops, the authors find that \(y_\tau=y_b\) holds at \(\mu_3=5.20^{+0.90}_{-0.76}\times10^5\) GeV and \(y_\mu=3y_s\) at \(\mu_2=7.20^{+3.69}_{-2.33}\times10^5\) GeV. The ratio \(\mu_2/\mu_3\) is stable under three-loop running (1.39 → 1.40). The Georgi–Jarlskog relation \(y_e=y_d/3\) arrives later, at about \(3\times10^8\) GeV. For contrast, at \(2\times10^{16}\) GeV the three ratios are off by 30–70%.

A sparse Pati–Salam texture produces all three relations. An \(SU(4)\)-singlet bidoublet fills the 12, 21 and 33 entries, and an adjoint fills the 22 entry. However, tree-level vector-leptoquark exchange in \(K_L\to\mu e\) requires \(M_X\gtrsim2.5\times10^6\) GeV, which is above \(\mu_{2,3}\). That leaves two routes: - Electron mass from elsewhere. The heavier-generation relations then survive at \((5\)–\(7)\times10^5\) GeV, with \(K\to\mu\mu\) as the leading probe. - Two fixed-parameter theories that keep all three relations: - The “123” theory reaches unification through flavor diagonalization alone, at \(4.6^{+1.2}_{-0.9}\times10^6\) GeV. - The “12” theory has symmetric Yukawas and unifies at \(1.5^{+0.7}_{-0.5}\times10^7\) GeV. It predicts \(|V_{cb}|=0.042\pm0.002\); the paper compares this only to the PDG value \(0.0411\pm0.0012\). It also needs an 11% charged-scalar threshold, a negligible up-sector 2–3 rotation, and \(\sin\beta\approx0.07\)–0.17.

The \(\mu\to e\) conversion rate is \(\mathrm{CR_{Al}}\approx2.8\times10^{-17}(10^7\,\mathrm{GeV}/M_X)^4\). Mu2e and COMET Phase II reach about \(8\times10^6\) GeV, covering most of the 123 range; Mu2e-II and AMF would cover both theories.

Caveats: - About 75–90% of the variance in the unification scales comes from \(m_d\) and \(m_s\). - The leading-order texture predicts \(|V_{us}|=0.219\) against a measured 0.2243; matching it needs a relative phase of about 92° between the up and down light blocks. - Scalar contributions to \(\mu\to e\) conversion are omitted. - In \(G_{422}\), nucleon-decay bounds of several \(10^6\) GeV can arise, but they depend on assumed Planck-suppressed operator coefficients.

Monday, October 26, 2026

arXiv:2610.32543 §

Analytic two-loop corrections to top-quark pair production at hadron colliders

Federico Buccioni et al.

Buccioni, Nega, Sauer, Tancredi and Wagner give the complete two-loop \(q\bar q\to t\bar t\) and \(gg\to t\bar t\) amplitudes in analytic form, with full \(m_t\), \(N_c\), \(n_l\) and \(n_h\) dependence, through \(\mathcal O(\varepsilon^2)\). The 789 \(gg\) and 218 \(q\bar q\) two-loop diagrams reduce to 417 masters. These are cast into \(\varepsilon\)-factorised differential equations spanning 13 elliptic curves and one genus-two curve, which lives in the non-planar sector nB463. Up to weight four, 300 of the 449 independent differential forms enter the finite remainder. None of the quadratic-type elliptic forms survive, but 55 of the 85 genus-two forms do. That is the basis for the “first genus-two finite remainder” claim; the curve itself had been identified earlier.

Boundary values are fixed at threshold. The authors run AMFlow at \((x_1,x_2)=(10^{-4},2\cdot10^{-4})\) with 100–300 digits, match to generalised series, then fit constants with PSLQ. The elliptic and genus-two constants resist any known basis and are kept numerically. The public hqpAmp code evaluates renormalised finite remainders across phase space, switching between double and quad precision.

On practical impact, NNLO \(t\bar t\) already ran on numerical grids, and the \(q\bar q\) channel was already analytic. The gains here are: - control and speed; - \(\mathcal O(\varepsilon^{1,2})\) terms for three-loop IR studies; - a stress test of canonical-basis methods beyond elliptic.

The paper does not address bound-state effects. Its threshold expansions are fixed-order input that toponium-vs-continuum fits would still need to combine with a Coulomb/NRQCD treatment.

arXiv:2610.32845 §

The Galactic Center Gamma-Ray Excess and the Look-Elsewhere Effect

Isabel S. Sands et al.

Sands and Hopkins analyse two FIRE-2 Milky Way analogs, m12f and m12m (m12i is dropped for its warped disk), with spectrally resolved CR transport. They compare each snapshot over 460–20 Myr lookback against a baseline “template”: the mean of the snapshots whose total ROI flux is below the median. For every ROI (10°, 20°) and line-of-sight cut (0, 2, 7 kpc), the Fermi GCE lies inside the 25th–75th percentile band of residuals, and many residuals are several times larger. One m12f snapshot matches the GCE in spectrum and is roughly spherical inside 10°, produced by at least three clustered supernova episodes. The physical driver is that CR diffusion across the central kpc takes ~1 Myr, comparable to the timescale of ISM disruption, so steady-state templates are structurally inadequate.

The caveats are substantial, and the authors state most of them: - Star formation. Central SFRs are 1–2 orders of magnitude above the CMZ, and total diffuse flux is a few to 10 times Fermi’s. The residual amplitude is therefore compared against a much brighter galaxy. - Sample size. There are only two galaxies and no AGN, so no frequency or look-elsewhere statistic can be computed. - Baseline choice. A baseline built from below-median snapshots biases residuals positive. - Comparison method. Residuals here are not a template fit; the authors’ proposed test is to run actual Fermi pipelines on the simulations.

A potentially useful discriminant is that the excesses are typically flanked by in-plane flux deficits.

arXiv:2610.31745 §

FeynFacet: AI agents for analytic NLO QCD

Zhong-Bo Kang et al.

Kang, Vogelsang and Zhang use LLM agents to write and run the process-specific programs for analytic NLO calculations. Physicists fix the observables, projections and HVBM conventions, and the finished programs run without further model calls. The pipeline: - spin and colour algebra done in parallel with FeynCalc, FORM and Tracer; - reverse unitarity for the real-emission phase space; - dimensional shifts to remove evanescent numerators; - Kira IBP at symbolic \(d\), then SubTropica for the masters.

The new results are NLO transverse-spin-transfer hard functions: - all seven \(q^\uparrow b\to c^\uparrow X\) classes through \(\alpha_s^3\), including the regular-only \(q^\uparrow g\to\bar q^\uparrow X\) from identical-quark interference; - \(\ell q^\uparrow\to q^\uparrow X\) through \(\alpha_{\rm em}^2\alpha_s\), with the scattered lepton integrated.

They find \(\hat\sigma_{T,XX}=\hat\sigma_{T,NN}\) in every channel, so the spin dependence is purely \(\cos(\phi_a-\phi_c)\).

Validation reproduces published UU/LL \(pp\) and \(\ell p\) results, double-transverse \(pp\), and the one-loop TMD matching and soft functions. The checks on the new results are weaker than on the benchmarks: - The \(pp\) transfer results agree across the three algebra routes only at test points. Crossing from incoming-TT is symbolic only at Born level. - The \(\ell p\) result has no external benchmark, only Born normalisation, pole cancellation and RG checks. - All three routes share the same generated amplitudes, reduction and masters, so common-mode errors would go undetected.

As a test case for agentic theory work, the release of code alongside the coefficients is what makes it auditable.

Friday, October 23, 2026

arXiv:2610.31377 §

Measurements of the branching fractions of Λ_c⁺→ΛK⁺π⁰ and Λ_c⁺→ΛK⁺η decays at Belle and Belle II

Belle et al.

Belle and Belle II (980 + 570 fb\(^{-1}\)) report the first observation of \(\Lambda_c^+\to\Lambda K^+\eta\) at >10σ statistical significance, from 1107 ± 45 signal events. Relative to the reference mode, the measured ratio is \(\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{B}(\Lambda\pi^+\pi^0)=(8.73\pm0.35\pm0.43)\times10^{-3}\), giving \(\mathcal{B}=(6.20\pm0.25\pm0.31\pm0.30)\times10^{-4}\). The three SU(3)\(_F\) predictions are \((1.9\pm0.6)\), \((0.45\pm0.10)\) and \((0.59\pm0.24)\times10^{-4}\). The measurement exceeds them by 5.5σ, 11.2σ and 10.1σ respectively. The companion SCS mode is measured as \(\mathcal{B}(\Lambda K^+\pi^0)=(1.29\pm0.05\pm0.05\pm0.06)\times10^{-3}\). This agrees with BESIII and with the 2024 fit that includes the color-symmetric Hamiltonian (1.36 ± 0.25), and sits well below the earlier 3.5–4.5 × 10⁻³ predictions. The precision is markedly improved over BESIII’s ±0.27 statistical error.

So the same SU(3)\(_F\) fit that now gets \(\Lambda K^+\pi^0\) right undershoots \(\Lambda K^+\eta\) by an order of magnitude. That is the charm-baryon entry in the season’s flavor-symmetry strain noted in the digest. The caveat is visible in the paper itself. The two-body invariant-mass spectra deviate significantly from phase space, and efficiencies are reweighted to the data Dalitz distribution. The predictions assume non-resonant, S-wave-dominated meson pairs, so intermediate resonances in \(\Lambda K^+\eta\) (smaller phase space) could account for the excess rather than a failure of the symmetry itself. A Dalitz analysis would discriminate. On the experimental side, the η systematic is dominated by photon reconstruction (4.4%), and there is a 4.8% reference-BF uncertainty.

arXiv:2610.30239 §

Faithful Uncertainties in ML-Based Inverse Problems: GPs, NNs and NTKs for the Triplet PDF

Nina Elmer et al.

Methods that agree on the central \(T_3\) disagree on what their error bars mean. The setup is a closure test on BCDMS \(F_2^p-F_2^d\), using 248 points with the full covariance, NNPDF4.0 truth, and a data region of \(x\gtrsim0.07\). The normalised bias \(R_b\) is computed on a single \(L_1\) realisation:

Model \(R_b\) (full covariance) \(R_b\) (diagonal only)
RBF GP 1.08 0.38
Gibbs GP 0.86 0.53
NTK, trained 0.67 —
NTK, at initialisation 9.5 —
\(L_2\) NN ensemble 0.74 —
\(L_1\) NN ensemble 2.84 —

The \(L_1\) ensemble excludes data uncertainty by construction, so its large value is expected. Dropping covariance correlations shifts \(R_b\) non-monotonically, so pointwise width alone does not determine calibration. Two other failure modes stand out: - Heteroscedastic loss. It learns broad diagonal variances, which the authors read as likelihood misspecification rather than faithful uncertainty. - Endpoint preprocessing. It sets the small-\(x\) answer. The fit at \(\alpha=1\) versus \(\alpha=0.5\) is identical in the data region but diverges below 0.07. Preprocessed GP variance vanishes at the endpoints by construction, giving bands that miss the truth.

In the HERA extrapolation test, the initialisation NTK degrades NC-EM by \(\Delta n_\sigma\approx5.0\), while all other models stay below 1. The authors are candid about the limits. With \(n_{\rm eig}\) = 4–19, the test rules out gross underestimates but cannot certify faithfulness. A single seed visibly changes the reconstructions, and the \(L_2\) ensemble is only NNPDF-inspired.

arXiv:2610.30510 §

Same Events, Weaker Bound: Model Dependence in Magellanic Microlensing Limits on Primordial Black Holes

David Morales-Zapien et al.

The authors hold OGLE’s 13 events, efficiencies and \(t_E\) posteriors fixed and change only the rate model, inside OGLE’s own relaxed likelihood. The benchmark uses a flattened MW halo (\(q_h=0.72\), \(\rho_\odot=0.30\) GeV cm⁻³), a cored Burkert LMC halo, Ibata disks, and a lighter LMC with a shallower IMF. It weakens the 20-year relaxed limit by up to 1.6 near 0.32 \(M_\odot\). A deliberately extreme stress test weakens it by up to 3.4.

The headline “~6×” (6.2 for the benchmark, 13.2 for the stress test) is relative to the strict limit, so it also folds in OGLE’s own event-origin difference. In the planetary-mass window, the factors are 1.19 and 2.39 for smooth PBHs, and 3.4 and 6.0 for 30 pc clusters under a Lyα shot-noise cap. A one-at-a-time ablation at 0.45 \(M_\odot\) shows the IMF matters most (\(R=0.68\)), while the heavier disks push the other way (1.10). LMC-induced disequilibrium raises the lensing-weighted halo density by about 6%, undercutting a common critique.

The caveats are explicit: - Parameters were chosen to suppress the rate and are fixed, not marginalized. - The IMF endpoints are not a joint confidence region. - The stress test conflicts with 6D tracer masses. - Its isothermal LMC halo fails the LMC mass constraints.

Oddly, the weaker limits coexist with a larger preference for nonzero \(f\) near 0.03 \(M_\odot\): \(\Delta\chi^2=9.2\)–10.2, versus 3.7 in OGLE’s analysis.

Thursday, October 22, 2026

arXiv:2610.28744 §

The renormalization-group running of neutrino mixing is undetectable

Gabriela Barenboim et al.

Barenboim, Bustamante and Liu argue that every experiment measures one physical mixing matrix \(\hat U\). They define it by the eigenvectors of the on-shell physical mass matrix \(m_{\rm eff}=m(\mu)+\Sigma_M-\tfrac12(\Sigma_K^Tm+m\Sigma_K)\), which does not depend on \(\mu\). Momentum transfer \(Q\) never flows through the external-leg self-energy. If one sets \(\mu=Q\), that self-energy picks up \(\ln(Q^2/M_X^2)\) terms that rotate \(U(Q)\) back to \(\hat U\). The premise in the prior literature keeps \(U(Q)\) but drops these logs, which the authors call an inconsistent truncation.

Only vertex and box corrections carry both flavor and \(Q\) dependence, and they lack the \(1/(m_i-m_j)\) degeneracy enhancement. The paper gives their sizes in each setting: - MSSM: a flavor-diagonal ~0.7% rescaling of the \(\nu_\tau\) coupling at 10 TeV. - GeV HNL: at most \(4\times10^{-9}\). - Light-scalar seesaw: zero at one loop.

For Model 2 of Babu et al., the claimed running disappears once the Dirac masses are allowed to run along with \(Y_N\).

Independently of the theory argument, NuTeV and NOMAD limits on zero-baseline transitions would require \(|\Delta\theta_{23}|<0.7^\circ\), \(|\Delta\theta_{12}|<2.3^\circ\), \(|\Delta\theta_{13}|<2.4^\circ\) and \(|\Delta\delta_{\rm CP}|<16^\circ\) between \(m_\pi\) and a few GeV, if the premise held. This bound excludes the \(\beta_\delta=0.15\) used to illustrate a proposed reactor signal.

Caveats: - The data bounds assume one parameter shifts at a time, so correlated shifts could partly compensate. - They do not apply when running starts above a few GeV, as in the MSSM case or the ~5 GeV double-bang configuration. There only the theory argument applies. - Off-diagonal vertex terms beyond the SM, up to ~\(10^{-2}\), survive and act as non-standard charged-current interactions. - Two of the targeted papers are the authors’ own, including a step-at-\(Q=M\) HNL paper they now say also used an incorrect \(\delta m\). That self-correction gives the refutation more weight.

arXiv:2610.28731 §

Simulation-Informed High-Velocity Dark Matter in the Effective Theory of Direct Detection

Xiuyuan Zhang et al.

Zhang, Herrera, Thoyas and Necib build a local dark matter (DM) velocity distribution from two pieces: - a Gaia DR3 reconstruction, combining Gaia–Sausage–Enceladus stars convolved with a simulation-calibrated kernel and a generalized-Gaussian diffuse component; - the m12b LMC analog from FIRE-2, which contributes 32 particles at 738–846 km/s and 0.3% of the local density.

The resulting laboratory-frame endpoints, and the maximum accessible splitting \(\delta_{\max}\) for a 1 TeV particle on xenon, are:

Distribution Endpoint (km/s) \(\delta_{\max}\) (keV)
Standard Halo Model (SHM) 793 381
Reconstruction alone 835 423
Reconstruction + LMC 846 434
Auriga-25 (Au-25) 975 575

Limits across all 14 non-relativistic EFT operators coincide below \(\delta\approx200\) keV and then fan out. Velocity-suppressed operators separate first.

For \(\mathcal O_1\), the region that fits the LZ 248 keV event plus empty sidebands sits at different splittings: - SHM: \(\delta=304\)–360 keV. - FIRE-based: 320–390 keV. - Au-25: 376–448 keV, with mass confined to 171–468 GeV.

In the digest’s context, a tail reaching 1100 km/s would need support well beyond anything here.

The authors are explicit that the result is limited: - The tail rests on 32 particles. - The Sun has to be placed out of the disk to match the LMC geometry. With the observer in the disk, the LMC particles come out slower. - The Milky Way-only endpoint has a 16–84% posterior spread of 773–912 km/s. - Their counting likelihood differs from LZ’s published limits by up to ~3×. - They conclude that a single LMC analog cannot define the tail uniquely.

arXiv:2610.28742 §

Radiative corrections to double-β decay and their impact on experimental analyses

Jordy de Vries et al.

This is the full companion to the authors’ earlier Letter. In a heavy-nucleus EFT with explicit \(1^+\) intermediate states, de Vries, Mereghetti, el Morabit and Sandner compute all \(\mathcal O(\alpha)\) virtual and real corrections for arbitrary excitation energy \(\omega_n\). They also give new closed-form heavy-propagator triangle and box integrals via an auxiliary-mass trick.

Main structural results: - No \(Z\)-enhanced terms survive. The \(\mathcal O(\alpha Z)\) and \(\mathcal O(\alpha Z^2)\) terms cancel class by class, except the static Coulomb piece already contained in the Fermi functions. - A new topology matters. The electron–electron box has no single-β analogue and brings in dependence on the two-electron invariant mass \(s\). - The correction factorizes at large \(\omega_n\). At leading order in \(1/\omega_n\) it is the tree-level rate times a “double-weak Sirlin function” \(f_{2\nu}(\beta_1,\beta_2,\beta_{12})\). This function differs from the sum of two single-β Sirlin functions, and its \(\mu\) dependence cancels against \(g_V^4\) (\(g_V-1\approx1.9\)–2.0% at \(\mu=2E_0\)). - One new nuclear input appears at next order. It is \(\zeta_{31}\), a log-weighted \(M_{GT}\) ratio, which the authors find negligible. - There is a non-trivial check. The total-visible-energy spectrum has no \(\ln m_e\) dependence as \(m_e\to0\), as the Kinoshita–Lee–Nauenberg theorem requires.

Because the electron-only and calorimetric (\(\epsilon_{\rm tot}\)) spectra differ, the correction depends on how a detector handles the real photon. The authors provide the fully differential photon spectrum and ask experiments to simulate it. For \(0\nu\beta\beta\), the inclusive rate rises by ~5%, independent of the nuclear matrix elements and \(m_{\beta\beta}\).

The extracted text ends before the numerical \(\xi_{31}\)-bias results, so their size cannot be quoted here.

Acknowledged approximations: - \(\mathcal O(\alpha ZRE_e)\) finite-size terms are not yet mapped rigorously. - The Fermi function \(F\) is also used for the \(\beta_1\beta_2y_{12}\) term. - The \(1/\omega_n\) expansion holds for \(^{136}\)Xe, but the authors cite larger deviations for \(^{82}\)Se.
