arXiv:2610.27105 §

: The Electron-Recoil Response Tail and the LZ High-Energy Event Background

The published LZ response does not fix the ER leakage that the 248 keV event’s background estimate depends on. The authors rebuild the ER continuum from NEST’s EFT2024 configuration and get a per-decay leakage of \(P\simeq1.3\times10^{-7}\) at a 4.1σ displacement in \(N_e\). That yields \(N^{\rm ER}_{\rm leak}=(1.9\pm0.1\pm0.4)\times10^{-4}\) counts, against LZ’s 0.0106 total in the \(500<\mathrm{S1c}<600\) phd region. They then construct three families of distributions (generalised-normal, skew-t, Johnson \(S_U\)) that match the executable model’s mean, variance and skewness exactly. The families agree within a factor of ~2 at matched quantile deviation, yet the tail probability spans more than five orders of magnitude.

The thresholds are close to the model:

  • 0.14% quantile agreement: ER reaches 10% of the quoted background.
  • 0.47%: ER equals the whole quoted background.
  • 1.10%: ER reaches 0.1 counts.

LZ’s counter-argument, that no events appear between the event and the ER band, does not discriminate between these tails. Even the heaviest family predicts only 0.69 events in that gap. Two further points:

  • Printed vs. implemented response. The printed Supplement Eq. 4 gives a fluctuation width 1.475× the implemented one. Taken literally, it implies 0.60 ER counts, far above LZ’s quoted total for all backgrounds.
  • Calibration reach. The tritium and ¹⁴C calibrations stop at S1c = 500 phd, while the event sits at 540 phd.

Caveats. The authors explicitly do not claim the tail is wrong, and they do not propagate the result to LZ’s 2.6σ global (3.4σ local) significance. The link between the NEST commit and the Letter is circumstantial. The ER selection efficiency is unpublished. The ¹²⁴Xe/¹²⁵I lines are excluded.

What would settle it. A tail measurement at the \(10^{-5}\) level near \(N_q\sim5\times10^3\), or a shape fit using ~\(3\times10^3\) events per slice (rising to \(3\times10^4\) for the mildest problematic tail).

arXiv:2610.28506 §

: Isospin breaking in ρππ couplings

The missing isospin-breaking (IB) ρππ coupling correction comes out small and cuts the corresponding τ-route uncertainty to roughly a third of the White Paper’s assumed ±4.7×10⁻¹⁰. The method is a symmetry-preserving Dyson–Schwinger/Bethe–Salpeter contact-interaction model. IB enters dynamically through flavour-dependent beyond-rainbow-ladder couplings \(\xi_{u,d}\), tuned to \(\Delta m_\rho\), and explicitly through \(m_d/m_u\in[1,2]\). Results:

  • Coupling ratio: \(g_{\rho^0\pi\pi}/g_{\rho^+\pi\pi}=1.0024(17)(1)\).
  • Width difference: \(\Delta\Gamma_\rho=+0.39(51)\) MeV, compatible with but tighter than PDG (+0.31 ± 1.3) and a data-based extraction (−0.58 ± 1.01).
  • Effect on \(a_\mu\): the ρππ term is \(\Delta a_\mu=-2.32(1.64)\times10^{-10}\), about one-third of the White Paper’s error.

The −2.32 is not the net shift. It nearly cancels against the radiative-correction term (−2.22) and the pion-mass phase-space term (+4.09). The total form-factor-ratio IB is \(-0.25(1.64)\times10^{-10}\), compared with the older \(-1.62(65)\times10^{-10}\).

Caveats. - The contact interaction is a crude kernel; the symmetric coupling comes out at 6.29 against 5.94 from the PDG width. - The error is driven almost entirely by the poorly known input \(\Delta m_\rho=0.7(0.8)\) MeV. - The τ-side numbers rely on a Gounaris–Sakurai parametrisation. - The same framework predicts \(R_\phi=1.484\pm0.029\), 2.2σ below BESIII.

arXiv:2610.28283 §

: Automation of amplitude-level QED resummation with ACRAIC

The extracted text cuts off before the validation section, so the headline numbers here come from the abstract.

ACRAIC brings coherent (CEEX-style) amplitude-level YFS exponentiation to general processes inside Sherpa. Previously this existed only for hand-coded fermion-pair production in KKMC. The generalisation works in three steps:

  • Photon assignment. The initial/final assignment of photons becomes an assignment to emission stages. Gauge invariance forces these stages to be partitions of the charged legs into charge-neutral sets, including resonance stages for \(Z/\gamma^*\) and \(W\) pairs.
  • Remainders. The infrared-finite remainders are tree-level helicity amplitudes with \(N+2\) and \(N+3\) legs and partition-dependent propagator shifts, computed by Comix’s Berends–Giele recursion.
  • Virtual correction. The one-loop electroweak virtual enters as a per-helicity factor from OpenLoops or Recola.

Validation is against KKMC for \(e^+e^-\to\mu^+\mu^-\) at photonic \(\mathcal O(\alpha^2)\). Comparison is also made with the squared-level \(\text{YFS}_{\rm NLO_{EW}}\), which shares the resummation and differs only in matching. At \(\mathcal O(\alpha^2)\) the two matchings agree on the size of the missing higher orders to ~\(10^{-3}\), and the perturbative uncertainty drops about tenfold relative to \(\mathcal O(\alpha)\). The author reports that squared-level matching can go unstable in four-lepton production while the amplitude-level version stays stable. Example applications are the FCC-ee Higgs recoil mass and Z-pole distributions.

Caveats. - “Photonic \(\mathcal O(\alpha^2)\)” omits two-loop \(\gamma Z\) boxes and weak corrections. - The paper is described as mostly technical, with phenomenology left to dedicated studies.

arXiv:2610.26792 §

First observation of weak magnetism in neutron decay

Miriam Camarasa-Domene et al.

The weak-magnetism form factor comes out as \(g_M = 4.47(87)\), which excludes \(g_M=0\) at 5.1σ and sits 0.3σ from the CVC value of 4.71. The β asymmetry alone cannot separate \(g_M\) from \(\lambda\). The authors fix \(\lambda = -1.27598(61)\) independently, using \(r_n\tau_n(1+3\lambda^2)=2\mathcal{F}t\) with: - \(\tau_n=878.36(43)\) s, the PDG bottle average with \(S=1.81\); - \(\mathcal{F}t=3070.01(1.90)\) s from \(^{26m}\)Al alone, reanalyzed with updated charge radii.

They then reinterpret the asymmetry data as an effective \(\tilde A\) that carries explicit \(g_M\) dependence. Two refinements matter: - They evaluate the energy moments over each experiment’s actual window rather than the full phase space. This raises the \(g_M\) sensitivity relative to the earlier 3σ global fit. - The linearized version, \(g_M = 4.40(50)_{\mathcal{F}t}(39)_{\tau_n}(59)_{\tilde A}\), shows that all three inputs contribute comparably to the error.

The robustness checks are the main content. The significance ranges across dataset choices as follows:

Dataset choice \(g_M\) Significance
Lifetime set including beam values (\(S=2.3\)) 4.63(92) 5.0σ
Adding the \(a\), \(B\), \(\lambda_{AB}\) correlations 4.85(85) 5.7σ
PERKEO III + UCNτ only 4.68(81) 5.8σ
Post-2012 asymmetries only (no scale factor needed) 5.00(82) 6.1σ

Fitting the spectral shape of \(A(E_e)\) would tighten \(\sigma(g_M)\) by only 3–7%, based on Asimov studies. A recast of the PERKEO III Fierz likelihood gives a consistent 5.07(92).

Caveats: - The baseline \(\mathcal{F}t\) rests on a single transition. It carries a \(\delta_{\rm NS}\) error (1.44 s) that is comparable to its statistical error. - The \(\lambda\) determination relies on the SM relation between \(\tau_n\) and \(\mathcal{F}t\). The result is therefore a consistency test within the SM, not a model-independent extraction.

arXiv:2610.24068 §

Measurement of the neutrino cross section across the TeV scale using IceCube data

Rasmi Hajjar et al.

The analysis uses only the public eight-year IceCube upgoing-track sample: 305,735 events, more than 99.9% pure \(\nu_\mu\) CC. It extracts the average ν/ν̄ CC cross section in five bins from 150 GeV to 40 TeV. Earth absorption and the event energy distribution break the flux–cross-section degeneracy. Relative to CSMS, the normalizations are:

Bin Normalization
0.15–0.36 TeV \(1.11^{+0.12}_{-0.13}\)
0.36–1.17 TeV \(1.22^{+0.24}_{-0.17}\)
1.17–3.79 TeV \(1.11^{+0.15}_{-0.12}\)
3.79–12.3 TeV \(0.92^{+0.15}_{-0.12}\)
12.3–40 TeV \(1.01^{+0.15}_{-0.13}\)

All bins are within 20% and consistent with the SM. This bridges the energy-resolved collider points (up to about 2 TeV) and the astrophysical ones (above about 10 TeV). Between 360 GeV and 2 TeV the uncertainties already beat FASER’s.

Freeing ν and ν̄ separately gives a non-zero ν̄ cross section above 360 GeV at only 1.6σ (p = 0.06). That drops to 1.1σ if \(\sigma_\nu>\sigma_{\bar\nu}\) is enforced, because it is partly driven by an anticorrelated swap in bin 4 (ν: 0.40, ν̄: 2.1).

The main caveat for anyone treating this as a reference curve is the first bin. It carries a 16% Gaussian prior from fixed-target data, and that prior anchors the atmospheric flux normalization. Without it: - the fit prefers a lower flux; - the other four bins shift up by 20–30%; - the first bin lands about 2σ above CSMS.

So the result is limited by the flux–cross-section anticorrelation and is partly calibrated by accelerator input. A tenfold exposure forecast gives under 10% on the average cross section and more than 7σ for ν̄.

arXiv:2610.26648 §

Dark Matter Interpretations of the Galactic Center Gamma-Ray Excess with 18 Years of Fermi-LAT Data and the FL16Y Source Catalog

Sang Hwan Kim et al.

The GCE is re-extracted from 17.7 years of FRONT-only data with FL16Y masks, scanning 80 GALPROP diffuse models. Model X fits best, ahead of the next model by \(\Delta(-2\ln\mathcal L)=72\). Systematics come from a full 14×14 covariance built from 22 Galactic-plane control regions.

Against new combined limits from 30 dwarfs, with J-factor uncertainties profiled, the picture is: - \(b\bar b\) and other hadronic channels: the best fit is (39.5 GeV, \(1.2\times10^{-26}\) cm³/s) with χ² = 27.5/12. Its entire 68% region lies above the dSph limit, as do those for \(c\bar c\), light quarks and gluons. - Effective four-body channels: the 68% regions for \(4b\) and \(2b2\tau\) are fully excluded. - \(\tau^+\tau^-\): the best fit (9.3 GeV, χ² = 22.8/12) sits just 0.67% above the limit, and part of its 68% region survives. - \(4\tau\): the best fit is 8.7% above the limit, and part of its 68% region survives. - \(4b\) cascades: these fit best near \(r=m_\phi/m_\chi\simeq0.3\). Per the abstract, part of the 68% region stays below the limits for \(r\lesssim0.4\).

AMS-02 antiprotons are generally weaker than the dwarfs and constrain only part of the \(4b\) cascade region. That antiproton likelihood uses the BIG propagation model and includes only the experimental covariance; adding propagation-model uncertainties could weaken it further.

For GCE model-building, including today’s dark-Higgs construction, this leaves a τ-rich or light-mediator corridor.

Caveats are substantial: - The exclusions are conditional on Model X and a fixed γ = 1.2 gNFW J-factor. - The 80-model envelope, a factor 3–4 above 1 GeV, is shown but not propagated. - Even the best fits have χ²/dof ≈ 2. - Extending the dwarf data from 14.3 to 17.7 years weakens the high-mass limits for every catalog tested, and the authors do not yet understand why.

arXiv:2610.23675 §

Complete g⁶ pressure of hot Yang–Mills theory

Keijo Kajantie et al.

The missing nonlogarithmic hard coefficient is \(\tilde\beta_{\rm E1}=6236\pm40\). That converts to \(q_c(N_c{=}3,N_f{=}0)=-3591.2\pm16.9_{\rm pert}\pm250_{\rm mag}\), consistent with the old lattice-inferred \(\tilde\beta_{\rm E1}\simeq6000\pm600\).

Method. - The 65 four-loop vacuum graphs give about \(10^5\) sum-integrals. These reduce to 21 canonical structures, and \(\xi\)-dependence cancels at that stage. - Nine nonfactorizing sum-integrals were unknown. The authors evaluated them with thermal loop-tree duality. Matsubara sums are done analytically, and new local IR counterterms for zero-mode soft regions are added to BPHZ-style UV subtractions. The finite remainders were Monte Carlo integrated, at roughly \(10^6\) CPU-hours. - The pole coefficients come out analytically. They confirm the previously inferred hard-scale IR poles and their explicit cancellation against EQCD/MQCD. - The method independently reproduces the known \(I_6\).

Lattice comparison. The comparison uses Borsanyi et al. data in an EQCD-resummed scheme, with scale set by FAC and varied by a factor of 2. The band agrees with the data down to a few \(T_c\). In the same scheme, \(g^3\) and \(g^4\) overshoot Stefan–Boltzmann and \(g^5\) undershoots. The authors read this as support for the “Laine conjecture”.

Caveats. - The magnetic constant \(B_G\) now dominates the error, by a factor of about 15 over the new perturbative piece. - The full result holds only for SU(3), because the four-loop MQCD matching is incomplete for general \(N_c\). - The “only at \(g^6\)” statement is tied to this resummation prescription. The authors note that other schemes behave better at lower orders.

arXiv:2610.23676 §

The pressure of the gluon plasma at 𝒪(g_s⁶⁾, perturbatively

Mikael Chala et al.

This is an independent route to the same quantity, giving \(q_c(0)=-3846\pm515\). That is about 0.5σ from the value \(-3526(4)(55)(30)\) obtained by fitting the \(g^6\) formula to Borsanyi et al. trace-anomaly data over \(10\)–\(1000\,\Lambda_{\overline{\rm MS}}\).

Method. - Background-field Feynman gauge, with a different propagator basis. - Reduction via SIRENA using unimodular loop-momentum changes and no genuine four-loop IBP. This yields 10 connected and 6 factorizable sum-integrals. - The connected integrals are evaluated numerically: Schwinger parameters, sector/subsector decomposition, partial Poisson resummation, and Sobol QMC with 4 Matsubara modes. - Poles are cross-checked by doing the hard-to-soft matching in the full bosonic SM 3D EFT. This needs a four-loop EFT vacuum counterterm that they compute for the first time. The resulting 22 equations have rank 14 and fix 7 pole combinations.

Lattice comparison. The strict expansion at \(\bar\mu=2\pi T\) matches the lattice within 3% above \(2.5\,\Lambda_{\overline{\rm MS}}\), and within 1.5% from \(3\) to \(1000\,\Lambda_{\overline{\rm MS}}\).

Caveats. - The error is about twice the Helsinki total. The two \(\beta_G\) errors alone account for only about \(\pm170\) of it, so the numerical sum-integral allowances dominate. - Both groups compare against the same lattice dataset, so the lattice check is not independent across the two papers. - The cross-check between the groups covers one shared master integral and the final \(q_c\); the two central values differ by about 255.

arXiv:2610.22490 §

Explaining b→sℓ⁺ℓ⁻ with CKM Unitarity Violation? A No-Go Theorem

Andreas Crivellin et al.

Updated fit. The authors refit LFU \(b\to s\ell\ell\) data with new LHCb/CMS inputs. The best fit is \(C_9=-0.87\), \(C_{10}=0.24\), at 5.4σ (\(\Delta\chi^2\approx33\)). This excludes the bins nearest the \(J/\psi\).

The proposed escape. Rescaling \(V_{cb}\), and hence \(V_{ts}\), alone can buy about 23 units of \(\chi^2\). The natural operator for this is \(C^{(3)}_{23}\) (for \(C^{(3)}_{22}\), the required couplings are \(\mathcal O(10)\)). But SMEFT/WET running gives \(C_9\approx(33C^{(3)}_{23}+24C^{(1)}_{23})\) and \(C_{10}\approx-20(30C^{(3)}_{23}+27C^{(1)}_{23})\), in units of TeV/\(\Lambda^2\) at \(\Lambda=2\) TeV. So custodial \(C^{(1)}=-C^{(3)}\) at the high scale does not survive.

Why it fails. - Requiring the right-sign \(\delta V_{cb}\) together with \(C_9<0\) forces \(C_{10}\lesssim-22C_9\). - The only good-fit region is fine-tuned: \(C^{(1)}_{23}\approx-1.1C^{(3)}_{23}\), \(C^{(3)}_{23}\approx-1\). There the \(V_{cb}\) shift has the wrong sign and is overcompensated by \(Z\)-\(b\)-\(s\). - A 10% positive shift already raises the \(B_s\)-mixing prediction by about 20%. - Vector-like quarks at 2 TeV, either \(U\) alone or the custodial pairs \(D{+}T_2\) and \(T_1{+}T_2\), cap \(C_9\) below a quarter of the needed size, given \(B_s\) mixing, EWPO and \(\epsilon_K\).

Scope caveats. - Right-handed currents (\(O_{Hud}\)) are excluded. The authors argue these cannot resolve the inclusive/exclusive \(V_{cb}\) split. - The analysis fixes the scale at 2 TeV. - The underlying anomaly still rests on form-factor and nonlocal-hadronic inputs.

arXiv:2610.21479 §

Determining the Threshold Excess in Top-Quark Pair Production through a Global PDF Analysis

Richard D. Ball et al.

The fit. Ball and ter Hoeve float a threshold excess Δ alongside \(m_t\), \(\alpha_s(m_Z)\) and the PDFs. They use the Theory Covariance Method on ATLAS and CMS \(m_{t\bar t}\)-differential data, measured against fixed-order NNLO QCD with NLO EW and missing-higher-order uncertainties. The Δ prior is deliberately broad, at 200% of a 6.43 pb (13 TeV) / 2.19 pb (8 TeV) central value.

Results. - 13 TeV: \(\Delta = 14.14^{+6.44}_{-6.31}\) pb and \(m_t = 172.85^{+0.59}_{-0.58}\) GeV, which is 2.2σ from the continuum. The \(m_t\) error is 2.3× larger than in their Δ-free fit, and the correlation is \(\rho(\Delta,m_t)=0.84\). - 8 TeV: \(\Delta = 0.57\pm3.83\) pb, or \(3.28^{+2.66}_{-2.32}\) pb with positivity imposed, with \(\rho=0.69\). They attribute the energy dependence to the gluon being probed at higher \(x\) at 8 TeV. - Conditioned on \(m_t=172.5\) GeV: \(\Delta = 11.14^{+4.08}_{-4.41}\) pb. This is close to the ATLAS (9.3 pb) and CMS (8.8 pb) toponium cross sections but with larger errors, which the authors suggest come from the PDF correlation.

Why it matters. Any threshold-excess number is effectively a statement about \(m_t\) as well. Conversely, \(m_t\) fits that ignore Δ absorb part of the excess.

Caveats. - No individual 13 TeV dataset deviates significantly except CMS dilepton, at about 2σ. The excess only appears in the combination, whose central value exceeds every individual one; the authors explain this as a BLUE-type correlated-combination effect. - The 68% intervals use only replicas with Δ > 0. - Bin migrations from the top width are neglected. - The ATLAS 13 TeV inputs use 36 fb⁻¹. - Δ is defined relative to fixed-order NNLO, not NRQCD.

The proposed way to break the Δ–\(m_t\) degeneracy is to add spin-correlation observables to the fit.

arXiv:2610.21041 §

Testing the inelastic dark matter interpretation of the LZ event with heavy-element experiments

Peter W. Graham et al.

The halo question. For the 1.1 TeV thermal Higgsino, the 248 keV event’s preferred splitting depends on the halo model, but stays below the solar-capture bound (δ ≲ 566 keV) in the standard ones:

Halo model Efficiency window Preferred δ (keV) Status
SHM LZ ROI (0–250 keV) 340–360 Excluded by solar bound
Simulated LMC LZ ROI 495–515 Excluded by solar bound
SHM / LMC Full 0–650 keV Contours drop below the Higgsino line Disfavoured
LMC tail cut 100× (\(w=0.0026\%\)) Full 440–510 Moves up to the Higgsino line, still excluded
LMC tail extended to \(v_{\max}=1100\) km/s at 10% flux Full 500–600 Partly survives

Two numbers frame the last two rows: - The 100× flux reduction corresponds to \(w=0.0026\%\), two orders of magnitude below the usual benchmarks. - The 10% flux of the extended tail sits an order of magnitude below the lowest flux current simulations resolve.

The authors’ conclusion is that the Higgsino question becomes a halo-simulation-resolution question.

Proposed tests. - Pb paleodetectors (Laurionite, 60 cm³, 15 nm SAXs): cover most of the event contour. - Two different elements: fix \(m_\chi\) and δ through the apex recoil energy. - Mineral-age scans: the signal-to-noise should peak at 50–100 Myr if the tail comes from the LMC. - JUNO with a (20 m)³ Pb or ²³⁸U shell: covers the 566–600 keV window, and measures δ directly from the photon line. Varying the shell yields the decay length, which is calculable for the Higgsino.

Caveats. - The analysis rests on a single event. - The contours are relative likelihoods, not goodness of fit. - The efficiency is assumed to stay near 1 up to 650 keV, beyond LZ’s conservative ROI. - The extended tail is admittedly speculative.

arXiv:2610.22219 §

Gallium Anomaly and Solar Neutrinos

Steven R. Elliott et al.

The calibration. Elliott and Robertson use the Sun as a calibrated source. Its strength is fixed by Borexino elastic-scattering fluxes (⁷Be, CNO), the SNO neutral-current ⁸B flux, and a luminosity-constrained pp flux of \(5.966(27)\times10^{10}\) cm⁻²s⁻¹. Survival probabilities use JUNO and Daya Bay mixing, and solar models enter only through the MSW density profile.

Result. The predicted rate is \(67.5^{+1.4}_{-1.1}\) SNU against a measured \(66.1\pm3.1\) SNU from SAGE and Gallex/GNO, a ratio of \(0.980^{+0.050}_{-0.049}\). The radioactive-source experiments give \(0.817^{+0.028}_{-0.048}\). More than 90% of the solar rate comes from the ground-state cross section derived via detailed balance.

Why it matters. This disfavours the proposed ~20% factorization breakdown (Cadeddu et al.), which sits roughly 3.5σ from the solar ratio, and any error in the ⁷¹Ga cross section more generally. It also validates the extraction and counting efficiencies of SAGE, Gallex and BEST.

Correction to the digest’s framing. This removes the mundane explanations, not the anomaly. The authors conclude that its “source evidently lies elsewhere” and say nothing against sterile neutrinos.

Caveat. The solar rate is dominated by pp neutrinos (40 of 67.5 SNU). The test therefore weights the cross section differently from the source experiments.

Full text was not supplied for arXiv:2610.22224 or arXiv:2610.20793, so they are not covered here.

arXiv:2610.20611 §

A global analysis of B̅→DP and B̅→D̅P decays

Bhubanjyoti Bhattacharya et al.

Adding \(D^0\)–\(\bar D^0\) interference observables pushes the SU(3)\(_F\)-breaking requirement from ~20% to ~60%. The SU(3)\(_F\)-limit fit to all 25 observables (15 branching ratios, 6 direct and 4 time-dependent CP asymmetries, with \(\beta\), \(\beta_s\), \(\gamma\) fixed to world averages) gives \(\chi^2_{\min}/{\rm dof}=153.4/12\). The branching ratios alone are already bad: \(\bar B\to DP\) gives 105/5. Splitting by strangeness, the \(\Delta S=0\) fit is excellent (0.2/1), but \(|T_1/T_0|-1=0.15\pm0.03\) and \(|C_1/C_0|-1=0.36\pm0.13\).

Allowing first-order breaking diagrams but capping their ratios at 30% leaves \(\chi^2_{\min}=17.0\), and every ratio saturates the cap. The dominant pulls come from \(\mathcal A_{CP}(B^-\to D_{CP}\pi^-)\) (\(\chi^2=10.3\)) and \(S(\bar B^0\to D^-\pi^+)\). Raising the cap to 60% brings \(\chi^2_{\min}\) down to 2.8. The remainder is irreducible: - ~1.0 from an isospin-related pair of branching ratios; - ~1.4 from the measured \(S(D^\pm\pi^\mp)\) central values exceeding the bound \(2\kappa/(1+\kappa^2)=0.0341\) that their own branching ratios imply.

Alongside the ~1000% needed in \(B\to PP\), this is a second system where the symmetry expansion is under strain.

Caveats: - The 30% and 60% fits use 35 parameters for 25 observables, so there are no degrees of freedom. The case for >30% breaking rests on cap saturation and the \(\chi^2\)-vs-\(R\) scan. - Many fitted ratios are poorly determined (e.g. \(r_{T_1}=0.6\pm0.5\)). - The \(\bar B\to\bar DP\) sector has only five measured branching ratios. - Isospin breaking is ignored.

Measuring V_cb at electron-proton colliders

David Marzocca

The projection is a sub-percent \(|V_{cb}|\) from \(b\)-tagged charged-current DIS. The fit uses binwise ratios of \(b\)-tagged to flavour-inclusive CC counts in \((x,y)\). It profiles projected PDFs, the other CKM elements, top background and tag calibration, with no \(|V_{cb}|\) prior or unitarity input. Projected precision:

Collider Luminosity Unpolarised Polarised
LHeC 1 ab\(^{-1}\) 0.89% 0.79% (\(P_e=-0.8\))
FCC-eh 2 ab\(^{-1}\) — 0.67%
EIC — — 22–32%

At the EIC, \(|V_{cb}|=0\) remains allowed within 3σ. For comparison, the PDG gives \(|V_{cb}|_{\rm excl}=39.5\pm0.5\) and \(|V_{cb}|_{\rm incl}=42.0\pm0.5\) (×10⁻³), so the LHeC number would be competitive with orthogonal systematics.

Calibration, not statistics, sets the floor. At the LHeC the error grows from 0.27% (statistics only) to 0.49% with CKM, PDF and top uncertainties, then to 0.79% once tag calibration is added. The \(b\)-efficiency calibration enters as roughly \(\delta_b/2\); its correlation with \(\mu_{cb}\) is −64%. Halving calibration widths gives 0.60%, and a better tagger on top gives 0.55% at the LHeC and 0.40% at FCC-eh. Using today’s PDF4LHC21 instead of projected PDFs degrades the LHeC result to 1.0%. Mistag-shape uncertainties of 5% and 10% give 0.91% and 1.0%.

Caveats: - The calculation is Born-level with flat tag responses. - Radiative \(b\) production and photoproduction are not treated. - The neutral-current background is only modelled via an assumed electron-loss probability.

arXiv:2610.14144 §

Planck-Scale Constraints on Quantum Gravity-Induced Neutrino Decoherence with IceCube

R. Abbasi et al.

IceCube sees no oscillation damping in 368,071 up-going \(\nu_\mu\) events (10.7 yr, \(E_\text{reco}\) 0.5–100 TeV). The sample is the same as the sterile-neutrino analysis, now at 99.9% purity and split into starting and throughgoing tracks. The decoherence fits use a Lindblad operator with a single rate \(\Gamma(E)=\Gamma_0(E/1\,\text{TeV})^n\), tested in two forms: “state selection” (all eight off-identity entries \(\Gamma\)) and “phase perturbation.” The 90% CL limits at \(E_0=1\) TeV are \(\Gamma_0<2.4\times10^{-15}\), \(4.6\times10^{-16}\), \(7.3\times10^{-19}\) and \(6.1\times10^{-21}\) eV for \(n=0\)–3. Against the 2024 IceCube result (quoted at \(E_0=1\) GeV), the gains are about 13× at \(n=2\) and about 30× at \(n=3\). At \(n=3\) the limit falls below the natural expectation \(\Gamma_0=E_0^n/M_\text{Pl}^{n-1}\) for the first time, so a cubic-scaling effect would need extra suppression or a non-power-law form. The \(n=2\) case matters most for theory, because it is the scaling found in string-motivated black-hole and D-foam calculations.

Caveats: - \(n=3\) margin. Evaluating the paper’s formula at \(E_0=1\) GeV gives about \(6.7\times10^{-30}\) eV for the natural expectation, against a limit of \(6.05\times10^{-30}\). That is a marginal crossing, not a deep exclusion. - \(n=0\). The new limit is actually looser than 2024 (\(2.36\) vs \(1.17\times10^{-15}\) eV). - Model assumptions. CPT conservation is assumed, since IceCube cannot separate \(\nu\) from \(\bar\nu\). The \(D\) matrix is diagonal, and decoherence is taken as independent of matter density. - Fit quality. Pre-unblinding goodness-of-fit p-values were only 4–5%.

arXiv:2610.17177 §

MadNIS, Ready for Production at LO

Theo Heimel et al.

MadNIS now covers full \(pp\) processes with all their partonic subprocesses, and it runs untuned from defaults set by a three-iteration VEGAS survey. It will be MadGraph7’s LO default. The main additions are: - learned sampling of symmetry permutations, and of flavour with a PDF prior; - merging of matrix elements, channels and, optionally, subprocesses; - buffered training on partially unweighted events; - concurrent training and generation on GPU.

On an H100 with training excluded (\(\kappa_\infty\)), MadNIS beats VEGAS on every benchmark, by 1.27× (\(t\bar t\) dileptonic) up to 247× (\(\ell\ell+3j\)). Distributions match a sliced MG5aMC baseline in pulls, including the tails.

The amortization numbers the digest flagged look like this for \(t\bar t+nj\) on an A100. Break-even with VEGAS comes at \(9\times10^6\), \(1.5\times10^5\) and \(6\times10^3\) events for \(n=1,2,3\), after 8 s, 11 s and 1.4 min of training. At \(10^3\) iterations, the speed-ups are already 2.9, 8.9 and 16.

Caveats: - Training cost of the headline numbers. The process-zoo figures come from trainings of up to 6.7–8.8 h. - Retraining. Any change of process, cuts or parameters requires a new training. - CPU. CPU training is more than 10× slower in wall-clock time. - Open choices. When to merge subprocesses is unresolved, and the defaults are “likely to evolve” (alpha release v0.2.1). - Low multiplicity. At low multiplicity, VEGAS combined with MadSpace mappings already captures most of the gain.

arXiv:2610.15248 §

When hadrons collide: di-J/ψ structure in the final state (Eric S. Swanson (University of Pittsburgh))

Swanson gets a \(\chi^2/\text{ndf}=56/50\) fit to the LHCb di-\(J/\psi\) spectrum (6.2–7.7 GeV) without introducing any states. The production model is a compact octet–octet \(cc\bar c\bar c\) source in \(2^{++}\), rescattering through ten charmonium-pair channels. Quark-exchange kernels come from a constituent model whose parameters are fixed by the charmonium spectrum, and the scattering is solved to all orders with a Kohn–Hulthén–Kato resonating-group method.

The structures map onto thresholds: - a \(J/\psi J/\psi\) threshold enhancement in the \(X(6600)\) region; - a dip from flux lost to the \(J/\psi\psi(2S)\) channel, followed by a peak at 6.9 GeV; - a 7.2 GeV shoulder between the \(J/\psi\psi(3S)\) and \(\psi(2S)\psi(2S)\) thresholds.

Only three background parameters are free (\(a\), \(c\), \(\lambda\)), with DPS fixed to LHCb’s template. The fit needs both an incoherent remainder (\(c=0\) gives 90/51) and a corrected continuum slope (\(\lambda=0\) gives 152/51). The amplitude has poles at \(6809-68i\) and \(7102-57i\) MeV. Swanson argues these reflect a repulsive-shell/attractive-core interaction rather than tetraquarks.

Caveats: - CMS fit. CMS is fitted less well (117/55), and its dips are deeper than the model’s. - Missing and spurious features. The model undershoots the 6.9 GeV peak by 15–20% and predicts an unobserved \(\psi(2S)\psi(2S)\) bump near 7.4 GeV. - Omitted physics. Quark annihilation is left out, which removes absorption into \(D^{(*)}\bar D^{(*)}\). \(J/\psi\psi(3770)\) is excluded because it couples only through tensor forces. - \(\Upsilon\Upsilon\) test. The prediction gives broad features at 19.3 and 19.7 GeV and a sharp dip near 20.05 GeV. Because an earlier loop-cusp model predicted the same enhancements, this tests threshold rescattering in general, not this model specifically.

arXiv:2610.13873 §

Complete four-loop pressure of cold QED

Aapeli K\"arkk\"ainen et al.

The N³LO pressure of cold, dense massless QED (jellium setup) is now complete, the first new order since Freedman–McLerran in 1977. The soft and mixed sectors were already known, so the missing piece was the hard contribution: 10 four-loop 1PI vacuum diagrams at finite μ. The authors generated them in general \(R_\xi\) gauge and confirmed that the gauge dependence cancels. Canonicalization reduces \(O(10^5)\) scalar integrals to 75 masters, compared with 114 in QCD. The methods were: - cutting rules and self-energy techniques for the factorizable topologies, - IBP plus differential equations as a cross-check on a subset, - thermal loop-tree duality (LTD) Monte Carlo for roughly 70% of the integrals, at about \(10^6\) CPU-core hours.

The hard IR pole cancels exactly against the mixed sector. The new constant term is \(p_{3,5}=1.02270(2)-5.64(5)N_f^{-1}-8.50(7)N_f^{-2}\). Two independent checks hold up: - The Bugblatter sum gives \(b=-5.44(24)\times10^{-7}\), against the literature value \(-5.545(12)\times10^{-7}\). - Renormalization-group invariance holds through N³LO.

Along the way the authors corrected two errors in their earlier work: an \(O(\varepsilon)\) normalization error in the mixed term, and a factor of two in the N²LO handling that shifted \(p_{3,4}\).

The physics result is the uncertainty story. At \(\alpha_e=0.2\), order-to-order shifts far exceed the \(X\in[1/2,2]\) scale band. The Bayesian abc error band at N²LO, by contrast, contains the actual N³LO result near its center, and the series stays predictive up to \(\alpha_e\approx0.4\). Because the physical \(\alpha\) is small, the direct phenomenology is negligible. The value is as a benchmark and as a partial integral library for cold quark matter, and hence for the neutron-star EoS. One caveat is that the Bayesian conclusion rests on modelling the coefficients as draws from a common distribution.

arXiv:2610.13097 §

Threshold enhancement in π⁰π⁰ from ψ(3686)→π⁰π⁰J/ψ

BESIII Collaboration et al.

BESIII selects \(8.4\times10^6\) events from \(2.7\times10^9\) \(\psi(3686)\) decays, using \(J/\psi\to ee,\mu\mu\) and a 7C kinematic fit. Background is about 0.1% and non-peaking. A Breit–Wigner fit to the threshold structure gives: - 2554 ± 62 signal events, at more than 10σ, - \(M=274.3\pm2.1\pm0.6\) MeV, - \(\Gamma=17.1\pm1.5\pm0.5\) MeV.

These values are consistent with the charged channel (about 283 MeV and 17 MeV). The neutral mode carries no I=1 component and no ISR background, which makes it the cleaner confirmation.

The unfolded 2D spectrum, in \(m_{\pi\pi}\) and \(\cos\theta_{\pi^0}\), discriminates between models: - ChPT: adding FSI improves χ²/ndf from 48.6 to 6.5, but the model still misses the threshold region. - QCDME: with 2S–1D mixing fixed at \(\theta_{\rm mix}=12^\circ\) and FSI included, it describes the full spectrum. - Adler zero: the dip near 0.3 GeV persists, which BESIII ties to the Adler zero.

Caveats: - The mixing angle had to be fixed because it is strongly correlated with β. - A systematic trend remains in the \(\cos\theta\) pulls. - The ChPT parameter errors are implausibly tiny (e.g. \(g_1/g_0=-0.09426\pm0.00002\)). - The introduction says a structure near the π⁺π⁻ threshold would favor a rescattering cusp, but the paper never states whether the measured mass supports that reading. That question is left to theorists.

arXiv:2610.13867 §

Redshift-space galaxy power spectrum at two loops

Oliver H. E. Philcox

(The extracted text stops partway through the numerical section; the validation numbers below come from the abstract.)

Philcox gives the first complete two-loop, redshift-space galaxy \(P(k)\) in the EFT of large-scale structure (EFTofLSS). The model is renormalized at fifth order in fields and derivatives, and its ingredients are: - 17 leading-gradient, 7 next-to-leading-gradient and 1 next-to-next-to-leading-gradient bias operators, - spatially non-local counterterms generated by the dark-matter equations of motion, - 15 redshift-space counterterm combinations, - 6 stochastic terms.

That totals 49 coefficients, against 10 at one loop. Closure under renormalization is verified exactly at every power of \(f\). IR resummation is applied without the usual factorized approximation. The same kernels fix the deterministic one-loop bispectrum and trispectrum, and 18 of the coefficients are constrained by the one-loop bispectrum.

The loop integrals are precomputed with vegas, so a likelihood evaluation takes milliseconds. On simulations, \(k_{\max}\) rises from 0.2 to 0.34 \(h\)/Mpc for an LRG sample at z = 0.5. The new biases and counterterms absorb most of the added information, however, and \(\sigma_8\) does not tighten appreciably. The author also stresses strong parameter degeneracies. On its own the result is a template and a calibration of two-loop expectations, not yet a cosmology gain. Its main promise is in joint fits with higher-order statistics.

(No full text was provided for the other two picks, CMS arXiv:2610.13634 and Lee et al. arXiv:2610.13930, so they are omitted here.)

arXiv:2610.12208 §

Evidence for Normal Neutrino Ordering with Combined Atmospheric Neutrino Experiments

Miaochen Jin et al.

Inverted ordering is excluded at 3.3σ using atmospheric data alone. Only 2 of 4397 IO pseudoexperiments exceed the observed \(\chi^2_{\rm IO}-\chi^2_{\rm NO}=11.0\). The Clopper–Pearson 68% interval on that tail fraction gives \(Z\in[3.08,3.60]\sigma\), and the more conservative \(CL_s\) gives 2.55σ. The ingredients are: - Super-K I–V (pure water), with an independent high-statistics GENIE Monte Carlo and a bug-fixed SK release confirmed by the collaboration. - IceCube-DeepCore, 8 years. - ORCA6.

Flux and cross-section nuisances (39 of 143) are shared across experiments. The individual preferences (\(\Delta\chi^2=6.2\), 1.6, 0.5) add coherently via mantle/core MSW and parametric resonances at 2–10 GeV. Relative to a trivial \(\chi^2\) sum, the correlated treatment tightens \(\Delta m^2_{31}\) by 19% and \(\sin^2\theta_{23}\) by 9%. Results: \(\Delta m^2_{31}=2.44^{+0.06}_{-0.04}\times10^{-3}\) eV² (2.2%), \(\sin^2\theta_{23}=0.52\) with \(\Delta\chi^2=0.3\) between octants, and \(\delta_{CP}=1.30^{+0.24}_{-0.28}\pi\) (≈0.3π disfavoured at >2.7σ). The atmospheric contours sit closer to T2K than to NOvA. The authors estimate that adding JUNO’s \(\Delta m^2_{31}\) gives \(\Delta\chi^2\simeq16\), near 4σ, but that figure still needs joint pseudoexperiments.

Caveats. - The overall goodness of fit is \(p=0.011\), driven by ORCA6; it rises to 0.12 once finite Monte Carlo statistics are included. - The parameter goodness-of-fit test shows 2.2σ inter-experiment tension. IceCube and ORCA6 worsen by 13.7 and 9.6 in \(\chi^2\) at the combined best fit. - The shared CCQE normalization is pulled by −2.5σ. - \(\theta_{13}\) is fixed, and the ORCA energy-scale nuisance is absent. - The \(\delta_{CP}\) constraint draws more on data residuals than on the Asimov expectation. - This is a combination of public data, not a collaboration result.

arXiv:2610.11937 §

Reassessing the X(2370) in J/ψ→γK_S⁰K_S⁰π⁰

Xiang-Kun Dong et al.

A coherent coupled-channel fit does not require an X(2370) in \(K_S^0K_S^0\pi^0\). The fit uses a 12-channel K-matrix with dispersive loops, applied to digitized BESIII data: - two-dimensional mass maps (6264 \(KK\) bins and 6006 \(K\pi\) bins); - the parent-mass spectrum.

Adding an eighth bare state near 2.37 GeV lowers the deviance by only 1.26% (7926.4→7826.6, with 16 extra parameters), and the projected line shapes are nearly identical. The extra pole sits at \(2424-i\,149\) MeV, twice as broad as the PDG reference \(2377-i\,74\). Its identification with X(2370) is unclear.

The physics consequence is for the flavor-singlet argument. If the true X(2370)→\(K\bar K\pi\) yield is smaller than the 1D fits imply, the denominator of BESIII’s \(\mathcal R<0.081\) shrinks. The small \(K^*\bar K\) yield would then no longer demonstrate selective suppression, which weakens the glueball and \(\eta_c\)-analogy readings without excluding them.

Caveats, stated by the authors. - Bin contents are reconstructed from a colour palette, with no inter-distribution correlations, efficiency, or resolution. - No significance or pole uncertainties are quoted. - Only mesonic channels are modelled, so the \(\Sigma\bar\Sigma\) interpretation is not tested. - \(K_0^*(1430)\) parameters were tuned within their uncertainties. - The \(J/\psi\to K^{*0}\bar K^{*0}\) background is not modelled.

Observation of s-channel single top-quark production at 13 TeV with ATLAS

ATLAS Collaboration

(Abstract only.) ATLAS reports the first single-experiment observation of s-channel single top, at 5.1σ observed (5.2σ expected). The dataset is 140 fb⁻¹, with events selected for exactly one lepton, missing \(E_T\), and exactly two b-jets. The comparable statistical and systematic errors suggest further gains will need systematic reductions as well as more data. arXiv:2610.09093 §

A gauge-invariant measure for lattice chiral fermions

Nathaniel Craig

Craig claims a nonperturbative, smooth, exponentially local, gauge-invariant overlap-fermion measure for \(SU(5)\) with \(\mathbf{10}\oplus\bar{\mathbf{5}}\). He extends it to all four global forms \([SU(3)\times SU(2)\times U(1)]/\Gamma_{\rm SM}\), \(\Gamma_{\rm SM}=1,\mathbb{Z}_2,\mathbb{Z}_3,\mathbb{Z}_6\), by embedding SM links into \(SU(5)\).

The key move replaces Lüscher’s abelian interpolation of the gauge potential to zero, which can exit the admissible domain for nonabelian fields. Instead:

  • A smooth connection is reconstructed from \(U\) and sampled at auxiliary spacings \(h_n=3^{-n}\), with fine plaquettes obeying \(\|1-(F_hU)(p)\|\le C\varepsilon h^2\).
  • Hermitian Wilson operators at successive spacings are interpolated linearly. The intermediate operators are not link fields, and a uniform gap \(g\) is proved throughout.
  • Each step contributes a curvature-strip term \(B_n\), and the measure term is \(\mathfrak{L}=\sum_n B_n\).
  • Return phases telescope. An exact colour-trace identity for \(\mathbf{10}\oplus\bar{\mathbf{5}}\) cancels the leading terms, which makes the sum converge.
  • Global integrability on noncontractible loops uses an auxiliary \(SU(3)\) connection under which the fermions pair conjugately.

The general sufficient conditions are cubic-anomaly cancellation plus even multiplicity of half-integer-isospin representations under an \(SU(2)\subset SU(N_c)\). The author says the latter is an artefact of the method.

Where “admissible” bites: - The construction requires a sufficiently small plaquette bound \(\varepsilon\). It is volume-independent but not quantified in the extracted text. - The measure is fixed only up to one constant phase per connected component of admissible fields. The current’s transformation laws do not guarantee that every choice of these relative phases respects discrete symmetries.

The convergence, locality and gap estimates sit in appendices not included in the extract. Lean verification is relegated to supplementary material. As the digest notes, this is an existence construction, not a simulation algorithm.

arXiv:2610.10219 §

Is the Light Neutralino Dark Matter Still Viable in the MSSM After the LZ-2024 Results?

Zhiyang Bao et al.

LZ-2024 excludes the sub-100 GeV Bino \(h\)-funnel at \(Z>5.9\) (\(p=1.8\times10^{-9}\)) and the \(Z\)-funnel at \(Z>4.1\) (\(p=2.1\times10^{-5}\)). The result holds for \(\Omega h^2=0.12\) and for \(0.06\) (half the dark matter), without imposing the LHC bound \(\mu\gtrsim760\) GeV.

The argument rests on one coupling (\(C_h\) or \(C_Z\)) setting both annihilation and scattering. Finite mediator width and thermal smearing cap \(\langle\sigma v\rangle\), so the relic density imposes a floor on \(\sigma^{\rm SI}\) or \(\sigma^{\rm SD}_n\).

The analysis is deliberately conservative: - Loop corrections: full one-loop vertex corrections (904 diagrams) give \(K^{\rm SI}\lesssim1.5\) and \(K^{\rm SD}_n\lesssim1.3\). Scale variation changes these corrections by at most 5%. - Hadronic inputs: FLAG-2024 values (\(\sigma_{\pi N}=42.2\pm2.4\) MeV) are used, with worst-case \(3\sigma\) rescalings of 0.78 (SI) and 0.62 (SD) applied at every point. The authors note that the Roy–Steiner value \(\sigma_{\pi N}\simeq58\)–59 MeV would only raise \(\sigma^{\rm SI}\).

The resulting cross-section floors sit nearly an order of magnitude above the LZ-2024 limits. LZ-2022 gave only \(Z<2\).

Caveats: - The LZ-2024 recast is micrOMEGAs’ background-free Poisson model. It is calibrated near 90% CL and then extrapolated far into the tail. - DDCalc implements only LZ-2022. There, micrOMEGAs underestimates \(Z\), which supports the claim that the micrOMEGAs numbers are conservative. - Near-degenerate Higgsino–Wino points (splitting \(<3\) GeV) were cut for counterterm instability.

What remains for a thermal Bino is coannihilation, heavy-Higgs resonance or well-tempering, which together point to \(|m_{\tilde\chi}|\gtrsim200\) GeV.

arXiv:2610.09753 §

Can Nuclear Recoils and Solar Neutrinos Resolve the Composition of Singlino-Higgsino Dark Matter?

Jingwei Lian et al.

Abstract only. The paper’s point is a degeneracy: swapping ground and excited states can leave an inelastic recoil spectrum unchanged while shifting ground-state annihilation by orders of magnitude. Solar-neutrino signals from captured dark matter then distinguish compositions that recoils cannot.

Applied in the NMSSM to fits of the high-energy LZ candidate, the analysis finds: - Disfavoured: Higgsino-pair inelastic fits overproduce solar signals. - Solar-compatible: singlino ground states, and Higgsino ground states with weak inelastic transitions. - Escape route: an elastic Higgsino alternative evades the solar bound by closing the inelastic transition that drives capture.

The abstract gives no quantitative limits. The authors acknowledge residual degeneracies tied to annihilation, halo and detector assumptions.

arXiv:2610.07106 §

Molecular Structure Effects in Low-Lying P-wave Bottom-Strange Meson Spectrum

Wei Hao et al.

A compact \(b\bar s\) picture cannot reach the \(B_{s0}^*(5700)\): the plain confinement+OGE model puts the \(0^+\) at 5851 MeV. One-loop (\(\alpha_s^2\)) corrections to the OGE potential lower it only to 5816 MeV, still ~120 MeV above LHCb’s \(5698.9\pm1.5\pm0.6\) MeV. Coupling the bare state to \(BK\)/\(B^*K\) via \(^3P_0\) vertices supplies the missing shift and brings the \(0^+\) to 5727 MeV, within ~30 MeV of data. Near-threshold phase-space suppression accounts for the narrow width. The same machinery puts the \(1^+\) partner at 5755 MeV (\(B^*K\)-dominated), close to Nowak–Zahed’s chiral-doubling \(5747\pm2\) MeV. The \(j_\ell=3/2\) states stay essentially compact. The authors give E1 widths of 45 keV for \(B_{s0}^*\to B_s^*\gamma\) and ~55–58 keV for the \(1^+\to B_s^{(*)}\gamma\) modes, which they propose as the Run 3 discovery channels.

Caveats: - Molecular content not quantified. The paper does not report a \(B_s^0\pi^0\) width. That is today’s discriminating number against Albaladejo et al. and Sánchez-Illana et al. It also gives no explicit compositeness, despite promising to quantify the admixture. - Radiative widths may be mislabeled. The quoted 58/55 keV values come from the Table 4 rows for \(B'_{s1}(1P)\). The \(B_{s1}(1P)\) rows, which correspond to the state assigned to 5747 in Table 3, give 50.9/34.6 keV. - Bare-state wavefunctions. All radiative widths use quark-model wavefunctions, not the dressed ones. - \(B_{s2}^*\) width is a fit, not a test. The \(^3P_0\) strength \(\gamma_0\) was fitted to the \(B_{s2}^*(5840)\) width, so reproducing its 1.5 MeV is not an independent check. - Residual offset. The \(0^+\) still misses by ~30 MeV.

arXiv:2610.07818 §

Fully charmed tetraquarks on the Lattice with controlled errors

Zhenyu Zhang et al.

The headline result is a \(J^P=0^+\) \(cc\bar c\bar c\) state at \(5934.8(13)\) MeV, \(33.2(13)\) MeV below \(\eta_c\eta_c\), and therefore strong-decay-stable. The authors discretize a standard OGE+linear potential model on a periodic lattice and diagonalize it with Lanczos (“LQPM”). This is a lattice-discretized quark model, not lattice QCD. Benchmarks are good: - \(\eta_c\), \(J/\psi\) and the 2S states land within a few MeV of experiment. - \(\Omega_{ccc}=4817.3(9)\) MeV agrees with NNQS and lies below the variational GEM value of 4823.

They also find a \(1^+\) only 10.9 MeV below \(\eta_cJ/\psi\), which they flag as potential-dependent. The \(2^+\) sits 24 MeV above \(J/\psi J/\psi\), and they connect it to the CMS near-threshold \(2^+\) structure. A bound \(0^+\) contradicts essentially every prior model, including DeepQuark. The authors attribute their ~500 MeV lower masses to enforcing full antisymmetry with non-symmetric spatial parts. Restricting to symmetric spatial functions recovers NNQS (~6414–6508 MeV).

The “controlled errors” claim deserves scrutiny: - Coarse spacing. The tetraquark is computed at \(a\approx0.2\) fm, a spacing at which the supplement says charmonium has not converged. Finer spacings “do not converge.” - Threshold mismatch. The quoted 5968 MeV threshold equals twice the fine-lattice \(\eta_c\) mass. The text does not show it evaluated at \(a\approx0.2\) fm. - Small boxes, poor fits. Only five boxes are used, up to 1.8 fm. The extrapolation adds 16 MeV beyond the largest box, and the fits have \(\chi^2/\text{dof}=15\)–28. The ±1.3 MeV error is fit-only. - Scattering-like color structure. The color ratio converges to the 2:1 value characteristic of meson–meson scattering states.

arXiv:2610.08282 §

Relations between electroweak parameters to order α_s³

Andreas Maier et al.

Maier and Marquard complete the top-mediated four-loop QCD corrections to \(\Delta\bar r\) and \(\Delta\kappa\) beyond the leading \(\Delta\rho\) term. They supply the missing pieces: - the four-loop flavour-singlet polarization functions; - top-mass corrections to the \(bb\) correlator; - a sixth low-energy term in the axial correlator.

The expansion in \(M_Z^2/m_t^2\) is taken to \(M_Z^{10}/m_t^{10}\), with negligible truncation error. The new \(\alpha_s^3 m_t^{\le0}\) terms shift \(M_W\) by ≈ −0.8 MeV with an on-shell top mass and ≈ −0.2 MeV in \(\overline{\rm MS}\). For comparison, FCC-ee projects 0.16 MeV systematic and 0.18 MeV statistical uncertainty, so these terms are relevant at that precision.

Two points matter for projections: - Scheme dependence. In \(\overline{\rm MS}\) the subleading \(\alpha_s^3\bar m_t^0\) term (−0.22 MeV) exceeds the formally leading \(\alpha_s^3\bar m_t^2\) term (−0.03 MeV). The factor-of-four scheme spread at this order is itself a theory uncertainty at the FCC-ee scale. - Five loops needed. Extrapolating the on-shell pattern, the authors expect \(\alpha_s^4 M_t^2\) to contribute several hundred keV. That makes five-loop \(\rho\) desirable.

Scope is pure QCD on top/bottom loops, and the numerical study neglects the \(\Delta r\)–\(\Delta\bar r\) difference. Results are supplied in electronic form.

arXiv:2610.04048 §

Stars Would Remember a Recent Gravitational Transition: Testing G-step Resolutions of the Hubble Tension with Red Dwarfs

Jeremy Sakstein et al.

Both Hubble-motivated \(G\)-step benchmarks are disfavoured: \(\Delta G/G_0=+0.04\) at 73 Myr (EGSM) at 3.1σ, and \(+0.11\) at 130 Myr (OGSM) at 4.0σ. The profile likelihood has its minimum at \(\Delta G=0\) for both transition times.

The mechanism is a radius memory in fully convective red dwarfs. Homology gives an equilibrium radius \(R\propto G^{0.324}\), while the fast adiabatic response gives \(R\propto G^{-1}\). The perturbation then relaxes on \(t_{\rm relax}\simeq0.05\,t_{\rm KH}\sim10^8\) yr, which is comparable to the proposed look-back times. A drop from larger \(G\) therefore leaves today’s stars inflated.

The analysis uses: - MESA tracks with an implemented \(G\)-step; - a per-star starspot covering fraction treated as a nuisance parameter; - eight CHEOPS detached-eclipsing-binary M dwarfs (0.1–0.31 \(M_\odot\), periods ≥5 d) with mass–radius correlations \(\rho=0.53\)–0.90.

This gives \(\Delta\chi^2=9.52\) (EGSM) and 16.41 (OGSM). Because the spot fractions sit at their physical boundary, the significances come from a parametric bootstrap rather than Wilks’ theorem.

The test is independent of the disputed solar and terrestrial modelling arguments.

Caveats: - Boundary stars drive the result. All of the leverage comes from the four stars pinned at \(f_{\rm spot}=0\), where spots can no longer absorb the extra inflation. - One star dominates. Dropping J2343+29 cuts the significances to 2.45σ (EGSM) and 1.90σ (OGSM). - Model systematics matter. A coherent 1% downward shift of the model radii lowers \(\Delta\chi^2\) to 5.72 and 10.92. - Only positive steps are tested. Both benchmarks have \(\Delta G>0\).

arXiv:2610.04557 §

Violation and Restoration of Probability Positivity in Perturbative QFT

Bin Gong et al.

The central argument concerns exclusive processes with colourless and neutral external states. There, each renormalized loop amplitude is IR-finite on its own and no KLN sum over unresolved states is needed. Gong et al. conclude that truncating the squared amplitude is an inherited QED habit, and that \(|\sum_{l\le M}\mathcal A^{(l)}|^2\) is the right observable.

They recast eleven published NNLO results in the form \(\Gamma=\Gamma_{\rm LO}|1+a_s(c_1+ic_2)+a_s^2(c_3+ic_4)|^2\). In every case both complex coefficients have modulus below 1, with the largest, 0.67, for \(J/\psi+\eta_c\). Selected ratios \(\Gamma/\Gamma_{\rm LO}\) at NLO, NNLO:

Channel Amplitude-level Squared-level
\(J/\psi\to3\gamma\) 0.276, 0.126 −0.02, −0.44
\(\Upsilon\to3\gamma\) 0.426, 0.190 0.28, −0.11
\(e^+e^-\to J/\psi J/\psi\) 0.40, 0.058 0.26, −0.38

\(J/\psi\to3\gamma\) goes negative at NLO in the traditional expansion. \(\Upsilon\to3\gamma\) and double-\(J/\psi\) go negative at NNLO. Zhan et al. apply the same prescription to ultraperipheral \(J/\psi+\gamma\).

Caveats, visible in the paper itself: - Partial higher orders. The NLO square keeps \(|\mathcal M_1|^2\) but not the same-order \(2{\rm Re}(\mathcal M_0^*\mathcal M_2)\), so it contains incomplete \(\mathcal O(\alpha_s^2)\) terms. - Convergence is judged inside the modulus. The rates themselves still fall sharply from NLO to NNLO: by a factor 2.2 for \(J/\psi\to3\gamma\) and about 7 for double \(J/\psi\). - Positivity is not accuracy. Positivity is guaranteed by construction, which does not show the result is closer to the true value. - Mostly a reframing. The numbers re-express the group’s own earlier calculations, with \(\alpha_s\) re-chosen for the B-factory channels.

arXiv:2610.04309 §

Angular Observables in the Λ_b→Λνν̅ Decay

Wolfgang Altmannshofer et al.

The full cascade \(\Lambda_b\to\Lambda(\to p\pi^-)\nu\bar\nu\) is described by five angular coefficients, \(S_{1\text{–}4}\) and \(A_5\); all but \(S_1\) need \(\Lambda_b\) polarization. Z-pole \(\Lambda_b\) supply it: LEP measured \(\mathcal P=-0.40\pm0.14\).

The coefficients split by what they probe: - Chirality: \(S_1\) and \(S_2\) go as \(|C_R|^2-|C_L|^2\), which the mesonic modes cannot see. - CP phase: \(A_5\) is a T-odd CP asymmetry proportional to \({\rm Im}(C_LC_R^*)\), with no flavour tagging or time dependence needed.

Mesonic data fix only \(|C_L\pm C_R|\), so they confine the couplings to a ring via \((|C_R|^2-|C_L|^2)^2+4({\rm Im}\,C_LC_R^*)^2=|C_R+C_L|^2|C_R-C_L|^2\). The baryonic observables pick the point on that ring.

SM predictions: - branching ratio \((7.59\pm1.03)\times10^{-6}\), dominated by form-factor and CKM uncertainties; - \(\langle S_1\rangle=-0.926\pm0.018\); - an \(S_2\) zero crossing at \(12.6\pm1.2\) GeV² that is independent of the Wilson coefficients, and so tests the form factors.

Projected FCC-ee reach, assuming a 10% branching-ratio measurement: - Real couplings: \(S_1\) and \(S_2\) break the four-fold real-coupling degeneracy even at ±0.5 uncertainty on the angular observables. - Complex couplings: at ±0.1 all four benchmarks are distinguishable from the SM. At ±0.5 the \(A_5\) sensitivity is largely lost and only the pure right-handed benchmark survives.

This bears directly on the Belle II \(K^+\nu\bar\nu\) excess, which the authors put about 2.7σ above the SM.

Caveats: - No dedicated angular estimates. The ±0.1–0.5 uncertainties are assumed scans. - Simplified meson comparison. The mesonic side is a 5% toy estimate. - Only right-handed currents show up. A pure rescaling of \(C_L\) leaves every angular observable unchanged. - Dark-sector explanations not covered. Light invisible states are explicitly excluded, although several Belle II interpretations rely on them.

arXiv:2610.03687 §

Third-Order Logarithmic Resummation for Jet-Vetoed Higgs Production

Samuel Abreu et al.

The first N³LL+N³LO prediction for the gluon-fusion zero-jet cross section gives 30.11 (+3.2%/−3.0%) pb at \(p_T^{\rm v}=30\) GeV, with \(R=0.4\) anti-\(k_t\) jets at 13.6 TeV. That is ~3% above fixed-order N³LO (29.24 pb). The shift lies outside N³LO’s narrow +0.7/−2.2% scale band, so fixed-order scale variation alone misses the logarithmic correction. Relative to NNLL+NNLO, the central value rises ~4% and the uncertainty shrinks from 6–8% to ~3%. The new ingredient is the three-loop, algorithm-dependent rapidity anomalous dimension \(\gamma_\nu^{(3)}(R^2)\) from the companion paper, combined with the four-loop cusp, three-loop \(\gamma_V\) and two-loop boundary terms, all implemented in RadISH. The authors tested the expansion against NNLOJet \(H\)+jet with \(M=1\) TeV and \(R=0.1\). With \(\Delta\gamma_\nu^{(3)}\) set to zero, the expansion visibly fails to converge, so the three-loop clustering term is numerically essential.

The physics point is that the previous NNLL+N³LO result had an accidental cancellation near 30 GeV. N³LL lifts it and restores a uniform 3–4% third-order correction for \(p_T^{\rm v}\gtrsim20\) GeV. Alternative matching schemes and log-switching prescriptions all agree within the band. The code is public (JetVHeto).

Caveats: - Missing effects: top and bottom mass effects and small-\(R\) resummation are not included. Both were 1–2% in earlier work, comparable to the quoted uncertainty. - ln R terms: the authors flag large subleading \(\ln R\) terms in \(\gamma_\nu^{(3)}\) as motivation for \(\ln R\) resummation. - Factorization breaking: coherence-violating effects, which break factorization from five loops, are explicitly set aside. The non-global/super-leading front noted in the digest therefore sits outside this calculation.

arXiv:2610.03143 §

First observation of electron antineutrinos from nuclear reactors at Super-Kamiokande (K. Abe (The Super-Kamiokande Collaboration) et al.)

SK-Gd sees reactor \(\bar\nu_e\) via IBD with Gd-tagged neutrons. The exposure is 411.52 live days: SK-VI at 0.011% Gd and SK-VII at 0.033% Gd. The no-reactor hypothesis gives \(\Delta\chi^2=34.7\), about 6σ, and the oscillated fit has \(\chi^2=16.7/16\).

Reactor-only fit, with \(\theta_{13}\) fixed: - \(\sin^2\theta_{12}=0.500\pm0.155\) - \(\Delta m^2_{21}=(9.08^{+0.55}_{-0.54})\times10^{-5}\) eV²

Comparison with SK solar. SK’s own solar fit gives \(\Delta m^2_{21}=(6.10^{+1.26}_{-0.86})\times10^{-5}\) eV². The differences are 1.1σ in \(\theta_{12}\) and 2.2σ in \(\Delta m^2_{21}\). A parameter-goodness-of-fit test gives 1.9σ compatibility. The main text quotes \(\Delta\chi^2_{\rm PG}=7.25\) (p=0.027) while the appendix quotes 7.64 (p=0.022), a minor internal inconsistency. The combined SK fit is \(\sin^2\theta_{12}=0.332\), \(\Delta m^2_{21}=(8.94^{+0.46}_{-0.47})\times10^{-5}\) eV².

Why it matters: this is a new water-Cherenkov reactor handle on the solar sector, from the same detector that measures solar neutrinos. That makes it an internal CPT-motivated cross-check, and a demonstration of Gd-water for low-energy \(\bar\nu_e\).

The paper does not quantify the digest’s “~3σ above JUNO” framing. It calls its values consistent with global fits and recent reactor results, JUNO included.

Caveats: - Background model: the accidental background comes from a single low-reactor window (Nov–Dec 2020) and is statistics-limited. - Flux inputs: fluxes use electrical rather than thermal load factors. - Systematics: neutron-capture γ modeling (5.4%) and coincidence efficiency (5.5%) dominate the listed systematics.

arXiv:2610.03692 §

Higgsino dark matter compatible with the LZ high-energy nuclear-recoil event and IceCube constraints

Katherine Freese et al.

Heavy Higgsinos can explain the 248 keV LZ event without violating IceCube solar bounds, but the thermal 1.1 TeV case barely survives. The authors build a 96-bin approximate LZ likelihood digitized from LZ’s published data, calibrated with 30k toys, which reproduces LZ’s interval near δ=350 keV. They include the corrected (4× larger) inelastic cross section.

Thermal 1.1 TeV case. The LZ-compatible splitting depends on the halo model: - SHM: 360–370 keV. - SHM+LMC: 485–505 keV.

IceCube lower bounds on δ at 1.1 TeV depend on how captured Higgsinos cool in the Sun:

Cooling assumption Lower bound on δ
Capture–annihilation equilibrium imposed 566 keV
Inelastic + elastic SD 558 keV
Inelastic only 506 keV

The thermal Higgsino survives only at δ≈508 keV, and only under three conditions together: LMC tail included, all elastic cooling neglected, and the LZ sideband dropped. The authors call this a limiting case rather than a robust interpretation.

Heavier Higgsinos. The key novelty is the \(m_\chi^{-2}\) falloff of the elastic SD cross section, which weakens cooling at high mass. With it, compatible regions open at: - ~2 TeV (δ~520 keV) for SHM+LMC, no sideband and SD-only elastic cooling. - ≳10–30 TeV (δ≈350–400 keV) for the SHM. - Equilibrium imposed everywhere: ≳80 TeV for the SHM, and 13–120 TeV (465–520 keV) for SHM+LMC.

They also find Sommerfeld resonances near 6.5, 25 and 55 TeV that locally tighten the bounds.

Caveats: - Halo model: the LMC tail uses a single simulated halo with a large LMC/MW mass ratio (0.27). - Sideband acceptance: unpublished, and LZ has not calibrated its nuclear-recoil response there. - Solar treatment: cooling assumes stationary nuclei, radial orbits and a uniform-density population. - IceCube conversion: the \(W^+W^-\) limits are frozen above 10 TeV, so high-mass regions are extrapolations. - Cosmology: heavier Higgsinos need a non-standard abundance history.

arXiv:2610.00472 §

Fractional anomalous determinants and the chiral phase transition

Robert D. Pisarski

Pisarski proposes a “syncretic” chiral Lagrangian whose anomalous sector changes across \(T_\chi\). Below \(T_\chi\), topological charge is taken to be fractional in units of \(1/N_c\), so terms \(\mathcal F_j(\Phi^\dagger\Phi)\,[(\det\Phi)^{j/N_c}+\text{h.c.}]\) appear. The prefactors are fractional powers of \(\det\Phi^\dagger\Phi\), chosen so that each term behaves like \(\Phi^2\) as \(\Phi\to0\). Above \(T_\chi\), only integer powers survive. The argument rests on assuming that in the Veneziano limit the free energy depends on \(\theta/N_c\). This yields a Witten–Veneziano generalization to arbitrary \(N_f/N_c\) in which the \(\eta'\) stays massive at \(T_\chi\) without a large \(\xi_1\det\Phi\). The predicted transition order by flavor number: - \(N_f=1\): crossover (\(Z(2)\)-like). - \(N_f=2\): second order, \(O(4)\). - \(N_f=3\): still first order because the cubic \(\det\Phi\) remains, but possibly very weakly so, since \(\xi_1\) can be small. - \(N_f=4\): a \(\mathcal Q=2/3\) term could induce a weak, Halperin–Lubensky–Ma-type first-order transition.

The model is motivated by lattice data that find no first-order signal for \(N_f=3\). One example is Zhang et al.’s susceptibility scaling exponent \(\alpha\approx0.39\pm0.04\), which fits neither first order (\(\alpha=3\)) nor \(Z(2)\) (\(\alpha\approx1.96\)). Another is the measured \(b_2\approx-0.19/N_c^2\), read here as a dense liquid of fractional instantons. The proposed test is to fit the 2+1-flavor ratio \(\mathcal R_{\pi,a_0}(T)\) to \(\sigma_s^{4/3},\sigma_s^{2/3},\sigma_s\) between 150 and 300 MeV.

Caveats are substantial, and the author flags them himself: - The \(\mathcal F_j\) are unknown, and the ansatz is called “rank supposition.” FRG work already indicates additional invariants are needed. - The non-degenerate case relevant to the proposed lattice test is deferred to a separate paper. - QCD is “blithely” assumed close to the Veneziano limit.

arXiv:2610.02169 §

Drell–Yan lepton pair production from low to high transverse momentum

Beno\^it Assi et al.

The prediction is N³LO matched to approximate N⁴LL′ \(q_T\) resummation, with unknown four-loop boundary terms estimated in large-\(\beta_0\). Its main novelty is the nonperturbative input. The Collins–Soper kernel is taken from continuum-limit lattice QCD over \(0.15\lesssim b_T\lesssim0.8\) fm, giving \(c_0=0.032\pm0.012\) GeV² at \(B_{NP}=2\) GeV⁻¹. The only parameter fitted to Drell–Yan data is a Gaussian width, \(\kappa_{NP}=3.0\pm0.5\) GeV⁻².

Comparison with ATLAS 13 TeV data: - Agreement: deviations are at most 1.3% below 10 GeV, 2.0% for 10–30 GeV, and 3.5% above 30 GeV, with χ²/bin of 0.2, 1.0 and 1.6 in those regions. - Lattice uncertainty: the \(c_0\) uncertainty contributes up to 2.6% in the first bin and at most 0.8% elsewhere.

The calculation is transferred to event samples by maximum-entropy reweighting. Of 170 tabulated moments in \(r_T\) and \(\phi_{\rm acop}\), 14–19 are selected, giving event-local multipliers \(\propto\exp(\sum\lambda_j g_j)\) that are strictly positive. - Sherpa: the reweighted sample matches the calculation to about 2% in a typical bin, against a prior that was off by up to 40%. The effective sample size is essentially unchanged (7.6% → 7.3%). - POWHEG: agreement is about 3% in a typical bin, with effective sample size held at 99%. The largest deviation from data below 200 GeV falls from 19% to 6%.

Caveats: - The prediction is about 8× less precise than the data in a typical bin, and PDF uncertainties (a few percent) are assessed elsewhere. - The moment covariance is treated as diagonal. - Positivity applies to the multiplier; the Sherpa prior itself retains signed weights. - Between 200 and 900 GeV the ungated POWHEG weight overshoots the data by 4–40%. - Extension to \(W\) production is proposed but not done, which matters for any \(m_W\) application.

arXiv:2610.01825 §

Near Threshold Kaluza-Klein Graviton Decays and the Dark Dimension

Kevin Langhoff

On shell, the massive cubic KK-graviton vertex reduces to the double copy of the Yang–Mills vertex evaluated at massive momenta. The mass term cancels against the \(\Box\) terms, and the cancellation follows from the field redefinition \(h=e^H-1\). As a result the amplitude vanishes at threshold, and the decay is pure \(d\)-wave with \(\Gamma\propto p^5\propto Q^{5/2}\). Relative to the \(s\)-wave rate assumed in the dark-dimension cascade literature, the suppression is \(\sim(Q/\mu)^2\sim10^{-24}\) for \(Q\sim1\) meV and GeV parents.

Consequences for the flat dark dimension: - Cascade: summing over a flat tower with the completeness bound on \(g_{mn\ell}\) saturated, the mass-distribution peak moves by less than 0.1% by matter–radiation equality (\(\delta n=100\), \(R=0.05\)–\(0.2\,\mu\)m). - Dark matter: GeV-scale gravitons then live only \(\sim10^5\) yr, which excludes KK gravitons as the dark matter. - Irreducible background: freeze-in still produces \(\rho_h/\rho_{\rm DM}=1.3\times10^{-6}(T_{\rm RH}/5\,{\rm MeV})^3(m_{\rm KK}/5\,{\rm meV})^{-1}\). COMPTEL and EGRET MeV gamma-ray data then cap \(T_{\rm RH}\). - Combined bound: with \(T_{\rm RH}\ge5.96\) MeV from BBN+CMB+BAO, a viable window survives only for \(R\lesssim0.2\,\mu\)m (\(m_{\rm KK}\gtrsim1\) eV). That is roughly three orders of magnitude below the \(R\sim\Lambda^{-1/4}\) expectation.

Caveats: the analysis covers flat or weakly warped geometries only, and the cascade estimate uses a saturated coupling bound. The threshold suppression was found independently by Lee, Randall and Riojas (the Wednesday paper). A factor-10 improvement in MeV telescopes such as AMEGO-X or e-ASTROGAM would tighten the bound further.

Thursday, October 1, 2026

arXiv:2609.40229 §

𝒪(αα_s²⁾ corrections to the Top Quark mass

Daniele Gaggero et al.

The first three-loop mixed QCD–EW term in the top pole-to-\(\overline{\rm MS}\) relation shifts \(m_t(M_t)\) by −216 MeV at \(M_t=172.3\) GeV. The pure \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s^3)\) term shifts it by −554 MeV, so the mixed term is about 0.5% of the “NLO” result, comparable to the QCD piece. The total NLO→NNLO shift is −770 MeV, leaving \(M_t-m_t^{(n=2)}(M_t)=106\) MeV. Across the full \(M_t\) range the shift is a steady ~200 MeV, against the ATLAS+CMS uncertainty of ±0.33 GeV. The calculation uses the Jegerlehner–Kalmykov scheme with tadpoles kept, so the \(\overline{\rm MS}\) mass is gauge invariant. Master integrals are built in \(\epsilon\)-factorized form. The \(W\) topologies contain the two-loop-sunrise elliptic period, and the \(Z/H\) topologies contain the three-loop equal-mass banana. Several required forms do not survive into the physical result: the weight-four modular form and \(\mathcal{F}_1\). \(\gamma_5\) traces in the \(\chi\)-Goldstone diagrams are handled with local counterterms. The authors check the RGE-predicted poles, the Yukawa consistency of the single pole, and the NNLO QCD top width extracted from the imaginary part.

Why it matters: the term is the same size as the experimental error. In the effective series that groups the one-loop QCD and EW pieces together, the scale band shrinks from \(175.2^{+9.2}_{-7.9}\) GeV to \(172.1^{+2.4}_{-5.0}\) GeV.

Caveats: the residual band is still several GeV wide and asymmetric. The authors also warn that the known, sizable \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha^2)\) corrections are left out of their mass-scan plot.

arXiv:2609.40205 §

Unpolarized quasi- and pseudo-distributions at one loop: gluon correlator decomposition, matching, and the region |x|>1

Christopher Monahan et al.

The Balitsky–Morris–Radyushkin gluon-in-gluon kernel misses a finite polynomial. The polynomial is the same in all three of their index combinations. The authors trace it to one appendix step, where a loop-generated index between the two field strengths is summed over 2 transverse directions instead of \(d-2\). That drops an evanescent operator, which produces exactly the missing polynomial when it multiplies the collinear pole. Their \(\overline{\rm MS}\) result agrees with Yao–Ji–Zhang, and the same polynomial separates Yao–Ji–Zhang from BMR. In ratio-scheme pseudo-PDF extractions, \(\langle x\rangle_g\) is protected. The normalized second and third moments, however, come out roughly 11% and 13% too low at \(\alpha_s=0.3\), so the true \(\overline{\rm MS}\) gluon is harder than reported.

The framework splits the correlator into six covariant form factors with a \(6\times6\) mixing matrix. That matrix has three eigenvalue sectors (\(Z_{\perp\perp}^2\), \(Z_{\perp\perp}Z_{\parallel\perp}\), \(Z_{\parallel\perp}^2\)), so any lattice index choice follows without new loops. The result is cross-checked in \(R_\xi\) and axial gauge.

A second conceptual result uses Paley–Wiener. The \(|x|>1\) exterior comes entirely from the branch point of \(\log(\tilde z^2\mu^2)\) at \(\nu=0\), and at leading power it is fixed pointwise by the renormalization group. At one loop it is \(n_f\)-independent, with tail coefficients \(5C_A/6\) for \(\mathcal{M}_{pp}\) and \(11C_A/6\) for \(\mathcal{G}^{ziiz}\). Fitting the exterior with independent parameters therefore risks absorbing power corrections into the leading-twist PDF.

Caveats: the analysis is one-loop only, the moment shifts are an estimate at fixed \(\alpha_s\), and gluon target-mass corrections are not known.

arXiv:2609.38316 §

Constraints on Complex Electroweak WIMPs from Solar Capture

Juan P. Luengas et al.

Endothermic capture depends only on hypercharge: the \(Y=1\) rate is 4× the \(Y=1/2\) rate. For \(n\ge4\), the loop-induced SI elastic scattering (\(\propto n^4\)) and the Sommerfeld-enhanced annihilation keep the Sun in equilibrium. The IceCube bound is therefore a single universal curve per \(Y\) for fermions and scalars alike.

The excluded splitting is largest at TeV masses, ~600 keV for \(Y=1\) and ~570 keV for \(Y=1/2\), and it falls to 350–400 keV by 100 TeV. Under the SHM this excludes the \(3_1\), \(5_1\), \(4_{1/2}\) and \(6_{1/2}\) as sources of the LZ 248 keV event. The \(8_{1/2}\) is marginal. Only the \(10_{1/2}\) (158 TeV) and \(12_{1/2}\) (253 TeV) survive, because they sit in the heavy-mass LZ island.

The solar curve barely depends on the halo, since capture is dominated by slow particles. The LMC tail instead moves the LZ-preferred \(\delta\) above 500 keV. Under that tail, \(6_{1/2}\) and above become allowed, \(4_{1/2}\) is marginal, and \(3_1\), \(5_1\) escape IceCube but match LZ only at ~3σ.

For the doublets: - The fermion doublet reproduces the earlier Higgsino bound, \(\delta\lesssim566\) keV. - The scalar doublet has no spin-dependent sinking channel and stalls at 0.1–0.2 \(R_\odot\). Its bound peaks near 510 keV and is gone by 10 TeV.

Why it matters: it makes the velocity-tail question decisive for any heavy-multiplet LZ fit.

Caveats: - The fit rests on a single event, and its two mass islands are separated by only 1–2σ. - Sommerfeld factors are parametric, good to ~30% off resonance. - The analysis restricts to \(Y\le1\) and assumes no tree-level Higgs portal for scalars. A portal with \(\lambda_{\chi H}\gtrsim0.01\) would strengthen the scalar-doublet bound.

arXiv:2609.37895 §

Measurement of Neutrino Oscillation Parameters at JUNO and Indication of the Neutrino Mass Ordering

The JUNO Collaboration

JUNO used 207.2 live days, 8,294 IBD candidates, and a 52.5 km mean baseline. It measures \(\Delta m^2_{31} = +2.509^{+0.027}_{-0.025}\times10^{-3}\) eV² under NO and \(-2.482^{+0.025}_{-0.026}\times10^{-3}\) eV² under IO, a precision of about 1% with Feldman–Cousins intervals. It also reports \(\sin^2\theta_{12} = 0.3036\pm0.0064\) (2.1%) and \(\Delta m^2_{21} = (7.388\pm0.078)\times10^{-5}\) eV² (1.1%).

This is the first analysis to fold in TAO near-detector data (90,280 IBDs) alongside Daya Bay for the unoscillated spectrum. Three independent groups ran blinded analyses, and their results were consistent.

The mass-ordering statement is a reactor–accelerator tension test. NuFit long-baseline \(\Delta m^2_{31}\) pulls under each hypothesis give \(\Delta\chi^2_{\rm IO-NO} = 4.86\). Pseudo-experiments that scan \(\delta_{\rm CP}\) turn this into \(p = 0.0197\), or 2.1σ one-sided (2.3σ two-sided). An independent Bayesian MCMC gives \(\kappa = 10.18\).

The caveats are structural: - JUNO alone cannot pick the minimum. Its \(\Delta\chi^2\) profile has several degenerate local minima in \(\Delta m^2_{31}\). The Daya Bay constraint is what selects the physical one; JUNO then sets the precision. - The NMO evidence is not JUNO-internal. It rests on the mismatch with long-baseline experiments. - Systematics are starting to matter. Statistics still dominate, but Bi–Po backgrounds and detection efficiency are now significant contributors. - Energy resolution is slightly worse than simulated. It is about 3.5% at the ⁶⁸Ge peak, a bit above the MC prediction.

A by-product is the world’s largest geoneutrino sample, at \(73\pm11\) TNU.

arXiv:2609.38022 §

The new LHCb beauty-strange state as the bottom partner of the D_s0^*(2317)

Miguel Albaladejo et al.

LHCb’s narrow \(\bar B_s^0\pi^0\) peak sits at \(5698.9\pm1.5\pm0.6\) MeV, 74 MeV below the \(B^-K^+\) threshold, with 343 signal candidates. Two unitarized coupled-channel models were tested against it: - Model I: leading-order Weinberg–Tomozawa plus a bare \(b\bar s\) state, fitted to \(\bar B^{(*)}K\) lattice levels. - Model II: NLO HMChPT, with its low-energy constants fixed by charm-sector lattice scattering lengths and carried over by heavy-quark flavor symmetry.

Isospin breaking enters through physical masses, \(\pi^0\)–\(\eta\) mixing and \(m_u\neq m_d\), and adds no free parameters.

Predictions with no LHCb input land 1.1σ (Model II) and 1.3–1.7σ (Model I) from the observed mass. Freeing one parameter (the cutoff Λ or the subtraction constant \(a_b\)) reproduces the zero-width reference fit, with χ²/dof = 1.22 for 84 degrees of freedom. The required shifts are about 1.1–1.3σ, and joint fits with the lattice data cost only Δχ²_LQCD ≈ 1.1–2.5.

Properties of the state in the fitted models:

Quantity Model I Model II
\(\bar BK\) compositeness ~45–50% ~60%
Isospin-violating width 34 keV 82.5 keV
\(1^+\) partner mass 5748.8 MeV 5751.7 MeV

The factor of two between the widths is a rough measure of model dependence, and radiative decays are not included.

The key point is the \(1^+\)–\(0^+\) splitting of 49–52 MeV, which is essentially the \(B_s^*\)–\(B_s\) mass difference (49.4 MeV). The observed peak therefore cannot discriminate between the two assignments and may contain both states. \(\bar B_s^0\gamma\) searches are the direct test.

The authors are careful on several points: - A good fit does not prove a molecular state. - The lattice inputs are at unphysical quark masses, and the predictions carry no systematic uncertainties. - The same NLO amplitudes are in tension with ALICE \(D\pi\) femtoscopy in the charm sector. - The resolution kernel was extracted from LHCb’s published figure.

arXiv:2609.37563 §

The four-loop quark-to-quark splitting function in QCD

Thomas Gehrmann et al.

This paper gives the first fully analytic, all-\(x\) four-loop pure-singlet \(P_{\rm ps}^{(3)}\). Combined with the non-singlet result (cited here as the same authors’ arXiv:2604.09534), it completes \(P_{qq}\) at N³LO.

The method follows these steps: 1. Compute off-shell quark operator matrix elements, including mixing with gluon and gauge-variant operators. 2. Reduce integrals by IBP over finite fields and solve differential equations in an auxiliary parameter \(t\) to reach high Mellin moments. 3. Fit an ansatz of harmonic sums up to weight six to those moments. 4. Invert the Mellin transform to harmonic polylogarithms up to weight six.

The result reproduces the known fixed moments through \(n=22\). It also reproduces the \(\zeta_3\) contribution at \(n=2\) that arises from a spurious \((n-2)\) pole.

New content includes the previously unknown leading-power small-\(x\) coefficients of \(\ln x/x\) and \(1/x\). There is also a discrepancy with the double-log resummation of Davies et al.: the \(\zeta_2\ln^4 x\) coefficient lacks a \((d_F^{abcd})^2/N_c\) term there. The two agree at leading colour, matching the pattern seen in the non-singlet case, and the origin is unexplained.

The practical impact on evolution is modest. The existing moment-based approximation agrees with the exact result within its uncertainties over \(10^{-5}<x<1\). The main gain is that the approximation uncertainty for this entry of the evolution matrix is gone, while the gluon entries remain approximate.

The ancillary files include a numerical fit accurate to \(10^{-5}\) absolute and \(10^{-8}\) relative. The exact \(\gamma_{qq}^{(3)}\) may also help reconstruct \(\gamma_{gg}^{(3)}\) through reciprocity.
