arXiv:2609.31828 §

Impact of Helicity–Isospin Convention Matching on the Extraction of α from B⁰→(ρπ)⁰

Radek \v{Z}leb\v{c}\'{i}k

Belle and BaBar both define the ρ→ππ helicity angles in the cyclic convention, in which the ρ⁰ angle is referenced to the π⁻. They then extract α with the Lipkin–Nir–Quinn–Snyder relation \(A^++A^-+2A^0\). That relation holds only if the ρ⁰ angle is referenced to the π⁺. The mismatch amounts to a sign flip of \(A^0\) and \(\bar A^0\). Žlebčík corrects it by flipping the 12 of the 26 \(U/I\) parameters that are linear in \(A^0\) or \(\bar A^0\), along with the corresponding covariance off-diagonals. He first reproduces the public HFLAV ρπ and combined curves exactly, then reruns the scan.

The corrected ρπ curve is much flatter near 90°. Its tension with the indirect value (91.2°) drops from 2.9σ to 2.1σ. The combined result becomes \(\alpha=(87.0^{+11.8}_{-4.7})^\circ\), compared with HFLAV’s \((84.1^{+3.7}_{-3.0})^\circ\). The ππ+ρρ combination alone gives \((85.9^{+8.6}_{-3.9})^\circ\).

The paper says every phenomenological treatment it examined carries the mismatch, including the Belle and BaBar papers, HFLAV/CKMfitter and UTfit. It traces the root cause to Snyder–Quinn never specifying the reference pion.

Caveats: - The apparent precision was partly an artefact. The combined upper error roughly triples, and ρπ no longer improves precision; only the 2σ interval tightens slightly, to [79.1°, 108.1°]. - The ρπ best-fit χ² rises from 8.4 to 9.5 (17 dof), although the combined χ² improves by 1.4. - A mirror solution near 0° persists.

For Belle II and LHCb, the practical lesson is that the Dalitz convention must be fixed before the isospin fit.

arXiv:2609.35650 §

The LZ event with a two-state dark matter halo

Vinicius Oliveira

Oliveira rebuilds LZ’s inference from public material. He uses a binned likelihood from the Fig. 5 projection (3 S1c slices × 32 bins), the shell-model nuclear response, and a toy-MC Neyman construction. The Helm form factor would overestimate the rate at 248 keV by a factor of 5, which is why the shell-model response matters. The recast matches LZ’s endothermic upper limits within 26% at all 17 points with δ ≥ 50 keV, and within 5% at about half of them. Its local significances run 0.2–0.7σ low: Z = 2.5 versus 3.0 at 1 TeV and δ = 300 keV.

The recast then allows a surviving excited-state fraction \(f\). Freeze-out or freeze-in naturally gives \(f\approx1/2\) unless \(\chi_2\) decays. Exothermic scattering dominates above a critical \(f\) of \(6\times10^{-3}\), \(1.4\times10^{-3}\) and \(3\times10^{-5}\) at δ = 250, 300 and 350 keV. Above that point the required cross section scales as \(1/f\).

At \(f=1/2\) and 1 TeV: - The best-fit σ_N drops by 80, 360 and \(1.6\times10^4\) at those three splittings. - σ_N stays within \(3.5\times10^{-45}\)–\(3.6\times10^{-44}\) cm² over 250–500 keV. - The endothermic cutoff disappears. - Di Mauro’s thermal benchmark (297 keV, \(6.5\times10^{-43}\) cm²) sits 30× above the upper limit.

Caveats: - The exothermic intervals cannot be validated against any LZ result. - The likelihood is binned and the background is fixed. - Only the Standard Halo Model is used, which connects to Das et al. on halo dependence. - The paper does not impose the \(\chi_2\) lifetime bound. Radiative decay could drive \(f\to0\), which is exactly what Dutra et al. propose to test with a line in COSI. - The paper also flags unexplained internal inconsistencies in where LZ’s published lower limits turn on.

arXiv:2609.34984 §

A First Glimpse at Super-Leading Logarithms in Monte Carlo Evolution

Nicolas Schalch

Schalch extends the EFT-based Marzili framework to resum the SLL tower \(\alpha_s^{n+3}L^{2n+3}\). The tower comes from two Glauber exchanges, arbitrarily many incoming-collinear emissions, and one terminal soft emission into the gap. The calculation is at the leading nonzero colour order, relative \(1/N_c^2\). The RG path-ordered exponential is turned into a shower-like sequence of Sudakov-sampled emissions acting on paired amplitude and conjugate-amplitude colour flows.

The key technical point is that retaining \(h=d+p\le2\) bounds the flow mismatch to at most two partner swaps at any multiplicity. Glauber interference therefore survives without the colour space blowing up. In qq′, any real emission between the Glaubers gives zero. In qg, such emissions and unequal final flows contribute.

The validation is against analytic resummations at \(\Delta Y=2\), \(\xi=\alpha_sL^2=8\):

Channel Marzili Analytic
Octet (qq′) \(C_O=6.2759(47)\) 6.2754
Singlet (qq′) \(C_S=-11.6853(45)\) −11.6804
qg→qV \(C_{qg}=0.1099(247)\) 0.0971

The pronounced qg turnover is reproduced.

Caveats: - The calculation uses fixed coupling, strict-forward geometry and a single soft emission. The NGL–SLL interplay is not yet included. - There is a real sign problem in qg: the mean absolute weight is about 52× the mean signed weight, hence the ~25% error. - A mapping to CVolver’s cutoff-regulated Coulomb treatment is still open.

A parallel parton-shower effort (Helliwell) is noted.

The full texts of arXiv:2609.31833 and arXiv:2609.33151 were not supplied, so they are not expanded here.

arXiv:2609.30775 §

Parnassus for the CLD Detector: A Generative Machine-Learning Surrogate for Detector Simulation and Reconstruction at the FCC-ee

Umar Sohail Qureshi et al.

What they did. A two-stage conditional flow-matching model maps generator-level particles to CLD PandoraPFA objects:

  • Event stage: a residual MLP generates multiplicity, \(H_T\) and missing-momentum components.
  • Particle stage: a cross-attention DiT with about 5M parameters generates the objects themselves.
  • Outputs: kinematics, species, and \(d_0, z_0\) with their significances.
  • Conditioning: the truth production vertex, which is what lets the model reproduce displaced heavy-flavor tails.

It was trained on \(Z\to q\bar q\) and \(b\bar b\) at 91.2 GeV, with \(c\bar c\) and \(s\bar s\) held out as out-of-distribution tests.

Results.

  • Distance to full simulation: Parnassus is closer than Delphes for every observable, metric and flavor, typically by an order of magnitude. For example, \(W_1(d_0/\sigma_{d_0})\) runs 0.14→0.41 from \(q\bar q\) to \(b\bar b\), against 2.06→4.94 for Delphes.
  • Tagging: a transformer tagger reproduces Pandora AUCs to within about 1%. For a tagger trained on full simulation and applied to surrogate jets, accuracy drops only from 0.694 to 0.682.
  • Speed: full simulation plus reconstruction costs 6.0 s per event. The surrogate costs about 39 ms per event on one CPU core and about 1.3 ms on an A100. The abstract quotes 1.2 ms.

Why it matters. Delphes’s CLD card overestimates flavor separability: it reaches 0.732 accuracy against Pandora’s 0.693. It also mis-models species fractions and \(m_{jj}\). FCC-ee flavor-physics projections built on Delphes may therefore be optimistic. The authors frame this as their own caution about compensating effects in the transfer test.

Caveats.

  • Parnassus slightly underestimates separability. At 80% \(b\)-efficiency, the \(b\)-vs-\(c\) mistag is 1.58% against 1.00% for Pandora.
  • \(b\bar b\) is reproduced worse than the held-out \(c\bar c\) and \(s\bar s\) channels.
  • Validation covers only the Z pole.
  • Hyperparameters are taken unchanged from the CMS configuration.
  • The HitPF variant shows residual impact-parameter deviations.

arXiv:2609.31425 §

Quantum probes of invisible particles in top-quark pair production at the LHC

Blanca Belmonte et al.

What they did. This is a dileptonic \(t\bar t+Y_{0,1}\) study at 14 TeV and 3 ab\(^{-1}\), with the invisible mediator at 10–100 GeV. The selection is \(E_T^{\rm miss}>100\) GeV and \(m_{T2}^{\ell\ell}>90\) GeV. It reduces \(t\bar t+tW\) to 0.04% while keeping 1.3% (scalar) and 4.0% (vector) of the signal at 10 GeV.

Four alternative analyses of the same sample are compared, and not combined:

  • the inclusive yield;
  • \(dN/dM^{\rm vis}_{t\bar t}\);
  • \(dN/dD_{\rm evt}\);
  • the fiducial \(\mathcal D(M^{\rm vis}_{t\bar t})\), fitted with a Gaussian likelihood.

Results.

  • Exclusion ordering: yield < \(M^{\rm vis}\) < \(D_{\rm evt}\) < \(\mathcal D(M^{\rm vis})\), for both mediator spins and all masses.
  • Robustness of the angular variable: \(D_{\rm evt}\) with 50% shape uncertainty still beats \(M^{\rm vis}\) with 20%.
  • Coupling structure: toy-calibrated scalar/pseudoscalar separation is also best with \(\mathcal D(M^{\rm vis})\). The advantage grows at higher mediator mass, where rate-based discrimination degrades.

Why it matters. Spin information carries reach that rate and kinematics alone do not capture, which moves collider QI from demonstration to search tool.

Caveats.

  • The helicity frame is built from generator-level top momenta, which is truth-assisted.
  • \(E_T^{\rm miss}\) is generator-level.
  • There is no detector simulation, and diboson and Z+jets backgrounds are neglected.
  • The gain rests on \(\delta\mathcal D_{\rm syst}=0.015\) and is lost at about 0.05.
  • After cuts, \(\mathcal D\) is no longer a density-matrix quantity.
  • The axial-vector case is deferred because of unitarity issues.

arXiv:2609.30335 §

Metastable Neutron Stars as Transient-Rate Detectors of Heavy Dark Matter

Ziwen Yin et al.

What they did. For neutron stars in a metastable hadronic mass window, the present conversion rate is \(\mathcal R_{\rm tr}=n_{\rm NS}f_{\rm meta}\Gamma_\chi e^{-\Gamma_\chi t_{\rm meta}}\). This rate peaks at \(\Gamma_\chi=1/t_{\rm meta}\). The benchmarks are:

  • \(n_{\rm NS}=3\times10^{15}\) Gpc\(^{-3}\);
  • \(f_{\rm meta}=0.01\);
  • \(t_{\rm meta}=1\) Gyr;
  • \(\mathcal R_{\rm SGRB}=2\times10^3\) Gpc\(^{-3}\)yr\(^{-1}\).

Requiring \(\mathcal R_{\rm tr}\) below the SGRB rate excludes \(7\times10^{-11}<\Gamma_\chi<4\times10^{-9}\) yr\(^{-1}\). An interval exists only if \(C<1/e\); the benchmark gives \(C=0.067\).

Resulting DM bounds. With \(U_{\max}=10\) GeV and gravitationally focused halo DM:

Channel Excluded band
Annihilation (\(m_\chi\gtrsim5\) GeV) \(8\times10^{-34}<\langle\sigma v\rangle(5\,{\rm GeV}/m_\chi)^2<5\times10^{-32}\) cm\(^3\)/s; disfavors thermal relics at \(4\times10^3\)–\(3\times10^4\) GeV
Decay \(3\times10^{29}<\tau_\chi(m_\chi/10\,{\rm GeV})<2\times10^{31}\) s
Inelastic scattering \(\sigma_{\rm inel}(10\,{\rm GeV}/m_\chi)\) between \(2\times10^{-80}\) and \(10^{-78}\) cm\(^2\)
Macroscopic DM narrow band \(\sigma\approx(1.3\)–\(1.6)\times10^{-27}\) cm\(^2\) (with weak \(m_\chi^{1/19}\) scaling), for \(4\times10^{-7}\lesssim m_\chi\lesssim2\times10^{14}\) g

Why it matters. The bound needs no individual star to be identified as hadronic. The allowed rapid-trigger branch has no analogue in survival-based bounds.

Caveats.

  • Every conversion is assumed to yield an observable SGRB, and all SGRBs are attributed to isolated sources (\(f_{\rm iso}=1\)).
  • A single \(t_{\rm meta}\) is used.
  • Energy is assumed to couple maximally to the nucleation mode (\(\epsilon_{\rm bub}\sim1\)).
  • Only hadronic deposition counts; electromagnetic showers are too diffuse.
  • The local halo density of 0.4

arXiv:2609.28752 §

Inclusive B decays, lifetimes, and |V_cb|: a unified heavy-quark expansion analysis

Matteo Fael et al.

The default fit gives \(|V_{cb}|=(42.32\pm0.28_{\rm fit}\pm0.10_{\rm h.o.})\times10^{-3}\). Two methodological choices produce it. First, \(\mu_{\alpha_s}\), \(\mu_{m_b^{\rm kin}}\) and \(\mu_{\overline m_c}\) are floated as fully correlated nuisances under a flat Rfit penalty. Second, no ad hoc power-correction covariance is added; missing orders are instead probed by refitting at \(\mathcal O(1/m_b^{4,5})\). Across those orders \(|V_{cb}|\) moves by 0.2%, while \(\mu_G^2\) shifts by up to 43%.

The fit quality is poor: \(\chi^2/{\rm dof}=119.5/67\), \(p=8.4\times10^{-5}\). Leave-one-out isolates the Belle \(q^2\) moments, and dropping them gives \(p=0.23\). The higher-order coefficients appear to absorb data tension rather than measure genuine power corrections. The fit also finds \(\mu_G^2\) about a factor 3 below the \(B^*\)–\(B\) splitting relation.

The central value is about 1σ above the recent \(41.64\pm0.47\), which includes \(\mathcal O(\alpha_{\rm em})\) effects that lower \(|V_{cb}|\). For the digest’s inclusive-vs-exclusive tension, this is a tighter but method-dependent inclusive number. The authors caution that: - \(\mu_{\alpha_s}=9.13\) GeV sits on the boundary of its range. - The reduced uncertainty “is inherent to the model” and not due to improved inputs. - With scales fixed, \(|V_{cb}|=43.18\) with a \(^{+1.4}_{-0.7}\) scale variation.

Lifetimes come out 13% below experiment (1.1–1.2σ), rescued only by a strongly asymmetric non-leptonic scale error. The ratio \(\tau_{B^+}/\tau_{B^0}=1.082^{+0.031}_{-0.013}\) agrees with \(1.0832\pm0.0034\). The actionable output is the call for new correlated moment and branching-fraction measurements.

arXiv:2609.28819 §

Heavy Inert Doublet Reconciles LZ and IceCube

Hitoshi Murayama et al.

Murayama and Noether reuse the Fan–Reece likelihood and find that the inert-doublet \(\phi_0\to\phi_3\) upscatter matches the higgsino phenomenology to \(\mathcal O(10^{-9})\). The LZ data fix the splitting, \(\delta_0=368^{+12}_{-8}\) keV, essentially independently of mass; they do not select a mass. The \(1/M\) number-density loss is offset by the halo integral as \(v_{\rm min}\) falls.

The mass window comes entirely from other constraints: - Lower edge, 11.3 TeV: an independent re-implementation of the Bose et al. IceCube \(\nu_\mu\) solar limit (at \(\sigma_{\rm SI}^{\rm loop}=0\)) agrees with that analysis to 1–6%. - Upper edge, 20.3 TeV: freeze-out through longitudinal \(W/Z\) requires \(\kappa\approx6.92\,(M/11.3\,{\rm TeV})\), which hits the unitarity bound \(\kappa<4\pi\).

Expected yields are 0.05–0.72 events in the 215–270 keV window, compared with 0.93 for the 1.08 TeV higgsino.

The caveats are substantive: - Solar escape requires \(|\eta|\lesssim0.013\), but \(\kappa^2\) running of \(\eta\) demands percent-level tuning of its initial condition. - The Landau pole sits at only \(\sim23M\) down to \(6M\), or \(2.6M\) at large \(\lambda_\Phi\). - Neutrino spectra were generated at 1080 GeV and reused at all masses; the authors bound the resulting yield error to a factor 1.1–1.7.

\(\Delta T\simeq0.0015\) is harmless. The model is also untestable at FCC-hh.

arXiv:2609.28700 §

Measuring Dual-Superconductor Length Scales in the QCD Vacuum at HERA

Tobias Toll et al.

Toll and Vasim replace Gaussian hotspots with Clem’s vortex profile, \(K_0(\sqrt{b^2+\xi_v^2}/\lambda)\), in a Good–Walker dipole model. Coherent and incoherent moments are computed analytically, with log-normal saturation-scale fluctuations. They fit all 104 H1/ZEUS \(J/\psi\) photoproduction points (67 coherent, 37 incoherent; \(|t|<30\) GeV², \(40\le W\le251\) GeV) and get \(\chi^2/{\rm ndf}=70.8/98\) at \(N_{hs}=8\).

The extracted vacuum parameters are: - \(\lambda=0.191(5)(19)\) fm and \(\xi_v=0.087(5)(12)\) fm. - \(\kappa=2.7(3)(7)\), which is type-II. - \(1/\lambda=1.03(11)\) GeV, compared with the lattice \(1^{+-}\) gluelump at 0.87(15) GeV. The authors note that the gluelump value is scheme-dependent.

The Ginzburg–Landau string tension reproduces \(\sqrt{\sigma_0}\) for \(\alpha_S=0.37(9)\), close to \(\alpha_S(1/\lambda^2)=0.36(2)\). Alternative tails (stretched exponential, power law) worsen the fit by \(\Delta\chi^2=30\). A floating tail exponent returns \(n=1.17(15)\), consistent with the \(K_0\) value of 1.

Caveats: - The scalar sector fits less well. \(m_S=\sqrt2/\xi=3.9(7)\) GeV is 2.4(4)× the \(0^{++}\) glueball. - The dominant uncertainty comes from the weak sensitivity to \(N_{hs}\). - A free normalization, about 1.2, is floated. - The type-II prediction of vortex repulsion cannot be tested, because saturation fluctuations are degenerate with geometric variance at \(|t|\sim1\) GeV². \(M_Y\) tagging at the EIC would break this degeneracy.

The claimed \(Q^2\) independence is a clean EIC/LHeC test.

arXiv:2609.26965 §

Tension between MiniBooNE and MicroBooNE within a 3+1 Sterile Neutrino Framework using Simulation-Based Inference (Julia P. Woodward (Massachusetts Institute of Technology) et al.)

With MiniBooNE’s f_π pion-flux correction removed, adding MicroBooNE BNB+NuMI data lowers the trials-calibrated 3+1 preference from ≥3.6σ (MiniBooNE alone) to 2.7σ. The two experiments sit in ≥2.5σ parameter-goodness-of-fit tension inside 3+1. The frequentist fit uses an SBI likelihood-ratio estimator (DNRE) with a normalizing-flow posterior (SNPE-C) to locate the best fit. The new ingredient is an SBI analogue of the Maltoni–Schwetz PG statistic, built from Δχ² differences relative to null. With it, 48,000 null trials become affordable.

The Wilks’ comparison shows why calibration matters. Without f_π, Wilks gives 7.1σ for MiniBooNE, 5.2σ for the joint fit, and 4.4σ tension. With f_π kept, the SBI joint preference drops to 1.8σ, but the tension rises to ≥3.2σ. In every f_π combination the BNB-only Wilks tension stays above 2σ, so the disagreement is not an artifact of inconsistent flux corrections.

Caveats: - Trial cap. 48k trials cap resolvable significance at ~4.25σ, so anything above 3.5σ is quoted as a lower bound. - Reconstructed NuMI prediction. MicroBooNE has released no event-level MC. The authors rebuilt the NuMI prediction from g4numi/PPFX/GENIE with a tuned approximate detector response (10-knot efficiencies per sample). Along the way they fixed a decay-at-rest flux bug worth up to 10% below 250 MeV. - Suspicious goodness of fit. MicroBooNE’s own χ²/dof of 86.62/179 is anomalously low. - Unexplained ν_μ excess. The low-energy ν_μ excess seen in both detectors is not understood, and the authors warn that quantitative comparisons should be read with care.

arXiv:2609.28457 §

Sterile Neutrino Dark Matter Cries for GeV Heavy Neutral Leptons

Marco Drewes et al.

This is the first νMSM scan using full quantum kinetic equations from baryogenesis through dark-matter production. The kinetics include chirality-flip effects, lepton-asymmetry back-reaction, and, decisively, the charge-odd (MSW-like) part of the HNL Hamiltonian. At the M₁ = 7 keV benchmark, the requirement to produce the QCD-epoch lepton asymmetry needed for Shi–Fuller conversion confines the HNL pair to a closed island.

Viable mass ranges: - Normal ordering (NO): the resonant-decay mechanism survives for 4 ≲ M ≲ 50 GeV. The 50 GeV upper bound is new. - Ignition mechanism (new): a self-amplifying MSW-driven instability that boosts μ_α by ~3 orders of magnitude, to |μ_α| ≃ 10⁻³. It reopens M < 5 GeV down to just above the kaon mass. - Inverted ordering (IO): only 0.8–7 GeV survives.

Experimental reach: - In the meson-accessible range, U² sits at least an order of magnitude above the seesaw floor. - SHiP covers the entire region below 2 GeV and much of 2–5 GeV. HL-LHC and Tera-Z machines cover the rest. - The predicted ΔM_phys adds a DM-specific test. For NO, HNL oscillation lengths can fall inside the SHiP decay volume; for IO, the pair looks Dirac-like.

Caveats: - A single DM mass is studied. - The scan uses momentum-averaged equations and neglects asymmetry depletion by N₁ production; both are checked only on the island boundaries. - Rates come from Fermi theory with free quarks, so the mass edges are uncertain below ~500 MeV and above ~50 GeV. - Structure-formation bounds on the computed spectra are deferred.

arXiv:2609.28348 §

Big Bang Nucleosynthesis as a Probe of First-Order Phase Transitions

Masanori Tanaka

Tanaka adds the reheating (α_n) dependence to BBN bounds on baryon inhomogeneity from supercooled transitions. The local reheating map is nonlinear, the spectral shape comes from the finite-bubble transition-time power spectrum, and proton/neutron diffusion is included. The result is imposed through the deuterium bound ε_BBN < 0.28 (1σ).

Results: - No bound exists for α_n < 0.1 with an optimistic k_cut = ∞, or for α_n < 0.5 with a conservative cut at the mean bubble separation. - For large α_n and 0.5 < T_n < 100 GeV, the disfavored region is β/H_n ≲ 5–6 (conservative) or ≲ 10–13 (optimistic). - Above ~10² GeV, diffusion weakens the bound.

NANOGrav: the NANOGrav-compatible region survives the conservative cut. It is largely disfavored only if the spectrum holds at sub-bubble scales, though much of it is still excluded keeping just modes with k < 2k_th. Evading the bound requires baryogenesis after reheating, or during the transition with baryon production tracking local entropy injection. That is the basis for the digest’s “baryogenesis timing” claim.

Caveats: - The result is 1σ only. - The baryon power spectrum is an admitted effective parametrization, and the bound is highly sensitive to the cutoff. - It assumes a pure exponential nucleation rate and energy release into the SM sector. - The paper is internally loose on bubble-wall baryogenesis. The abstract allows it if production tracks entropy, while Sec. 3 says the bound is “not evaded in general.” - A concurrent linearized analysis (Elahi et al.) finds β/H ≳ 10.3 α/(1+α), which Tanaka calls qualitatively consistent.

Full text was not supplied for the other two picks (Maier et al. arXiv:2609.26870, Gruzinov et al. arXiv:2609.26888), so no deep dives are given for them.

arXiv:2609.25178 §

Discovery of an unexpectedly light and narrow beauty-strange state

LHCb collaboration et al.

LHCb reconstructs \(B_s^0\pi^0\) candidates in 9 fb⁻¹ of Run 1–2 data across three \(B_s^0\) channels (\(D_s^-\pi^+\), \(J/\psi\phi\), \(D_s^-\pi^+\pi^+\pi^-\)), finding a narrow peak at a common mass in all three. A simultaneous fit yields \(343^{+44}_{-41}\) signal events at \(M=5698.9\pm1.5\pm0.6\) MeV/\(c^2\), with local (global) significance exceeding 8 (7)σ. The width is consistent with zero, \(\Gamma<9.8\) (11.8) MeV at 90% (95%) CL. Interpreted as the \(0^+\) scalar \(B_{s0}^*(5700)^0\) — the beauty counterpart of \(D_{s0}^*(2317)^+\) — its \(B_s^0\) splitting of \(332.0\) MeV/\(c^2\) tracks the charm-sector \(349.2\) MeV/\(c^2\), and sits well below quark-potential predictions, the same “mass deficit” seen in charm. This is billed as the first observation of a nonconventional single-beauty hadron, extending the exotic-dynamics pattern (chiral doubling, \(BK\) molecules, or compact tetraquarks) into the open-beauty frontier that had lacked a counterpart.

The main caveat is spin-parity ambiguity: the structure is equally compatible with the \(1^+\) axial-vector partner decaying to \(B_s^{*0}(\to B_s^0\gamma)\pi^0\) with the photon unreconstructed (then \(M=5748.3\) MeV/\(c^2\)), or a superposition of both. Resolving the \((0^+,1^+)\) doublet requires the \(B_s^0\gamma\) spectrum and awaits the larger post-2022 upgrade dataset. The state also enables the first data-driven \(0^+\) pole for \(B_s^0\) heavy-to-light form factors, and predicts light narrow partners for \(\Omega_{cc}^+\) and broad \(B\)-sector states.

arXiv:2609.25158 §

Observation of η(2600) and Threshold Enhancements in the ΛΛ̄ System

BESIII Collaboration et al.

Using 2712.4 million \(\psi(3686)\) events, BESIII performs a model-independent partial-wave analysis of \(\psi(3686)\to\gamma\Lambda\bar\Lambda\), exploiting the self-analyzing weak decays of \(\Lambda/\bar\Lambda\) to extract spin correlations and cleanly separate \(0^{-+}\) from \(0^{++}\). The near-threshold region (97.6% purity) is dominated by the \(^1S_0\) (\(0^{-+}\), 13.2σ) and \(^3P_0\) (\(0^{++}\), 5.4σ) waves, both showing strong threshold enhancements. In the \(^1S_0\) wave a new pseudoscalar \(\eta(2600)\) emerges at \(M=2625\pm26\pm6\) MeV/\(c^2\), \(\Gamma=140\pm58\pm46\) MeV, significance 7.0σ — mass-consistent with the \(X(2600)\) seen in \(J/\psi\to\gamma\pi^+\pi^-\eta'\), and the heaviest light meson to date. Its production relative to \(\eta_c\) (12–22%) matches the \(X(2600)/\eta_c\) ratio (31%) in the other channel, supporting a common identity. The \(^3P_0\) wave gives a \(2262\pm34\) MeV/\(c^2\), \(215\pm35\) MeV state near \(f_0(2200)\).

The finding supports theoretical suggestions of a pseudoscalar glueball-like state near 2.6 GeV, and the \(\eta(\Lambda\bar\Lambda)\) threshold enhancement invites hexaquark/baryonium interpretations (a predicted pseudoscalar \(\Lambda\bar\Lambda\) bound state at ~2.27 GeV/\(c^2\)). Caveats: the \(\eta(2600)\) mass and width depend on lineshape choice (BW vs K-matrix), the \(\eta(\Lambda\bar\Lambda)\) pole position is model-unstable (BW mass 2169 vs KM 1899 MeV/\(c^2\)), and the dominant systematic on the \(^3P_0\) branching fraction reaches −22.8% from additional-wave assumptions.

arXiv:2609.25214 §

Nonlocal advantage of quantum coherence in tau-lepton pairs from electron–positron collisions

Yoav Afik et al.

Afik et al. argue that \(e^+e^-\to\tau^+\tau^-\) is uniquely suited to certify Nonlocal Advantage of Quantum Coherence (NAQC) — the strongest collider quantum correlation studied — because, unlike top pairs at the LHC where NAQC survives only in unmeasurably thin phase-space slivers, tau pairs exhibit NAQC over broad angular windows. For unpolarized \(T\)-states with symmetric correlation matrix, they show NAQC reduces to \(\sum_i|C_i|>\sqrt6\) and construct a single-observable witness \(W_{\rm NA}=\sum_i|C_{ii}|\) (maximized in the diagonal basis), measurable via a pion opening-angle distribution \(D_i=\mathrm{Tr}[P_i\mathbf C]/3\), with \(D_i\ge\sqrt{2/3}\) sufficient for NAQC. At Belle II (\(\sqrt s=10.58\) GeV) NAQC peaks at \(\mathfrak C^{l_1}_{\rm NA,max}=2.59\) within \(|\cos\Theta|<0.23\), containing \(f_{\rm NAQC}=0.18\) of events; at the FCC-ee \(Z\) pole, \(2.96\) within \(0.39\), \(f=0.31\). A 5σ observation needs ~1% precision at Belle II, a few percent at FCC-ee.

The result matures the collider-QI program from entanglement toward a certifiable NAQC hierarchy (\(\text{NAQC}\subset\text{Bell}\subset\ldots\)), and includes the first proposed steering-game implementation in a collider — with asymmetric steering flagged as a future CP-violation probe. Caveats are honest: event counts and NAQC signal are anti-correlated (both peak/dip at \(\cos\Theta=0\)); estimates omit detector acceptance and reconstruction efficiencies; and the analysis is LO electroweak with an ultrarelativistic \(T\)-state approximation. The general proof that NAQC implies Bell nonlocality remains missing (numerical evidence only).

arXiv:2609.22698 §

First-Principles Determination of the QCD Contribution to the Axion-Photon Coupling Using Domain-Wall Fermions

Tian Lin et al.

Using two near-physical \(N_f=2+1\) domain-wall ensembles (24D and 32Dfine, both \(M_\pi\approx143\) MeV), Lin et al. compute \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}\) — the model-independent QCD piece of \(g_{a\gamma\gamma}\) — via anomalous Ward identities rather than direct topological-charge insertion. The trick is to split \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}\) into an exact anomaly term (\(5/3-\xi\), fixed by charge assignments and \(z=m_u/m_d\)) plus a light-quark-mass-suppressed correction extracted from the isoscalar \(P^0\)-VV three-point function, itself only a few percent of the total. Continuum extrapolation gives \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}^{\rm IS}=1.619(30)\), \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}^{\rm IB}=0.347(22)\), and \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}=1.965(35)\), with the IB shift dominated by the explicit \(-\xi\) term and a tiny pion-pole correction (\(-0.0059\), 0.3%).

Why it matters: this is an independent lattice benchmark for the quantity entering every axion-photon search, in a regime where \(E/N\) and \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}\) partially cancel. Crucially, the authors resolve the standing NLO ChPT spread (1.63–2.05): the SU(3) result had a sign error (since corrected by its authors), and the U(3) IB discrepancy traces to an effective \(m_u/m_d\) ~15% too large. With common inputs, ChPT and lattice reconcile. Caveat: chiral fermions keep discretization mild, but only two lattice spacings enter the continuum limit; the extrapolated correction carries ~60% statistical uncertainty, and systematics are explicitly “not yet complete.” Their IS value sits \(2.2\sigma\) from the competing gluonic background-field determination (\(\mathcal{C}_{\rm QCD}^{\rm IS}=1.47(6)\)), underscoring the value of independent systematics.

arXiv:2609.24950 §

Search for a top-philic heavy resonance in association with top quarks

ATLAS Collaboration

ATLAS searches for a colour-singlet top-philic \(Z'\) decaying to \(t\bar t\) and produced via \(t\bar t Z'\) or \(tZ'\), using same-sign-dilepton and \(\geq3\)-lepton final states in 140 fb\(^{-1}\) (13 TeV) plus 56 fb\(^{-1}\) (13.6 TeV). A mass-parameterised transformer classifier (Salt framework), fed event- and object-level features including the \(Z'\) mass hypothesis, discriminates signal from the dominant \(t\bar t t\bar t\) and \(t\bar t W/Z/H\) backgrounds; \(t\bar t W\) and non-prompt backgrounds are constrained in dedicated control regions. No excess is seen (largest local significance \(2.4\sigma\) at \(m_{Z'}=400\) GeV, \(c_t=3\)). For \(c_t=1\), \(\theta=0\), masses below 1150 GeV are excluded (1250 expected); for \(c_t=3\), below 2000 GeV (\(\theta=0\)). Cross-section limits improve ~15× over the previous ATLAS single-lepton \(t\bar t Z'\) search, which could not exclude \(c_t=1\) at any mass.

Why it matters: this is the vector-resonance handle the toponium-vs-scalar debate around the low-mass \(t\bar t\) excess has lacked — a direct top-philic \(Z'\) constraint, now reaching \(c_t=1\). The \((c_t^2,\theta)\) scans exploit the interplay of three-top (\(tZ'\), \(\propto\cos^2\theta\)) and four-top (\(t\bar t Z'\)) modes. Caveats: the result is statistics-dominated, with leading systematics from \(t\bar t t\bar t\) modelling; the SM four-top cross-section itself sits high versus theory (\(22.5^{+6.6}_{-5.5}\) vs \(12.0\pm2.4\) fb), and an alternative +70% \(t\bar t t\bar t\) fit leaves the limit unchanged only through competing normalisation/uncertainty effects.

arXiv:2609.24988 §

Interpreting the LZ 248 keV Event using Dark QCD

Francesco Sannino et al.

Sannino and Turner read LZ’s \(248\pm23_{\rm stat}\pm23_{\rm sys}\) keV recoil candidate as composite inelastic dark matter. A confining \(SU(3)_{\rm D}\) theory with SM-singlet dark quarks produces a Dirac baryon \(B\sim UUD\); a dimension-ten, dark-number-violating operator involving a \(U(1)_B\)-breaking scalar \(S\) splits it into two Majorana states \(\chi_{1,2}\) with \(\delta=m_2-m_1\). Endothermic \(\chi_1 N\to\chi_2 N\) scattering, mediated by a leptophobic baryon-number vector \(V\), suppresses low-energy recoils. Their benchmark — \(m_1=1\) TeV, \(\delta=300\) keV, \(M_V=1.44\) TeV, \(g_B=0.146\) (\(M_V/g_B\simeq9.9\) TeV) — gives \(\sigma_p^0=6.5\times10^{-43}\) cm², inside LZ’s two-sided 90% interval, yielding ~0.26 accepted events in 2.84 tonne-years. A 300 keV splitting maps to a matching scale \(\Lambda_*\simeq12\) TeV for order-one coefficients. The construction embeds anomaly-cancelling spectators, a stabilising \(\mathbb{Z}_{18}\) (forbidding proton decay), and pion/pseudoscalar decay channels.

Why it matters: it is today’s cleanest confining-sector reading of the LZ event, tying the inelastic splitting to explicit dark-QCD dynamics rather than a bolted-on mass gap, and it survives solar-neutrino (\(\sigma_p^0<9.9\times10^{-43}\) cm² for \(b\bar b\)) and ATLAS dijet (\(g_q^{95}\simeq0.084>0.05\)) bounds. Caveats are frank: the benchmark probes the high-speed halo tail near threshold (\(E_R^\star=267\) keV), so rates depend sensitively on the assumed velocity distribution and nuclear response near a diffraction minimum; the event is only \(2.6\sigma\) post-look-elsewhere; the excited fraction must be \(f_2<10^{-3}\); and relic abundance is treated as input, not derived.

arXiv:2609.21947 §

Test of lepton flavor universality with B̅→D⁽*)τν̅_τ and B̅→D⁽*)ℓν̅_ℓ at Belle II

Belle II Collaboration et al.

Using 387×10⁶ Υ(4S) decays with hadronic tagging, Belle II performs a simultaneous 2D fit in missing-mass-squared and residual calorimeter energy across five exclusive \(D^{(*)}\) samples, extracting \(R(D^*)=0.242\pm0.019\text{(stat)}\pm0.016\text{(syst)}\) and \(R(D)=0.439\pm0.055\pm0.046\) (correlations −0.40 stat, −0.20 syst). This supersedes the earlier hadronic-tag \(R(D^*)\) with twice the precision and delivers the first hadronic-tag \(R(D)\) at Belle II — the most precise hadronic-tagged determination to date, matching the best simultaneous measurements. Systematics are dominated by simulated-sample size (4.8%/8.4%) and, for \(R(D)\), \(B\)-tagging and low-momentum \(\pi^0/\gamma\) efficiencies.

\(R(D^*)\) sits within \(0.5\sigma\) of the SM; \(R(D)\) is \(2.0\sigma\) high, but the combination agrees with the SM at \(1.5\sigma\) and with the world average at \(1.3\sigma\). The \(R(D^*)\) central value landing below SM is the notable point for the LFU-anomaly narrative: this measurement neither sharpens nor kills the tension, easing rather than reinforcing it. Honest caveats are visible: the poorly-known gap-mode background is floated and found 42–82% smaller in data than simulation (validated against a \(\bar B\to D^*\pi^0\ell\nu\) control sample), and the hadronic-\(B\) normalization in the \(D^{*0}_{[D^0\pi^0]}\) sample sits \(3.1\sigma\) below prediction in the signal region — both absorbed into systematics rather than driving new uncertainty. The combined signal-plus-\(q^2\)-sideband fit shifts the central values by only ~2%.

arXiv:2609.21921 §

Observation of the doubly charmed baryon Ω_cc⁺

LHCb collaboration et al.

LHCb reports first observation of \(\Omega_{cc}^+\) (scc) in \(\Omega_c^0\pi^+\), with \(\Omega_c^0\to pK^-K^-\pi^+\), using 6.3 fb⁻¹ of 13.6 TeV data from the upgraded detector. A BDT-driven selection (32% signal efficiency, background retention \(2.4\times10^{-4}\)) yields a peak at \(8.7\sigma\) global significance (9.0σ local; 184±24 signal events), validated against the \(\Xi_{cc}^{++}\to\Xi_c^+\pi^+\) control channel (2770±110 events). The mass is \(3725.9\pm1.0\text{(stat)}\pm0.2\text{(syst)}\pm0.4\text{(lifetime)}\pm0.6\text{(ext)}\) MeV/\(c^2\), extracted via the mass difference \(M(\Omega_{cc}^+)-M(\Xi_{cc}^{++})=104.3\) MeV/\(c^2\) to cancel momentum-scale and companion-pion biases. The structure persists above 9σ with a >170 fs decay-time cut, consistent with a weakly decaying state and disfavoring a strong resonance.

This completes the SU(4)\(_f\) spin-½ 20-plet of ground-state baryons — every predicted member now established — and lands squarely in the 3660–3780 MeV/\(c^2\) theory window, adding another rung to the doubly-charmed spectrum after this year’s \(\Xi_{cc}^+\) observation. The main caveat is intrinsic: the \(\Omega_{cc}^+\) lifetime is unmeasured, so the selection-induced mass bias (parameterized linearly over 90–190 fs, correction 0.30 MeV/\(c^2\) at the 140 fs midpoint) carries a dedicated 0.4 MeV/\(c^2\) uncertainty. Lifetime and additional decay channels await the larger Run 3 dataset already in hand.

arXiv:2609.21823 §

LZ's Stairway to Heaνen: limits on elastic DM scatters from solar capture

Debajit Bose et al.

Bose et al. take the elastic NREFT operators that LZ reported as ≥3σ fits to the 248 keV LZ230616 recoil and reverse-engineer their best-fit Wilson coefficients. Since LZ published favored regions only for \(\mathcal{L}_{10}^s\) (magnetic-dipole spin-spin) and the inelastic \(\mathcal{O}_1^s\), they build an approximate energy-dependent likelihood calibrated to reproduce LZ’s published local significances, validating against LZ’s own \(\mathcal{L}_{10}^s\)/\(\mathcal{O}_1^s\) curves (agreement to a factor of a few). They then compute solar capture rates (WimPyC) for each operator, assume equilibrium annihilation to \(\{W^+W^-, b\bar b, \tau^+\tau^-, \nu\bar\nu\}\), and confront the resulting neutrino fluxes with IceCube (2016/2025 solar-direction \(\nu_\mu\), 2014 atmospheric \(\nu_e\)) and Super-K atmospheric data.

Large weak-scale mass ranges fitting LZ230616 are excluded, with the direct \(\nu\bar\nu\) channel giving the strongest and \(b\bar b\) the weakest limits; for \(\mathcal{L}_6^v\), \(\mathcal{L}_8^s\), \(\mathcal{L}_8^v\) the LZ-favored regions are almost entirely ruled out. This extends the “constraint-and-complementarity” mode the digest flags, mirroring the inelastic-operator treatment of Nguyen et al. Two honest caveats: the \(\mathcal{L}_4\) operators survive even the \(\nu\bar\nu\) channel, and for several operators the excluded couplings lie where the EFT cutoff falls below the ~GeV solar-core momentum transfer — i.e., the NREFT description itself is invalid. Standard-halo velocities are assumed throughout, and LMC boosts or substructure could shift both the fits and the capture bounds.

Friday, September 18, 2026

arXiv:2609.19948 §

Constraints on the Higgs boson total width from on-shell signal–background interference in H→γγ

ATLAS Collaboration

ATLAS extracts a direct, coupling-independent bound on \(\Gamma_H\) from the interference between the resonant \(gg\to H\to\gamma\gamma\) amplitude and the continuum diphoton background, which distorts the \(m_{\gamma\gamma}\) lineshape — an antisymmetric peak shift (~\(-70\) MeV at the SM width) plus a ~\(-1.7\%\) yield reduction, both scaling as \(\sqrt{\mu\,\Gamma_H/\Gamma_H^{\rm SM}}\). Using the full 140 fb\(^{-1}\) Run 2 dataset in two \(p_T^{\gamma\gamma}\) categories, with \(m_H\) externally constrained from \(H\to ZZ^*\to4\ell\) and \(\mu\) profiled, they set an observed (expected) 95% CL limit \(\Gamma_H<307\,(258)\) MeV, ~75× the SM value; the best fit is \(\hat\Gamma_H=13^{+75}_{-13}\) MeV and \(\mu=1.08\pm0.12\). Statistics-only, the limit tightens to 105 (87) MeV.

Why it matters: unlike the off-shell \(ZZ/WW\) method (ATLAS combined \(\Gamma_H=3.6^{+2.1}_{-1.6}\) MeV), this probe makes no assumption that on- and off-shell couplings coincide, so it remains valid under exotic or invisible decays — a genuinely orthogonal handle even at ~30× weaker sensitivity. The caveat is explicit and dominant: the measurement is systematics-limited, with the largest single contribution (49 MeV at 68% CL) from the 39% theory uncertainty on the \(gg\)-interference \(K\)-factor (\(K_I=1.64\), NLO→NNLOsv), followed by the \(m_H\) constraint (43 MeV). Because interference scales as \(\sqrt{\Gamma_H}\), the \(K_I\) uncertainty dominates entirely near the limit. The authors flag that interference calculations matched to parton showers are the needed improvement, and note only two \(p_T^{\gamma\gamma}\) bins are justified given current modelling.

arXiv:2609.20545 §

Computability of GPDs near x=±ξ in Lattice QCD

Yushan Su et al.

Su, Ji, Liu, and Zhang argue that the LaMET large-momentum expansion for GPDs does not require the naive EFT condition \(2|x\pm\xi|P^z\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}\) near the transition points \(x=\pm\xi\), where one parton momentum vanishes. Their argument parallels DVCS factorization: soft contributions associated with the vanishing scale are power-suppressed, just as photon-DA physics enters DVCS only at \(O(\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^2/Q^2)\). The condition reduces to keeping at least one hard scale, \(2(|x|+|\xi|)P^z\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}\), dramatically enlarging the computable window that would otherwise exclude bands of width ~0.4 around \(x=\pm\xi\) for realistic \(P^z\sim2\)–3 GeV.

Reanalyzing four existing nonzero-skewness datasets (ETMC20/21/25, MSULat23) with complete one-loop matching and a carefully ordered regulator hierarchy (\(\epsilon_2\ll|\epsilon_1|\to0\), the only convergent ordering), they recover GPDs that are continuous at \(x=\pm\xi\) but with discontinuous derivatives — the expected partonic threshold kink from soft-quark zero modes, seen on the lattice for the first time and consistent with polynomiality-preserving GPD models. The previously reported discontinuities are attributed to regulator artifacts in the matching. This yields the first first-principles prediction of GPDs in the distribution-amplitude-like region (\(|x|<\xi\)), smoothly connecting quark and antiquark PDF-like regimes — directly relevant to DVCS extractions and the GUMP global-analysis program.

Caveats: the result rests on one-loop matching applied to existing data at coarse pion masses (mostly 260 MeV) and modest momenta, and the region remains uncomputable when both \(x\) and \(\xi\) are small (near \(x=0\)) or near the endpoints \(x=\pm1\).

arXiv:2609.20154 §

Observation of double ss̅ production in e⁺e⁻ collision at √(s)=3.08 GeV

BESIII Collaboration et al.

BESIII reports the first systematic observation of double-\(s\bar{s}\) production in the \(e^+e^-\) continuum, tagging a prompt \(\phi\) recoiling against a second strangeness-carrying system \(X(s\bar{s}) \in \{\eta,\eta',K^+K^-,K^+K^{*-},K^-K^{*+},K^0\bar{K}^0,K^0\bar{K}^{*0}\}\). From 167 pb\(^{-1}\), they measure \(\sigma(e^+e^-\to\phi+\text{anything})=506.3\pm6.9\pm19.7\) pb (a first) plus the individual \(\phi+X(s\bar{s})\) cross sections, with \(\phi K^+K^{*-}\), \(\phi K^-K^{*+}\), \(\phi K^0\bar{K}^0\), \(\phi K^0\bar{K}^{*0}\) reported for the first time. After subtracting ISR (\(\phi\gamma_{\rm ISR}\)) to isolate the QCD-driven part, the ratio is \(R_{s\bar{s}}=(40.4\pm1.7_{\rm stat}\pm1.5_{\rm syst})\%\).

Why it matters: this large ratio directly echoes the double-\(c\bar{c}\) surprise seen by Belle and BABAR at 10.6 GeV, where \(R_{c\bar{c}}\) exceeded early NRQCD predictions and drove years of theory refinement. Extending the phenomenon to the strange sector — in the deep non-perturbative regime — supplies a clean hadronization number that challenges the frameworks tuned to \(R_{c\bar{c}}\) and opens a window on possible flavor correlations in fragmentation. The synergy of strange and charm “flavor portals” is the framing.

Caveat, stated by the authors: the measurement omits higher-multiplicity final states (e.g. \(\phi K^+K^-\pi^0\)), so \(R_{s\bar{s}}\) is a lower bound on total double-\(s\bar{s}\) rate; \(f_{s\bar{s}}\) assumptions for \(\eta/\eta'\) and \(f\)-states carry model dependence, and several sub-channels have 6–11% systematics dominated by efficiency and background modelling.

arXiv:2609.17267 §

Non-singlet splitting functions at four loops

Thomas Gehrmann et al.

The full non-singlet splitting functions \(P_{\rm ns}^{+}\), \(P_{\rm ns}^{-}\), and \(P_{\rm ns}^{s}=P_{\rm ns}^{\rm v}-P_{\rm ns}^{-}\) are now known analytically at four loops for arbitrary Mellin moment \(n\) — the reference the field will cite for N³LO evolution. The method computes off-shell quark operator matrix elements for twist-two spin-\(n\) operators, using generating functions to keep \(n\) arbitrary while retaining standard IBP identities (via Qgraf, Form, Reduze2, and the finite-field Finred code). Rather than fight the elliptic geometries appearing in one color sector, the differential equations in the tracing parameter \(t\) are Taylor-expanded about \(t=0\), where the integrals reduce to known four-loop propagator masters; moments up to \(n\sim4000\) are then generated and the all-\(n\) result reconstructed from an ansatz of harmonic sums up to weight 7. Results confirm all known color coefficients and reproduce independent fixed-moment checks, the four-loop cusp coefficient \(A_q^{(4)}\), and yield fully analytic virtual anomalous dimensions \(B_q^{(4)}\) and \(B_g^{(4)}\) plus the eikonal soft anomalous dimension \(f_r^{(4)}\).

Why it matters: consistent N³LO LHC cross sections require exactly these functions, previously available only as numerical approximations or partial analytics — anchoring today’s precision-QCD theme. Agreement with the Moch–Ruijl–Ueda–Vermaseren–Vogt numerics is good, though \(P_{\rm ns}^{(3),+}\) sits slightly above their error band at small \(x\). One honest wrinkle: the \(\ln^4 x\) small-\(x\) terms disagree with the resummation prediction of Davies et al., by a piece subleading in \(N_c\) and proportional to \(\zeta_2\) — possibly an \((i\pi)^2\) phase effect the authors flag for investigation. The singlet case remains, requiring gauge-variant operator renormalization.

arXiv:2609.16219 §

Enhanced Standard Model bound on charm CP violation from QCD penguins

Ali Mohamed et al.

Using light-cone sum rules with an auxiliary momentum inserted at the weak vertex (following the \(B\to\pi\pi\) methodology of Khodjamirian) to avoid parasitic cuts, Mohamed and Piscopo compute for the first time the QCD-penguin operator matrix elements for \(D^0\to K^+K^-\) and \(D^0\to\pi^+\pi^-\) at leading order in \(\alpha_s\). The scalar penguins \(Q_5,Q_6\) are strongly enhanced: \(-i\langle Q_6\rangle_{\pi\pi}=2.09\) GeV³ versus \(0.228\) for the current-current \(Q_1^d\) — roughly a factor of ten. Two sources drive this: quark-condensate contributions arising at tree level (enhanced by \(16\pi^2\) over the loop-induced terms) and an annihilation-type contribution from the \(\bar u u\) component entering already at twist three. Recomputed current-current matrix elements agree with the earlier Lenz et al. determination to within 5–10%, and the resulting branching ratios (\(\mathcal{B}(\pi\pi)=1.22\times10^{-3}\), \(\mathcal{B}(KK)=2.44\times10^{-3}\)) match HFLAV within their ~100% uncertainties.

Why it matters: the 2019 LHCb \(\Delta A_{\rm CP}\sim10^{-3}\) needs penguin-to-tree ratios of order unity, an order of magnitude above naive estimates, and whether this fits the SM hinges on hadronic matrix elements. Enhanced penguins narrow the SM-vs-experiment gap — direct progress on the standing charm-CPV puzzle flagged in August. Caveat, stated plainly: the strong phases remain unconstrained at LO \(\alpha_s\), so no quantitative CP-asymmetry prediction follows; the bound assumes maximal phases. The annihilation approximation carries an expected \(\geq30\%\) error because \(s_0/m_D^2\sim20\)–30% (versus ~3% for \(B\) decays).

arXiv:2609.16120 §

Dark Radiation Sticks Together: Dark QCD and the Hubble Tension

Matthew R. Buckley et al.

Buckley, Fernandez, and Putney propose a confining \(SU(N_c)\) dark sector with heavy stable quarks (the dark matter) and light quarks whose confinement at \(\Lambda_{\rm D}\sim\) MeV turns the dark radiation into light dark pions. Crucially, residual strong interactions make this radiation a viscous imperfect fluid rather than free-streaming — modeled in a modified CLASS with drag (\(\Gamma_{\rm drag}\)) and relaxation-time equations. The viscosity suppresses high-\(\ell\) CMB oscillations relative to free-streaming \(\Delta N_{\rm eff}\), letting the model raise \(H_0\) without spoiling the high-\(\ell\) multipoles or \(S_8\). A full MCMC over Planck, ACT, DESI DR2, Pantheon+, SH0ES, and BBN yields \(H_0=71.60\pm0.63\) km/s/Mpc with a residual tension of \(\sim3\sigma\) (\(Q_{\rm DMAP}=3.0\sigma\) vs \(6.0\sigma\) for \(\Lambda\)CDM), competitive with Hubble World Cup “finalists.” It also fits DESI BAO better than Planck-only \(\Lambda\)CDM.

Why it matters: this is a genuinely different mechanism for the Hubble tension — not just extra \(N_{\rm eff}\), but self-interacting radiation whose fluid behavior evades the high-\(\ell\) constraints that cap free-streaming models near \(4\sigma\). The large late-time self-interaction cross section (\(\sim\Lambda_{\rm D}^{-2}\)) evades Bullet Cluster bounds because hard momentum transfer requires perturbative heavy-quark scattering. Honest caveats: the best-fit \(\Lambda_{\rm D}\sim1\) MeV produces dark acoustic oscillations in tension with Lyman-\(\alpha\), though \(\Lambda_{\rm D}\gtrsim3\)–5 MeV evades this with negligible fit degradation; and the CLASS modifications were partly LLM-generated.

arXiv:2609.15321 §

Confronting the Higgsino Interpretation of the LZ Event with Astrophysical Uncertainties and Solar Capture

Aditya Ghosh et al.

The higgsino reading of LZ’s 248 keV recoil cannot survive the solar-capture bound no matter how the halo is stretched. Ghosh, Chavez, and Kelso compute the expected LZ signal for a 1.1 TeV thermal higgsino (WimPyDD, including the factor-4 cross-section correction of Pospelov–Ramani) across the full Standard Halo Model parameter space and, separately, using \(N\)-body hydrodynamical halos that include the LMC’s imprint on the high-speed tail. IceCube/Super-K non-observation of solar annihilation neutrinos requires the inelastic splitting \(\delta\gtrsim 506\)–\(566\) keV, which for a 248 keV xenon recoil demands lab-frame speeds \(v_{\rm min}\sim 950\)–\(1025\) km/s. In the SHM, the \(\delta\gtrsim566\) keV bound requires \(v_0\gtrsim240\) km/s independent of \(v_{\rm esc}\) — well beyond the \(2\sigma\) range of any measured \(v_0\) (up to 256 km/s), even at optimistic \(\rho_\chi=0.45\) GeV cm\(^{-3}\) and with a 5%-probability upward fluctuation. In the LMC-aware simulated halo, \(\delta=514\) keV is the largest splitting that yields one event in the 200–300 keV window; all curves fall below the fluctuation threshold by \(\delta\sim530\) keV.

Why it matters: This is exactly the discriminator mode the digest tracks — an attempt to close the last astrophysical escape hatch rather than add another fit. The thermal higgsino is now doubly cornered: kinematics and solar capture together leave no viable window at \(\delta\gtrsim566\) keV.

Caveat: Only the weaker \(\delta\gtrsim506\) keV bound (tree-level \(Z\) up-scatter, not the loop-induced elastic channel) leaves a sliver of SHM viability, and only when both \(v_0\) and \(v_{\rm esc}\) sit at their upper limits. The LMC-tail extension beyond 950 km/s is a phenomenological exponential, not a simulated result.

arXiv:2609.15782 §

Argon as the test of two interpretations of the LZ 248 keV recoil

Howard Baer et al.

Baer and Barger reduce the endothermic-vs-exothermic debate to a single already-recorded measurement. Both interpretations require an internal splitting, but they flow energy oppositely. Read exothermically (pseudo-Dirac downscatter releasing \(|\delta|\)), the event predicts a nuclear-recoil line in argon at \(E_{\rm Ar}=|\delta|\,m_\chi/(m_\chi+m_{\rm Ar})\) — 336 keV for \(m_\chi=200\) GeV, ranging 268–525 keV over the exothermic family — at ~3–4 events/tonne-yr, roughly ten times the equivalent xenon rate. Argon wins because its Helm form factor at 336 keV sits in the first diffraction lobe (\(F^2=0.056\)) while xenon at 248 keV sits near its second-lobe minimum (\(F^2=1.6\times10^{-4}\)); the factorized rate ratio returns 9.5 at 200 GeV. Read endothermically (ground-state upscatter by the fast halo tail), argon predicts zero events: the argon threshold speed exceeds the halo maximum (1224 vs 796 km/s at 1.1 TeV). The ratio depends only on two-body kinematics, the two form factors, and the coupling’s charge structure — not the mediator or excited fraction.

Why it matters: DEAP-3600 already holds the data. The published 231-day exposure implies ~7 events (full target) or 1.7 (fiducial) at the benchmark rate; the 813-day first run holds ~25 and 6. A reanalysis for single-scatter nuclear recoils between 200–600 keV (near 85 keV\(_{\rm ee}\)) either finds the line and fixes \(m_\chi\) from the two line positions, or kills the exothermic reading — no new detector required.

Caveat: The fiducial mass at ~500 photoelectrons is unpublished; expected counts within the WIMP-search fiducial volume are only a few, and background control (shadowed alphas, \(^{39}\)Ar, radiogenic neutrons) rests on DEAP’s own estimates.

arXiv:2609.15655 §

First Observation of D_s⁺→f_0(980)μ⁺ν_μ

BESIII Collaboration et al.

BESIII observes the semi-muonic decay \(D_s^+\to f_0(980)\mu^+\nu_\mu\) (with \(f_0\to\pi^+\pi^-\)) for the first time, using 7.33 fb\(^{-1}\) at \(E_{\rm CM}=4.128\)–4.226 GeV and a double-tag technique (839k single tags across 15 hadronic modes). From \(196\pm22\) signal events they measure \(\mathcal{B}=(1.59\pm0.18_{\rm stat}\pm0.11_{\rm syst})\times10^{-3}\). Combined with the earlier BESIII electronic-channel result, the LFU ratio \(\mathcal{B}(\mu)/\mathcal{B}(e)=0.92\pm0.13_{\rm stat}\pm0.08_{\rm syst}\) agrees with the SM range 0.94–1.11 — no LFU violation at current precision, and the first such test in a scalar-meson \(c\to s\) transition. A dynamical fit (simple-pole form factor, relativistic Flatté line shape) yields \(f_+^{f_0}(0)|V_{cs}|=0.490\pm0.059\pm0.025\); averaging with the electronic channel gives \(0.500\pm0.016\pm0.020\), and with CKMfitter \(|V_{cs}|\) the most precise \(D_s\to f_0\) form factor to date, \(f_+^{f_0}(0)=0.514\pm0.017\pm0.021\).

Why it matters: Beyond extending LFU tests into scalar final states, the branching fraction implies a mixing angle \(\phi=(28.0\pm26.6)^\circ\), favoring an \(s\bar s\)-dominant \(f_0(980)\) in the \(q\bar q\) picture. The precise form factor discriminates sharply among models: consistent with CLFD/DR/QCDSR but in up-to-\(5\sigma\) tension with LCSR, LFQM, and CCQM predictions, all of which now incorporate the finite \(f_0\) width. This is the consolidating-null flavor picture the digest notes — LFU intact, precision turned onto hadronic structure.

Caveat: The 13% statistical LFU uncertainty leaves ample room; the extracted \(\phi\) carries a large error. \(\mu/\pi\) misidentification is handled by retaining both track pairings and fitting the wrong-combination shape.

arXiv:2609.12914 §

What has the LHC told us about the electroweakino sector of the MSSM?

Peter Athron et al.

GAMBIT’s most complete collider recast of the MSSM electroweak sector to date: a frequentist global fit over 34 Run 2 LHC searches, 63 Run 2 measurements (via Contur), and LEP cross-section limits, scanning \(M_1\), \(M_2\), \(\mu\), \(\tan\beta\) with all other sparticles decoupled. The headline is that a genuine general bound now exists where the 2018 fit found none — for a light bino \(\tilde\chi_1^0\), exclusion reaches \(m_{\tilde\chi_2^0}\gtrsim760\) GeV in both the \((m_{\tilde\chi_2^0},m_{\tilde\chi_1^0})\) and chargino planes. Adding a light (\(m_{3/2}=1\) eV) gravitino pushes exclusions to \((m_{\tilde\chi_1^0},m_{\tilde\chi_2^0})\sim(900,1000)\) GeV, but a Higgsino-dominated LSP survives down to \(\approx650\) GeV. The much-discussed ATLAS soft-dilepton excess (\(\Delta m\sim20\) GeV) is reproduced as a weak preference near \(m_{\tilde\chi_2^0}\approx200\) GeV, but the CMS zero-lepton and soft-lepton searches disfavour exactly the ATLAS-preferred region — no scenario fits both simultaneously.

Why it matters: this is the empirical backdrop the month of LZ-higgsino fits has largely ignored. The Higgsino-degenerate corner that survives at ~650 GeV is precisely where compressed dark-matter scenarios live, and closing it at small \(\Delta m\) is conceded to be “very challenging” even at HL-LHC. Honest caveats: signal yields use LO+LL (not NLO) cross-sections, monojet and long-lived-particle searches are omitted, the \(b\)-jet regions of ATLAS_2BoostedBosons are not implemented, and the \(\tilde G\)-EWMSSM best fit rests on a 36 fb\(^{-1}\) CMS diphoton excess that newer full-Run-2 photon searches do not corroborate.

arXiv:2609.12683 §

Search for a light pseudoscalar Higgs boson in boosted μμττ final states

CMS Collaboration

CMS searches \(H\to aa\to\mu\mu\tau\tau\) in 138 fb\(^{-1}\) of 13 TeV data, where the large \(m_H\)–\(m_a\) hierarchy collimates each \(\tau\tau\) pair. The key methodological advance is dedicated boosted-ditau reconstruction: modified HPS seeding for \(\tau_\mu\tau_h\)/\(\tau_e\tau_h\) (efficiency gains up to factors of 2–4 at high \(m_H\)), plus a new deep-network tagger, DeepDiTau, that classifies AK4 jets as \(\tau_h\tau_h\) at 70% efficiency and ~1% mistag — enabling the high-rate all-hadronic channel for the first time. Combining four \(\tau\tau\) channels covers 94% of \(a\to\tau\tau\) decays. No excess is seen. Model-independent 95% CL limits on \(\sigma_H\times\mathcal{B}/\sigma_{\rm SM}\) reach \(5\times10^{-5}\) to \(2.7\times10^{-4}\) for \(m_a=3.6\)–21 GeV at \(m_H=125\) GeV, and — for the first time at the LHC in \(\tau\tau\) final states — extend to a heavy scalar up to \(m_H=1\) TeV, where limits span \(3.5\times10^{-4}\) to \(1.5\times10^{-3}\) for \(m_a=3.6\)–50 GeV.

Why it matters: this is the day’s genuine light-pseudoscalar collider result, complementary to the LZ/axion model-building. It sets the most stringent \(\mathcal{B}(H\to aa)\) limits to date for \(3.6<m_a<4.2\) GeV in Type-I/II 2HDM+S, improves on prior \(\mu\mu\tau\tau\) results by factors 2–5, and opens the heavy-scalar \(\to aa\) window entirely. Caveats: sensitivity degrades at low \(m_a\) where decay products fully merge, \(\Upsilon\) resonances contaminate near \(m_a\approx10\) GeV, and above 4.2 GeV the charm-tagging analysis remains stronger.

arXiv:2609.12775 §

Constraints on Galactic Dark Photons from a DALI Prototype

Javier De Miguel et al.

A proof-of-principle DALI haloscope — a 20-plate yttria-stabilized zirconia Fabry–Pérot resonator read out by an antenna, run at fixed \(\lambda/8\) spacing with \(Q_L\approx2200\) near 6.907 GHz and \(T_{\rm sys}\simeq59\) K — reinterprets 36 hours of data (originally an axion search) as a dark-photon search. No magnetic field is needed since kinetic mixing converts dark photons at the dielectric discontinuities. After baseline removal, iterative RFI masking, grand-spectrum stacking with the lab-frame line shape, and an end-to-end injection efficiency of \(\eta_{\rm e2e}\simeq0.60\), no significant excess survives. Fourteen retained clusters all fall below adopted confidence and are flagged for rescan. The result excludes kinetic mixing \(\chi\lesssim6.8\times10^{-14}\) at 28.54 μeV (6.902 GHz), across 88.1% of the unmasked 28.482–28.610 μeV band at 90% CL — claimed as the strongest laboratory bound in this frequency range.

Why it matters: it fits today’s haloscope theme (alongside DALI’s companion resonator paper), demonstrating that the open Fabry–Pérot architecture — whose cross section decouples from resonance frequency — can deliver competitive high-frequency limits with mature commercial hardware. As a magnet-free dark-photon channel, it is a cheap, fast complement to cavity haloscopes. Caveats are explicit: only a single frequency step (one plate spacing), a scaled-down prototype (~1:100 in area), an undercoupled antenna (\(\beta\simeq0.36\)), assumption that dark photons saturate the local density with randomly-oriented polarization (\(\kappa^2=1/3\)), and a residual \(0.09\sigma\) baseline systematic.

arXiv:2609.11151 §

Qubit-Qutrit Quantum Tomography of hadronic Λφ and ΛK*⁰ systems

Veronica Kanachova et al.

Extending collider quantum tomography to unequal-spin final states, Kanachova et al. formalize the spin-½⊗1 density matrix for Λφ and ΛK*⁰ pairs and confront its inescapable incompleteness: the parity-violating Λ→pπ⁻ is fully self-analyzing, but the parity-conserving V→PP decay carries no rank-1 (dipole) analyzer, so only 23 of the 35 spin parameters are measurable — the meson vector polarization Pᵢ (3) and the dipole–dipole correlations Cᵢⱼ (9) drop out identically. The key result is that entanglement can still be certified exactly from this censored data. Zeroing the 12 missing parameters is equivalent to a “partial time reversal” on the meson (𝒰_B∘T_B), and the reconstruction ρ_meas satisfies ρ_meas^{T_B}=𝒰_B(ρ_meas), so the Peres–Horodecki test collapses to a single diagonalization: a negative eigenvalue of ρ_meas certifies entanglement, with no false positives, and no analysis of the same 23 observables can do better. Pure entangled states retain exactly half their negativity; mixed states have quantifiable “blind windows” (e.g. isotropic-noise dilution entangled for p>1/4 but uncertifiable until p>2/5).

Why it matters: this opens a new hadronization probe. Because local dressing cannot create entanglement, surviving correlations lower-bound the quark-pair correlation at production, and comparing degradation in Λφ/ΛK*⁰ (meson channel) against ΛΛ̄ (baryon channel) as a function of ΔR isolates p_meson vs p_baryon — a coherence-transport quantity never measured. Estimators are simple angular moments extractable from existing datasets. Caveat: the theorems’ validity relies on the specific 12-parameter censoring; other 12-subsets give false positives, and much of the numerical verification was AI-assisted (Claude Opus 5) with a stated but unproven pure-state conjecture.

arXiv:2609.11026 §

Positive- and negative-parity charm-strange and bottom-strange mesons from lattice QCD

Forrest Guyton et al.

Guyton and Meinel deliver a full RBC/UKQCD determination of decay constants, binding energies, and Weinberg compositeness for the lightest scalar, pseudoscalar, vector, and axial-vector Dₛ and Bₛ mesons, using domain-wall light/strange quarks and anisotropic clover charm/bottom on seven ensembles (mπ = 139–431 MeV, a = 0.073–0.114 fm) with combined chiral-continuum extrapolation. For negative-parity states they obtain f_{Dₛ}=251.4(2.1)(0.4)(2.5) MeV, f_{Dₛ}=272.5(4.3)(1.0)(2.7), f_{Bₛ}=228.4(5.8)(0.5)(2.3), f_{Bₛ}=229.2(4.4)(0.8)(2.3) MeV, plus ratios f_{Dₛ}/f_{Dₛ}=1.086(14)(11) and f_{Bₛ}/f_{Bₛ}=1.003(22)(10), consistent with FLAG. The positive-parity sector uses a GEVP with q̄q, derivative, and meson-meson-scattering operators (the latter at both source and sink), Lüscher’s method, and effective-range expansions to extract infinite-volume bound-state masses: m_{Dₛ0}−m_D−m_K=−48(14)(4) MeV, m_{Dₛ1}−m_{D}−m_K=−61(15)(2), m_{Bₛ0}−m_B−m_K=−69(13)(4), m_{Bₛ1}−m_{B}−m_K=−77(10)(5). The charm results agree with experiment (−45.0(6), −44.7(6) MeV). Positive-parity decay constants — including the first-ever lattice f_{Bₛ0*}=207(12)(8)(2) and f_{Bₛ1}=196(16)(11)(2) MeV — are reported.

Why it matters: the below-threshold binding, small positive-parity decay constants, and scattering lengths consistently support a predominantly D⁽⁾K/B⁽⁾K molecular interpretation of the Dₛ0*(2317)-like states, and the negative-parity constants feed dispersive bounds on b→s form factors relevant to the b→sℓℓ anomalies. Caveats acknowledged: two-pion left-hand cuts (which extend above the ground-state energies at low mπ) are neglected, invalidating strict Lüscher/ERE there, and weak-binding corrections to Weinberg’s criterion are expected to be large in some channels.

arXiv:2609.11641 §

Distinguishing a pseudoscalar from a vector t t̄ resonance with top-quark spin correlations at the HL-LHC

Ahmed Bellagroudi et al.

Bellagroudi and Fassi ask a post-discovery question: given a t t̄ resonance, can dilepton spin correlations separate a CP-odd 2HDM pseudoscalar A (0⁻, spin-singlet ¹S₀, iγ₅ vertex) from a leptophobic topcolor Z′ (1⁻, chiral vector)? Using MadGraph+Pythia+Delphes with matrix-element spin-preserving decays and neutrino-weighting reconstruction, they find a parton-level helicity-correlation gap Δc_hel≃0.43 (A: +0.33, Z′: −0.08), nearly mass-independent, surviving reconstruction with dilution ≈0.66. Normalizing A to CMS HIG-22-013 coupling limits and rate-matching Z′ so the test probes spin structure, the LO t t̄-only baseline gives 2.27σ (400 GeV) and 2.74σ (800 GeV) at 3000 fb⁻¹, using a 4×4 (c_hel, c_han) template likelihood. Reverse non-nested direction agrees; equal-yield tests give 1.86 and 2.70σ (confirming 800 GeV is shape-driven); profiling a Gaussian-constrained SM c_hel nuisance yields 2.07/2.63σ (optimistic) down to 1.67/2.26σ. A g⁴-mapped scan places 5σ at couplings ~22%/16% above reference.

Why it matters: this makes the toponium-vs-scalar interpretation debate quantitative, showing spin correlations can in principle assign resonance spin/parity where a rate excess cannot. The 800 GeV point is the cleanest demonstration; 400 GeV is intrinsically fragile because the near-threshold SM continuum is itself spin-singlet/pseudoscalar-like (the same physics driving threshold entanglement enhancement), and 1500 GeV is unreachable (~12 events). Honest caveats abound: LO normalization, fixed on-shell widths, no non-t t̄ backgrounds, and — critically — omitted signal–continuum interference, plus a single-mode “bracket” nuisance rather than a proper mass-dependent sideband fit.

arXiv:2609.09385 §

Heavy Higgsino Interpretation of the LZ Event

Kevin Langhoff

Langhoff performs a 2D likelihood fit of pure-higgsino inelastic scattering to the LZ 248 keV recoil plus the empty high-energy sideband (350–675 keV), then folds in recomputed IceCube solar-capture bounds. The data alone prefer a light branch, \(m_\chi\approx(240,450)\) GeV, but that region is excluded by solar capture (which sets a floor on the splitting \(\delta\) via neutrinos from captured-higgsino annihilation). The thermal higgsino at 1.1 TeV overfills the sideband (\(N_{\rm SB}\gtrsim2\), \(\Delta\chi^2\) up to 11). The only corner consistent with the event, the empty sideband and solar capture is \(m_\chi\approx(10^5,10^6)\) GeV with \(\delta\approx(330,480)\) keV — where DM dilution (\(\rho\propto1/m_\chi\)) forces small \(\delta\) and the sideband/ROI event ratio lands near 0.8–1 (versus \(\sim0.1\) light, \(\gtrsim15\) thermal), a testable prediction. Such a heavy higgsino cannot arise from freeze-out, so he builds a proof-of-concept: freeze-out during early matter domination reheating to \(T_{\rm RH}\approx1\) TeV, tied to the \(\mu\) term via a Kim-Nilles mechanism that also solves strong CP.

Why it matters: it closes today’s headline arc — the ordinary thermal reading is dead, and survival demands a \(10^7\) GeV gaugino scale plus a bespoke cosmology, connecting a direct-detection anomaly to axion/strong-CP physics. Caveats are frank: the \(\Delta\chi^2\) contours are a “heuristic guide” only, the heavy point is not the best fit, results depend on LMC-tail halo modeling, solar-capture curves bracket limiting behavior rather than a precise bound, and the model needs a tuned axion misalignment (\(\theta\lesssim0.05\)).

arXiv:2609.09318 §

Nucleon Strong-Interaction Size from Charmonium Photoproduction

Xiangdong Ji et al.

Ji, Joosten, Meziani, and Pefkou extract the nucleon’s overall strong-interaction radius by combining world near-threshold \(J/\psi\) photoproduction data (GlueX, Hall C, CLAS12) with a recent Bayesian quark-sector GFF analysis. Fitting the differential cross sections in a holographic-QCD model — \(A_g(t)\) dipole, \(C_g(t)\) tripole, \(B_g=0\) — yields the gluon stress-energy form factors (\(\chi^2/{\rm ndf}=1.005\) after dropping the lowest GlueX energy bin). Energy-momentum conservation then relates the total \(A,C\) form factors to the scalar (trace-anomaly) color-field distribution \(G^s(t)\), whose Breit-frame radius is \(\langle r_s^2\rangle^{1/2}=0.94\pm0.09\) fm — larger than the PDG charge radius (0.8409 fm) and larger than the mass radius, defining a gluonic “skin” analogous to the neutron skin in \(^{208}\)Pb.

Why it matters: it delivers a clean, quotable first experimental number for the nucleon’s strong-interaction extent, mapping the color field that generates most of the nucleon mass rather than its charge. The ordering (strong > charge > mass) agrees with lattice and dispersive determinations cited. Caveats are stated plainly: the identification of the photoproduction amplitude with the gluonic SET is framework-dependent (open-charm and alternative production mechanisms may contaminate), \(B_g=0\) is an assumption, and the result inherits both statistical and model uncertainty. SoLID and EIC/ePIC are projected to improve precision by up to an order of magnitude.

arXiv:2609.10504 §

Can Elastic Neutrino Scattering Account for the LZ230616 Event?

Ayan Chattaraj et al.

Chattaraj et al. systematically test whether coherent elastic neutrino–nucleus scattering (CEνNS) can produce the 248 keV LZ recoil. Kinematics alone require \(E_\nu\gtrsim118\) MeV, killing solar and DSNB fluxes; only atmospheric neutrinos reach that energy, but their SM rate in the signal bin (225–271 keV) is \(R_{\rm I}\simeq2\times10^{-6}\) events. Introducing BSM scalar or vector mediators tuned to give one signal-region event overshoots the low-energy bins by \(\mathcal{O}(10^5)\) events — six orders of magnitude above background — so the \(R_{\rm I}=1\) contour sits entirely inside the \(R_{\rm II}\)-excluded region. Exotic monochromatic fluxes fare no better: DM annihilation (\(\chi\chi\to\nu\bar\nu\)) requires \(\langle\sigma v\rangle\gtrsim10^{-18}\) cm³/s and DM decay \(\tau_\chi\lesssim10^{15}\) s, both far past existing bounds, while overpopulating low-recoil bins by hundreds of events. PBH evaporation is worst: the exponentially soft Hawking spectrum gives \(R_{\rm II}/R_{\rm I}\sim10^4\)–\(10^{23}\) and demands \(f_{\rm PBH}\gtrsim95\), exceeding the total DM density.

Why it matters: the useful null of the day. The broad recoil spectrum inherent to elastic scattering is fundamentally incompatible with an isolated high-energy feature, reinforcing that the LZ event demands an inelastic/threshold mechanism (higgsino, exothermic DM). Caveat: the analysis is deliberately restricted to elastic channels — inelastic neutrino processes and atmospheric-neutrino upscattering (their ref. [29]) are outside scope.

arXiv:2609.08712 §

From LUX-ZEPLIN to Colliders: Probing Higgsino Dark Matter

Kingman Cheung et al.

Taking the 248 keV LZ recoil at face value as 1.1 TeV thermal Higgsino inelastic DM with neutral splitting \(\delta m_0\simeq350\) keV, Cheung, Kang, and Kumar work out the necessary collider corollary: the charged Higgsino sits \(\Delta m_+\sim(330\)–\(350)\) MeV above the neutral state (electroweak loops dominating over a \(\sim-9\) MeV finite-gaugino tree contribution), giving proper lifetime \(\tau\approx0.025\) ns, \(c\tau\simeq(6\)–\(8)\) mm, and decays to a soft \(\sim300\) MeV pion. This is a sub-centimeter disappearing tracklet. Current ATLAS disappearing-track (\(m_{\chi^\pm}\lesssim225\) GeV) and CMS soft-track (\(\lesssim185\) GeV) limits sit far below the 1.1 TeV target because the survival probability through three pixel hits (\(R\sim9\) cm) is \(\sim3\times10^{-6}\) at \(\beta_T\gamma=1\); the 0.57 fb production cross section compounds the problem. HL-LHC needs better short-track reconstruction, not luminosity; dedicated studies find \(5\sigma\) reach at a 100 TeV collider (\(30\) ab\(^{-1}\)) and a 10 TeV muon collider (\(10\) ab\(^{-1}\)).

Why it matters: this is the digest’s cleanest LZ-to-collider bridge, converting a direct-detection splitting into a one-parameter trajectory in the \((m_{\chi^\pm},c\tau)\) plane — a genuinely falsifiable prediction rather than another fit. Caveats are stated honestly: the authors flag the unresolved high-energy \(S1c\) sideband tension (acceptance not public), note the charged splitting is not a precision pole-mass calculation, and stress the collider reaches are conditional projections tied to assumed detector geometry and beam-induced backgrounds.

arXiv:2609.09107 §

For Whom the Xenon Recoils: Magnetic Inelastic Dark Baryons

Pouya Asadi et al.

Asadi et al. build a strongly-coupled dark-baryon model where a transition magnetic dipole operator (\(g_M\simeq-2\sqrt3\), roughly twice the neutron moment) drives inelastic scattering while an accidental “\(\mathcal{H}\)-parity” forbids all diagonal one-photon moments, suppressing elastic scattering. In the benchmark \(N_c=N_f=3\) theory with dark quarks in an SU(2)\(_L\) triplet, the neutral 3-plet/5-plet baryons carry opposite \(\mathcal{H}\)-parity; hyperfine and \(W\)-exchange contributions to their splitting produce \(\delta\sim\mathcal{O}(100)\) keV near dark-quark mass \(\sim800\) GeV (\(m_\chi\sim2.4\) TeV). Fitting the LZ event (spin-dependent nuclear response) gives best-fit \((m_\chi,\delta)=(6.8\text{ TeV},300\text{ keV})\) for SHM and \((14\text{ TeV},280\text{ keV})\) for a MW+LMC profile, with viable ranges \(1\)–\(50\) TeV and \(100\)–\(500\) keV.

The distinctive handle: the excited state de-excites via a photon of energy \(\delta\), with decay length \(\sim4.7\) m — comparable to detector size — so a fraction of recoils carry a coincident \(100\)–\(500\) keV photon, offering directional information (the excited state travels with the heavy DM), a discriminant no other iDM reading provides. It also predicts weaker solar-capture constraints (spin-dependent capture on rare odd-mass isotopes; annihilation dominantly to dark mesons rather than neutrinos), evading the bounds killing the thermal Higgsino. Caveats: mass-splitting predictions carry \(\mathcal{O}(10^7)\) dynamic-range uncertainty from the variational quark-model estimate (only lattice could sharpen them), the single-event fit neglects backgrounds, and halo-profile choice dominates the preferred region.

arXiv:2609.05609 §

The Colors of Jet Quenching

Hannah Bossi et al.

Bossi, Larose, and Mehtar-Tani perform a data-driven extraction of quark and gluon quenching factors by jointly fitting ATLAS inclusive-jet \(R_{AA}\) and CMS energy-energy correlators (EECs) for \(R=0.4\) jets, restricted to the small-angle region (\(R_L<0.07\)) where hard-collinear dynamics dominate and soft medium response is subleading. The two observables have complementary flavor sensitivity — gluon EECs peak at \(R_L=0.025\) versus \(0.012\) for quarks — breaking the identifiability problem that plagues \(R_{AA}\)-only extractions. Under the coherent-energy-loss null hypothesis they extract \(S_q\simeq0.8\), \(S_g\simeq0.3\), incompatible with Casimir scaling (\(S_g=S_q^{9/4}\simeq0.54\)–0.58) at 8–11\(\sigma\). Adding color decoherence via the antenna/dipole quenching factor \(S_{\rm dip}(\theta)\) — which interpolates from coherent (\(\theta\ll\theta_c\)) to independent loss with a slow \(\theta^{-2/3}\) power-law tail — improves the fit (\(\chi^2\)/dof \(0.93\to0.13\)) and restores Casimir scaling (\(S_q\simeq0.65\), \(S_g\simeq0.40\), within \(1.5\sigma\)). The medium resolution angle is tightly constrained: \(\theta_c=0.057\pm0.005\,(\text{fit})^{+0.014}_{-0.008}\,(\text{syst})\), \(p_T\)-independent as expected.

Why it matters: this is direct evidence, drawn from data rather than tuned Monte Carlo, that the QGP partially resolves internal jet substructure down to \(\theta_c\simeq0.05\)–0.07 — the color-decoherence transition made quantitative, paired with Mehtar-Tani’s companion generating-functional derivation. Caveats are stated plainly: absolute \(\chi^2\) values are not meaningful because published uncertainties are added in quadrature without correlations, so only relative fit quality is claimed; it is a proof-of-concept, not a global analysis, with vacuum reference and flavor fractions held fixed and higher-order/medium-response effects deferred.
