arXiv:2609.04376 §

Spin-dependent azimuthal asymmetry of coherent J/ψ photoproduction in hadronic PbPb collisions

CMS Collaboration

What they did: CMS measured the azimuthal distribution of decay muons from coherently photoproduced J/ψ (reconstructed at \(p_T<0.2\) GeV, \(1.6<|y|<2.4\)) relative to the second-order event plane \(\Psi_2\) in 50–80% central PbPb collisions at 5.36 TeV, using 1.26 nb⁻¹. The \(\cos(2\Delta\phi)\) modulation amplitude is \(A_2=-0.194\pm0.022\,\text{(stat)}\pm0.018\,\text{(syst)}\) — negative, and different from zero by more than 5σ, the first such observation for spin-1/2 muons. \(A_2\) shows no significant dependence on rapidity or centrality (7–18% dominant systematic from signal extraction; 3–9% hadronic/incoherent contamination subtracted via a high-\(p_T\) fit extrapolation).

Why it matters: Combined with the positive modulation previously seen for spin-0 pions from ρ⁰ decays, this establishes spin-state dependence of azimuthal asymmetries in coherently photoproduced vector-meson decays — direct confirmation that the J/ψ inherits the incident photon’s linear polarization. Crucially, using \(\Psi_2\) rather than the meson-\(p_T\) interference proxy makes the polarization handle work across broad rapidity and different collision systems, opening a novel reaction-plane estimator that is less sensitive to nonflow and fluctuations, potentially valuable for small systems like pA. Caveat: the Wu et al. model describes the data well, but the saturation-based Shao et al. calculation overshoots the amplitude by roughly a factor of two, so the theory picture is not settled.

arXiv:2609.04673 §

Exothermic and Endothermic Inelastic Dark Matter Interpretations at LZ

James B. Dent et al.

What they did: Dent and Newstead reinterpret LZ’s single ~248 keV nuclear-recoil event (LZ230616, background \(0.0106\pm0.0008\)) as exothermic inelastic DM, where the ambient heavier state \(\chi^*\) down-scatters and dumps its splitting \(|\delta|\) as recoil, producing a peak at \(E_0=\mu_{\chi N}|\delta|/m_N\). An unbinned shape likelihood fixes the peak to the event energy with \(\sigma_p f_*\simeq(0.5\)–\(3)\times10^{-45}\) cm². The empty high-energy sideband (350–680 keV, zero events) disfavors broad heavy-mass peaks — expected sideband yields per observed event are 0.02 (10 GeV), 19 (42 GeV), 73 (100 GeV) — bounding \(m_\chi\lesssim90\) GeV at 95% CL. Excited-state stability then splits solutions into a ~40–90 GeV state with sub-MeV splitting and dark-photon mediator, or a sub-40 GeV state with ~1–10 MeV splitting requiring a leptophobic mediator (to close \(\chi^*\to\chi e^+e^-\)).

Why it matters: Exothermic scattering peaks above the elastic endpoint and is largely halo-tail-independent, sidestepping precisely the high-velocity-tail fragility that undermined the endothermic higgsino readings last week. It predicts ≃4.5 events in LZ’s projected 1000-day (~13 tonne-yr) run, and existing XENONnT/PandaX-4T exposures (1.1 and 0.5 expected events) could already test it in extended windows; the \(\mu_{\chi N}/m_N\) peak scaling gives a distinct argon/germanium signature. Caveat: the sideband recast relies on unpublished efficiency/energy-scale extrapolations beyond calibration, and the Helm form factor gives an \(\mathcal{O}(10^{-4})\) suppression with large uncertainty at this \(q\) — the authors stress this is no substitute for a collaboration analysis.

arXiv:2609.04761 §

Flavor Tomography of Long-Lived Neutrino-Mass Mediators

Renjie Wang

What they did: Wang shows that if the long-lived charged particle producing displaced-lepton signals at the HL-LHC is also the mediator generating Majorana neutrino masses (the “shared-coupling condition”), the Casas–Ibarra parametrization fixes the displaced-lepton flavor ratio \(N_e/N_\mu=\sum_i|U_{ei}|^2 m_i/\sum_i|U_{\mu i}|^2 m_i\) entirely in terms of PMNS data. At NuFit 6.1 best-fit with \(m_\text{lightest}=0\): \(N_e/N_\mu=0.140\) (normal) vs \(2.11\) (inverted), a discrimination ratio \(\mathcal{R}=15.1\). The prediction holds identically across Scotogenic, Type-III seesaw, inverse/linear seesaw, and lepton-portal DM models (given quasi-degenerate mediators and near-real \(R\)). Detector efficiencies cancel in the ratio; \(\delta_{CP}\) dependence is \(<3\%\). An exact Poisson test gives 3σ ordering discrimination with \(\mathcal{O}(25)\) timing-tagged (\(\Delta t>200\) ps) displaced leptons; Scotogenic yields for \(M_\text{LLP}=300\)–600 GeV are \(N_\text{disp}\approx64\)–2856.

Why it matters: This is a \(\delta_{CP}\)-free collider handle on the mass ordering, structurally immune to the degeneracy that limits T2K/NOvA and complementary to JUNO — because the observable depends on \(|U_{\alpha i}|^2\), not \(U_{\alpha i}\). It sharpens exactly where prior two-HNL flavor-counting proposals (capped at ≲90% classification) are broad. It extends the week’s detector-repurposing theme, exploiting the CMS MTD / ATLAS HGTD timing layers already under construction. Caveats: requires the shared-coupling class (real \(R\), degenerate mediators); complex-\(R\) and non-degenerate corrections reach \(\mathcal{O}(10\)–\(30\%)\); the worst-case \(3\sigma\) scan gives \(\mathcal{R}=8.85\), dropping to \(\mathcal{R}\geq2.2\) for larger \(m_\text{lightest}\) within the Planck-allowed range; and it presupposes a positive LLP discovery plus Majorana neutrinos.

Confronting the Higgsino Interpretation of the LZ Event with the High-Energy Sideband

Nicholas L. Rodd et al.

Abstract only. The empty region above the 248 keV event is the discriminant. A thermal 1.1 TeV higgsino tuned to the observed rate and spectrum — splitting δ ~ 370–490 keV depending on the halo model — would, Rodd et al. argue, generically populate a higher-energy sideband along LZ’s S1c axis with additional recoils. LZ reports zero events there, so the higgsino reading sits in mild tension with its own data. arXiv:2609.04181 §

Seasonal dark matter from the LUX-ZEPLIN high-energy event

Christopher McCabe

The high recoil energy forces the inelastic splitting against the kinematic ceiling set by the halo escape speed, and McCabe turns that into a sharp seasonal signature. A shape-only profile likelihood in the (m_χ, δ) plane confines δ to ~370–400 keV at 1σ for m_χ = 1 TeV, with small splittings disfavored because they place the spectrum below 248 keV. At δ = 350 keV the required v_min = 766 km/s sits just 44 km/s below the maximum lab-frame speed (810 km/s), so the entire signal comes from a thin sliver of the halo tail. The consequence: annual modulation amplitude A = 94% at (1 TeV, 350 keV), rising to 100% at 500 GeV — the rate literally vanishes for up to two-thirds of the year. The modulation is nearly pure timing information (97.2% of discriminating power is arrival-time, not energy correlation) and carries mass information even where spectra are degenerate. Reach: 11 events give 3σ on modulation, 31 give 5σ; XLZD at 200 t·yr reaches 7.5σ, and LZ’s own ~15 t·yr already hits 3σ for δ > 374 keV. A secondary handle is isotopic — ¹³⁶Xe supplies 47% of the rate at δ = 380 keV versus 8.9% natural abundance — though enrichment at scale is unrealistic. Honest caveat, stated twice: everything rests on the Standard Halo Model, precisely in the tail region where it is least trustworthy.

arXiv:2609.04144 §

Inelastic Signatures of Electroweak Dark Matter

Juri Smirnov et al.

Smirnov, Griffith, and Beacom show that Higgs-coupled minimal dark matter — a Majorana plus adjacent Dirac multiplet at the custodial point — reproduces the LZ event through a mechanism that is universal across the whole 3_M2_D through 13_M12_D sequence. The neutral Higgs Clebsch is exactly 1/√2 independent of representation, so both the pseudo-Dirac splitting δ ≃ y²v²/4m_χ and the off-diagonal Z coupling (σ_n^inel ≃ 1.9×10⁻³⁹ cm²) are fixed by (m_χ, y) alone. Requiring one expected inelastic event in LZ’s 2.84 t·yr defines a rate-selected δ_LZ(m_χ) ~ 350–380 keV and a universal band in (m_χ, y); intersecting it with each multiplet’s thermal relic trajectory selects a benchmark, from 3_M2_D at 1.44 TeV to 13_M12_D at 131 TeV. The payoff is a correlated, representation-dependent loop-induced elastic signal — from 1.3×10⁻⁴⁷ cm² up to 5×10⁻⁴⁵ cm² — that must lie above the neutrino floor. Existing null elastic results already disfavor 11_M10_D and 13_M12_D, leaving 3_M2_D, 5_M4_D, 7_M6_D as the preferred survivors, all testable by next-generation searches. Caveats are candidly flagged: the rate estimate uses no full detector-response/likelihood reconstruction; the relic calculation is done in the unbroken phase while the signal depends on sub-MeV broken-phase splittings (argued negligible but unchecked); and it remains a single-event excess. The distinctive value is predictivity — inelastic rate, splitting, and elastic channel are locked together rather than fit independently.

arXiv:2609.02823 §

Search for dark matter particle interactions in an extended nuclear recoil energy window with LZ

D. S. Akerib et al.

The now-official source of the week’s theory storm: in a 2.84 tonne-year exposure (220 live days, 4.71 t fiducial), LZ extended its nuclear-recoil window to ~270 keV to probe NREFT and inelastic WIMP models whose spectra populate higher recoils. After selections and salt removal, one event survives in the NR band, consistent with a 248±23(stat)±23(sys) keV elastic recoil (S1\(_c\)=540.1 phd, S2\(_c\)=9268 phd), sitting 1.5σ below the NR median and 6.7σ below the ER median. A profile-likelihood scan over 293 distinguishable models finds a maximum local significance of 3.4σ (typically for masses >200 GeV/c²) and a global 2.6σ after look-elsewhere correction. The upper limits are world-leading on all models; the lower limits lift off zero because of the event. Compatible interpretations include inelastic \(\mathcal{O}_1^s\) (Higgsino-like) with ~300–360 keV splitting and the magnetic-moment \(\mathcal{L}_{10}^s\).

Why it matters: this fixes the kinematics the interpretation papers have been chasing — mass and splitting are tightly constrained, which is exactly why Pospelov et al.’s solar bound can bite. Honest caveats abound in the text: the salt (bias-mitigation) injection failed to cover the high-energy signal region, so this is explicitly a non-blind analysis above 55 keV; the dominant local backgrounds (accidentals, atmospheric-ν CEνNS, MSSI) each carry a 100% MSSI rate uncertainty and rely on absence of a larger low-energy population; and no background could be positively identified as the culprit. One event at 2.6σ remains one event.

arXiv:2609.02775 §

Strong Constraints on Higgsino Dark Matter from Solar Capture

Maxim Pospelov et al.

Pospelov and Ramani turn the Sun into an inelastic-DM accelerator. Infalling quasi-Dirac Higgsinos (\(m_\chi\simeq1.08\) TeV, the thermal-relic mass) reach ~1400 km/s in the solar core — far above the terrestrial ceiling — enabling endothermic \(Z\)-exchange up-scattering off heavy elements (iron through uranium) and capture. Captured DM annihilates to \(W^+W^-\), and IceCube’s 10-year non-observation of solar neutrinos caps the annihilation rate at \(\Gamma_{\rm ann}^{\rm lim}=1.5\times10^{19}\) s⁻¹. Tree-level \(Z\) exchange alone, with up-scattering stalling near \(0.2R_\odot\) on uranium, already excludes \(\delta\lesssim506\) keV; including the robustly predicted loop-level spin-dependent elastic channel on hydrogen thermalizes the captured population and strengthens the bound to \(\delta>566\) keV, moving only ~60 keV across decades of loop cross-section.

Why it matters: the LZ event requires \(\delta\simeq340\)–360 keV, where the annihilation rate would exceed the IceCube limit by \(O(10^3)\). This flatly excludes the endothermic-higgsino reading that yesterday’s cluster (Fan–Reece, Freese, Su et al.) converged on — the exact counter-pressure the digest flagged. The authors note a factor-4 correction to the tree-level cross-section that has propagated (wrongly, for a vector-like Higgsino) in the literature. Caveat: the escape hatch is explicit — dark-photon-mediated inelastic models with sub-GeV \(A'\) produce no prompt solar neutrinos and evade the bound entirely, so the LZ event survives, just not as a Higgsino.

arXiv:2609.02475 §

Stringent limits on CPT- and Lorentz-invariance violation from B_s⁰ decays

LHCb collaboration et al.

LHCb exploits the interference between \(B_s^0\)–\(\bar B_s^0\) mixing and flavor-specific \(B_s^0\to D_s^-\pi^+\) decay in the full Run 2 dataset (6 fb⁻¹, 13 TeV, 378,500 signal candidates, ~84% purity) to search for \(CPT\) violation. The large \(\Delta\Gamma_s\) gives access to \(\mathcal{R}e(z)\) — complementary to \(B^0\) systems, which are sensitive mainly to \(\mathcal{I}m(z)\) — while the ~20× mean boost of \(B_s^0\) mesons in the forward acceptance amplifies any sidereal Lorentz-violating modulation. Results: \(\mathcal{R}e(z)=0.018\pm0.016\pm0.003\) and \(\mathcal{I}m(z)=0.003\pm0.004\pm0.002\), both consistent with \(CPT\) symmetry and the most precise in the \(B_s^0\) system. Within the SME, the Lorentz-violating four-vector reaches \(\Delta a_\parallel=(+0.62\pm0.71\pm0.32)\times10^{-14}\) GeV, with \(\Delta a_X,\Delta a_Y\) at the same \(10^{-14}\) GeV level.

Why it matters: a quiet precision milestone superseding the earlier \(J/\psi\phi\) (3 fb⁻¹) and D0 constraints, probing quark-content dependence of \(CPT\)/Lorentz coefficients through the \(b\bar s\) system. A binned sidereal-phase cross-check gives a 1.8σ preference for oscillation over the constant hypothesis — compatible with a statistical fluctuation, so no evidence for Lorentz violation. Systematics (dominated by fixed \(\Gamma_s\), \(\Delta\Gamma_s\), and decay-time acceptance) are subdominant to the statistical uncertainties throughout.

Wednesday, September 2, 2026

arXiv:2609.01385 §

Constraint on the Higgs boson width in the diphoton final state using signal-background interference in proton-proton collisions at √(s) = 13 TeV

CMS Collaboration

CMS turns the interference between \(gg\to H\to\gamma\gamma\) and the continuum \(gg\to\gamma\gamma\) amplitude into a width measurement, using 138 fb\(^{-1}\) from 2016–2018. The interference distorts the \(m_{\gamma\gamma}\) lineshape and shifts its peak by an amount that grows with \(\Gamma_\mathrm{H}\), so fitting that shift across categories binned in diphoton \(p_\mathrm{T}\) and BDT output constrains the width without ever leaving the on-shell region. The result is \(\Gamma_\mathrm{H}<92\) MeV observed (138 expected) at 95% CL, against a SM prediction of 4.1 MeV and a previous best direct limit of 330 MeV.

Why it matters: the tightest existing number, \(\Gamma_\mathrm{H}=3.0^{+2.0}_{-1.5}\) MeV, comes from comparing on-shell and off-shell \(H\to VV\) rates — which assumes the couplings governing the two regimes are the same, exactly the assumption new physics would break. On-shell rates alone cannot do better on their own, because a width inflated by a factor \(\alpha\) through undetectable decays is degenerate with rescaling every Higgs coupling by \(1/\alpha^{1/4}\). Interference breaks that flat direction using on-shell data only, so the weaker limit is doing more model-independent work than the raw number suggests. Caveats: sensitivity still sits more than an order of magnitude above the SM value, and the dominant experimental systematics are photon energy scale and resolution — which can mimic the very peak shift being measured.

arXiv:2609.01504 §

Higgsino Above the Sea of Fog

JiJi Fan et al.

Fan et al. read LZ’s single \(248\pm23\text{(stat)}\pm23\text{(sys)}\) keV event as inelastic higgsino scattering. Ordinary spin-independent elastic scattering cannot produce it: an event at 250 keV would have been preceded by thousands at lower recoil energy. Scattering between two nearly degenerate neutral states changes the kinematics, and the required minimum velocity — \(v_\mathrm{min}\approx[350\sqrt{E_R/250\,\mathrm{keV}}\,(m_\chi+m_N)/m_\chi + 425\sqrt{250\,\mathrm{keV}/E_R}\,(\delta/350\,\mathrm{keV})]\) km/s — puts the scatterer squarely in the high-speed tail of the local halo. A 1.1 TeV thermal higgsino with \(\delta\approx350\) keV works: under the Standard Halo Model the recoil spectrum starts near 170 keV, peaks around 200 keV, and integrates to 0.3 events in the 215–270 keV signal window.

The theoretical payoff is that the splitting is not a free parameter — it is induced by higgsino–gaugino mixing, so \(\delta\approx350\) keV pushes binos and winos to \(10^3\)–\(10^4\) TeV, and avoiding large logarithmic corrections to the higgsino mass drags the heavy Higgs doublet to the same scale: a heavy but unsplit spectrum, apart from the light higgsino. The caveats are the authors’ own and substantial. The high-velocity tail is highly uncertain; under an LMC-influenced halo with a fatter tail, \(\delta\) must instead exceed 350 keV. They also note the annual modulation peaks near June 2 and the event landed on June 16, 2023 — suggestive, but this is one event.

arXiv:2609.01592 §

Fermionic Dark Matter Absorption and the High-Energy Event in LUX-ZEPLIN

Yuanchao Lou et al.

The same event by a different mechanism, and then a self-inflicted null. Lou et al. propose neutral-current absorption of a fermionic dark matter particle on a xenon nucleus: for \(m_\chi\simeq247\) MeV the coherent process deposits a monoenergetic 248 keV nuclear recoil, matching LZ with no appeal to the velocity tail. But the momentum transfer, \(q\simeq m_\chi\sim p_F^\mathrm{Xe}\simeq262\) MeV, resolves individual nucleons, so an incoherent single-nucleon knockout channel with a broad 200 keV–100.2 MeV spectrum comes along unavoidably. That correlated coherent–incoherent pair is the model’s distinctive signature, and the primary knockouts are argued to fail standard LZ selection cuts, keeping the low-energy spectrum clean. One effective coupling at \(\Lambda\simeq11.5\) TeV, \(\sigma_{\chi N}^\mathrm{NC}\simeq1.07\times10^{-46}\) cm\(^2\), accounts for the single event.

Then they go looking where the incoherent channel would show. Recasting KamLAND’s neutron-emission channel \(\chi+{}^{12}\mathrm{C}\to\nu+n+{}^{11}\mathrm{C}^*\) at \(m_\chi=247.5\) MeV yields 90% CL bounds of \(\Lambda_{90}^V\simeq80\) TeV and \(\Lambda_{90}^S\simeq73\) TeV — both far above the 11.5 TeV the LZ interpretation requires, excluding the benchmark for vector and scalar operators alike. The authors call these bounds preliminary and valid “within the present signal and detector response treatment,” but the gap is wide. Worth reading precisely because it builds the case and then dismantles it, and because it shows large-volume scintillators now constrain xenon-anomaly interpretations.

Tuesday, September 1, 2026

arXiv:2608.29450 §

First Measurement of Solar Neutrinos through Elastic Neutrino-Electron Scattering at the keV Scale

XENON Collaboration et al.

XENONnT reports the first detection of low-energy solar neutrinos via elastic neutrino–electron scattering in a dark-matter TPC, using 2.46 t·y from the SR0 and SR1b science runs over a recoil-energy ROI of [1, 140] keV. An unbinned extended-likelihood fit rejects the background-only hypothesis at 5.0σ and yields a solar \(pp\) flux of \((10.2\pm2.0)\times10^{10}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}s^{-1}}\), with the 1 keV electron-recoil threshold translating to sensitivity down to 17 keV neutrino energy — the lowest neutrino detection threshold on record. Roughly 89% of the ROI signal is \(pp\), ~10% \(^7\)Be, the rest sub-percent. Discovery power comes mainly from [1, 60] keV. The enabling advance is radon reduction: dedicated distillation cut \(^{222}\)Rn to ~0.9 µBq/kg, comparable to the neutrino rate, with the \(^{214}\)Pb/\(^{222}\)Rn activity ratio (\(0.66\pm0.03\)) fixed by a \(^{222}\)Rn calibration.

Why it matters: it cements liquid-xenon TPCs — built for WIMPs — as genuine keV-scale neutrino detectors, complementing the recent \(^8\)B CEνNS observations and pointing toward XLZD sub-percent \(pp\) measurements for electroweak and solar-metallicity tests. Caveats are honest and several: the flux sits 1.9σ above Borexino; the fit relies on the FEA-stepping signal model (the more complete RRPA is only available below 32 keV); an unexplained “tritium-like” excess overlaps the highest-S/B region below 20 keV and is left free; and adding \(^{14}\)C at its maximal conceivable level drops significance to 4.3σ (Borexino consistency to 1.2σ). Total uncertainty is 20%, split evenly between systematic and profile-likelihood.

arXiv:2608.29412 §

Evidence for the dead-cone effect in bottom quark initiated jets in pp collisions at √s = 13 TeV

CMS Collaboration

CMS finds evidence for the dead-cone effect — mass suppression of collinear gluon radiation within \(\Delta R_{\rm dead}=m_q/E_q\) — in \(b\)-jets from dileptonic \(t\bar t\) events, using 59.8 fb\(^{-1}\) at 13 TeV. The emission density \(\rho(\Delta R)\) is measured via iterative Cambridge–Aachen declustering onto the Lund jet plane, in two jet-\(p_T\) bins (40–80, 80–200 GeV). Two novelties sharpen the measurement: a tag-and-probe selection of \(b\)-jets from \(t\bar t\) that avoids flavor-tagging substructure biases (91% \(b\)-quark purity, agreeing with generator-level selection to 2% versus 3–10% for direct DeepJet tagging), and a transformer encoder that partially reconstructs charged \(b\)-hadron decay products (95.6% signal efficiency) to restore the angle-ordered emission history. Compared to a HERWIG7 sample with the \(b\)-mass set to zero in the showering kernel, the data disfavor the massless model at 4.3σ (\(p=8\times10^{-6}\)) in the 40–80 GeV bin, while the nominal massive shower fits well (\(p=0.57\)).

Why it matters: this is a direct imprint of quark mass on the parton shower in \(b\)-jets — following ALICE’s \(D^0\)-tagged first observation and CMS/LHCb charm and beauty measurements — and provides a clean benchmark for logarithmically-accurate shower development and analytic Lund \(b\)-plane calculations. It dovetails with the recent heavy-quark energy-correlator work. Caveats: significance is confined to the lower-\(p_T\) bin; at 80–200 GeV the narrower dead cone leaves no significant discrimination (nominal \(p=0.03\), massless \(p=0.01\)). The massless-kernel comparison also shows hadronization plays a sizable role in the collinear suppression, so the effect is not purely perturbative.

arXiv:2608.29863 §

The inclusive B̅ →X_sγ decay rate with higher precision

M. Misiak et al.

Misiak et al. deliver an upgraded SM prediction \(\mathcal{B}_{s\gamma}^{\rm SM}=(3.54\pm0.14)\times10^{-4}\) for \(E_\gamma>1.6\) GeV, a ~4% total uncertainty. The headline improvement is retiring the long-standing \(m_c\)-interpolation: the dominant \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s^2)\) interference terms \(\hat G_{17}^{(2)}\), \(\hat G_{27}^{(2)}\) are now computed with exact charm-mass dependence via four-loop cut diagrams (companion paper arXiv:2608.29864), enhancing \(\mathcal{B}_{s\gamma}\) by 4.2% relative to the interpolated value — exceeding the previously assigned ±3% interpolation error by 1.4×. The \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s)\) rate is also rendered formally complete by adding four- and five-body \((\bar s b)(\bar q q)\) contributions (~−0.2%), and HQE matrix elements, \(m_b\) and \(|V_{cb}|\) are taken from the latest kinetic-scheme semileptonic fit including \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s^3)\). Including \(\mathcal{O}(\alpha_s^2)\) cuts the \(\mu_b\)-scale variation to ±1.3% over [1.5, 10] GeV.

Why it matters: the prediction sits in perfect agreement with the experimental average \((3.49\pm0.19)\times10^{-4}\), and translates into an absolute (any-\(\tan\beta\)) 2HDM-II charged-Higgs bound \(M_{H^\pm}>670\) GeV at 95% C.L. — stronger than comparable LHC direct searches. A convenient linear formula \(\mathcal{B}_{s\gamma}\times10^4=(3.54\pm0.14)-8.51\,\Delta C_7-2.16\,\Delta C_8\) is provided for generic BSM fits. Against the persistent \(R(D^*)\) tension, the inclusive-radiative sector is now fully SM-consistent. Caveat, flagged by the authors: the exact-\(m_c\) \(\hat G_{17,27}^{(2)}\) still omit the photon-energy cut (expected minor, given endpoint-peaked spectra), and PDG’s \(E_0\) extrapolation factors from experimental cuts down to 1.6 GeV await updating, ideally via SIMBA.

arXiv:2608.28377 §

Search for scalar leptoquarks produced via muon-quark scattering in pp collisions at √s = 13 TeV

CMS Collaboration

The first LHC search exploiting the proton’s charged-lepton content to produce a single TeV-scale scalar leptoquark resonantly through muon–quark fusion, using the full Run 2 dataset (138 fb⁻¹). The lepton comes from a photon fluctuation inside one proton (LUXlep PDFs), fusing with a quark from the other; the signature is a high-pₜ muon plus a light- or b-quark jet, with a soft second muon often lost down the beampipe. The observable is the μ-jet invariant mass, fit for a bump on a smooth background across eight categories (one/two muon × BDT tight/loose × b-tag). Data agree with background; 95% CL limits on σ·B exclude new territory in the (M_LQ, λ) plane: 1.5–3.6 TeV at couplings 0.2–0.6 for LQ(uμ), and M_LQ > 1.8 TeV at λ > 1 for LQ(bμ).

Why it matters: unlike pair production (QCD-driven, nearly λ-independent, giving a vertical exclusion) or nonresonant t-channel, this resonant mode scales as λ² and gains reach precisely where pair production dies from phase space — large λ and high mass. It’s a genuinely new handle motivated by the (now-fading) B-meson flavor anomalies, and complements existing modes rather than merely tightening them. Caveat: limits assume only lepton-induced production, neglecting gluon-induced single production; the authors flag this as conservative (weakening limits by ~10–15%) and verify via injection tests that the bias stays below half a standard deviation. The dominant systematic is the freely-floating background fit, i.e. statistics.

arXiv:2608.27894 §

Observation of the Ξ_c⁰ → pK⁻ decay and measurement of its decay asymmetry

LHCb collaboration et al.

First observation of the singly Cabibbo-suppressed, purely W-exchange decay Ξ_c⁰ → pK⁻, reconstructed through the clean cascade Ξ_b⁻ → Ξ_c⁰(→pK⁻)π⁻ in 5.4 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV pp data. A signal yield of 134±14 is extracted from a simultaneous fit with the normalization channel Ξ_c⁰ → pK⁻K⁻π⁺ (6930±90 events), giving a relative rate R = (9.26±0.94±0.43)×10⁻³ and, using the known normalization branching fraction, B(Ξ_c⁰ → pK⁻) = (4.5±0.5±0.2±0.9)×10⁻⁵. Exploiting the cascade, an angular fit of the Ξ_c⁰ helicity angle (assuming α_{Ξ_b} = −1, validated by the analogous Λ_b measurement) yields the decay asymmetry α = 0.32±0.15±0.01, compatible with zero.

Why it matters: pure W-exchange charm-baryon decays are notoriously beyond reliable computation, and BESIII’s recent Λ_c⁺ → Ξ⁰K⁺ analysis already showed deviations from theory. This measurement supplies both a branching fraction and an asymmetry for a channel never before seen. The measured B sits significantly below most predictions (IRA/TDA ≈ 3.1×10⁻⁴, i.e. the observed value is far lower once the normalization uncertainty is accounted for), and the asymmetry sharply discriminates models — consistent with IRA/TDA/SU(3) predictions near zero but excluding current-algebra (α = 0.99) and one IRA (−0.81) result. The asymmetry consistent with zero indicates negligible S–P interference. Caveats: the dominant uncertainty is the 20% normalization branching fraction; the α measurement is statistics-limited and assumes unpolarized Ξ_b⁻ (a 5% polarization systematic is assigned). CP-violation sensitivity awaits the upgraded-LHCb dataset.

arXiv:2608.27553 §

Imperfect axions with no domain wall problem

Marco Gorghetto et al.

A new resolution of the domain-wall problem for post-inflationary axions with domain-wall number N > 1. The standard cure — a small PQ-violating operator that biases the N degenerate QCD minima — is tightly squeezed between the nEDM bound (θ_eff ≲ 10⁻¹⁰) and dark-matter overproduction from late wall decay. The authors point out that because the QCD axion mass is strongly temperature-suppressed (m_a²(T) ∝ T⁻⁸), a temperature-independent PQ-violating potential can dominate before the QCD potential switches on at T⋆. In that regime the PQ term (which has trivial wall number) forms unstable walls at H = m_PQ that destroy the string network before the stable N > 1 QCD walls ever form. Analytics plus N = 2 lattice simulations (3072³, “fat-string” system) confirm the network decays for m_PQ/m_a(T⋆) ≳ 0.32; accounting for the logarithmic string-tension enhancement extrapolated to the physical log ≈ 60 raises this critical value to ≈ 1.8.

Why it matters: this opens viable N > 1 post-inflationary parameter space (including DFSZ-motivated N = 6) requiring f_a ≲ (2/N)·5×10⁹ GeV, i.e. m_a ≳ (N/2) meV, in the 10⁻³–10⁻² eV window. Crucially, the needed PQ violation predicts an nEDM within two orders of magnitude of current limits — squarely in reach of the planned factor-100 improvement, tying the domain-wall solution to a testable signal. Caveats the authors stress: simulations reach only log ≈ 4.5–6 versus the physical ~60, forcing extrapolation of c_PQ; and the large relativistic-axion energy density radiated during scaling may obstruct wall formation entirely (App. C), which would shrink the early-decay region below the purple-dashed line. Dedicated simulations near the physical point are needed.

Friday, August 28, 2026

arXiv:2608.23699 §

Deuterium Production in an Effective Field Theory Constructed from On-Shell Amplitudes

Tim M. P. Tait

Tait computes \(n+p\to d\gamma\) in a pionless EFT whose degrees of freedom are the nuclear states themselves: the amplitude is glued from on-shell three-point vertices (the \(d\)–\(n\)–\(p\) vertex normalized to the measured ANC \(A_S=0.8846\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1/2}\)), with \(S_0^1\) rescattering resummed dispersively, leaving just two short-distance constants — an isovector magnetic (\(c_{M1}\)) and an electric dipole (\(c_{E1}\)) contact. A joint Bayesian fit to two thermal-capture measurements and the 22-point SLEGS photodisintegration set returns \(c_{M1}=+0.162\pm0.015\) and \(c_{E1}=-0.88\pm0.21\), both of natural size, with a comfortable fit (\(\chi^2=20.3\) for 22 dof). The thermonuclear rate is pinned to \(0.22\)–\(0.24\%\) across \(T_9=0.01\)–\(3\), rising to \(0.25\)–\(0.27\%\) once truncation (bounded at \(0.12\%\)) is folded in. Propagated through PRyMordial, this shifts predicted primordial D/H by \(-0.06\%\) and cuts this reaction’s D/H uncertainty contribution from \(0.089\%\) to \(0.050\%\) — effectively retiring \(np\to d\gamma\) from the error budget, where the \(d+d\) burning reactions now dominate.

Why it matters: it delivers the sub-0.3% production anchor needed to keep pace with improving burning-rate data, and demonstrates the on-shell-amplitude program (previously applied to \(d(p,\gamma)^3\)He) generalizes to a second capture, with the shared \(d\)–\(n\)–\(p\) vertex promising correlated control over all four primary reactions. Honest caveats: the fitted electric contact pulls \(3.5\sigma\) against Rupak’s pre-2000 determination (\(L_{E1}=+14.9\) vs \(-5.3\,\mathrm{fm}^3\)), which Tait attributes to a genuine data–data tension between the near-threshold older set and SLEGS’s higher reach rather than a calculational disagreement. The two thermal measurements themselves differ by \(1.9\sigma\), requiring PDG error scaling. Notably, the paper states all analytic and numerical work was primarily derived using LLMs (Claude, GPT-5.6), human-validated.

arXiv:2608.26935 §

Search for high-mass resonances in photon-jet final states using 140 fb⁻¹ at √s = 13 TeV with ATLAS

ATLAS Collaboration

ATLAS scans the \(\gamma\)–jet invariant-mass spectrum over the full Run 2 dataset for localized excesses, finding none: BumpHunter yields no significant deviation across four regions (inclusive SR plus flavor-tagged SRL/SRC/SRB, the latter using the GN2 transformer tagger). The key advance over the 36.7 fb⁻¹ predecessor is heavy-flavor tagging of the leading jet, enabling separate excited-quark interpretations. For coupling \(f=1\), masses are excluded up to 6.0 TeV (\(q_L^*\)), 3.5 TeV (\(c^*\), via a simultaneous SRC+SRB+SRL fit), and 2.6 TeV (\(b^*\)) — the \(c^*\) result being the first direct constraint in this channel at the LHC. Quantum-black-hole thresholds are excluded to 5.3 TeV (Randall-Sundrum, \(n=1\)) and 7.5 TeV (ADD, \(n=6\)). Model-independent Gaussian-resonance limits are provided for widths 2%/7%/15%.

Why it matters: it is today’s one full-dataset collaboration result, extending compositeness and extra-dimension reach and closing the \(c^*\) gap in \(\gamma\)–jet. Backgrounds are modeled analytically with smoothly-falling functions, validated via spurious-signal tests (bias required \(<50\%\) of statistical uncertainty). Caveat: the ADD QBH exclusion (7.5 TeV) is actually slightly weaker than the previous 7.1 TeV in absolute terms only if misread — here it improves — but note downward data fluctuations at high mass, and minor \(\sim2\sigma\) local excesses near 1.8 and 2.6 TeV that stay within the expected-limit band. No theoretical uncertainties are assigned to the QBH benchmarks, following prior convention.

arXiv:2608.26376 §

Efficient binned profile likelihood minimization for precision measurements with RABBIT

David Walter et al.

Walter and Long present RABBIT, a TensorFlow-2 framework for binned profile-likelihood fits that uses automatic differentiation for exact gradients and Hessian-vector products, feeding a Krylov-subspace trust-region minimizer that solves the trust-region subproblem from HVPs rather than full Hessians. JIT compilation gives near-C++ speed with automatic CPU/GPU dispatch. The statistical model supports Poisson or \(\chi^2\) observation terms, log-normal or additive systematics, and Barlow–Beeston (full or lite) statistical uncertainties profiled analytically where possible; when all systematics are symmetrized and additive with a \(\chi^2\) model, the likelihood is purely quadratic and the MLE follows deterministically in one step. Physical observables (including unfolded cross sections) are obtained via differentiable “mappings” and “masked channels” with full post-fit uncertainty propagation.

Why it matters: this is the tooling-wave companion the digest flags — precision measurements already hit 3000+ bins and 4000+ nuisance parameters (CMS W-mass), and HL-LHC will push further. Benchmarks on synthetic models show decisive scaling advantages: at 1000 bins / 1024 systematics RABBIT finishes under 10 s where Combine and pyhf exceed the 10-minute limit, and at 100,000 bins only RABBIT succeeds. It was validated against Combine (NLL values and scans agree exactly up to constants) and already used in the CMS W-mass result. Honest caveats: GPU runs are memory-limited (largest fit had 2048 systematics on a V100); the tool complements rather than replaces established frameworks, and for simple models Combine remains fastest.

arXiv:2608.25476 §

Measurement of tZq inclusive and differential cross-sections in pp collisions at √(s)=13 TeV with ATLAS

ATLAS Collaboration

Abstract only. Working in the trilepton channel with the full 140 fb\(^{-1}\) Run 2 dataset (three light leptons, 2–4 jets, 1–2 \(b\)-tags), ATLAS measures the \(pp\to t\ell^+\ell^-q\) cross-section for \(m_{\ell\ell}>30\) GeV — including resonant and non-resonant dilepton production — as \(95.2\,^{+8.5}_{-7.9}\text{(stat.)}\,^{+5.5}_{-5.3}\text{(syst.)}\) fb, consistent with the most advanced SM predictions. Separate top-quark and top-antiquark cross-sections and their ratio are extracted, and absolute and normalised differential spectra are unfolded at both particle and parton level across many kinematic variables. The top-quark spin asymmetry, from the unfolded polarisation angle, is \(A=0.34\pm0.14\text{(stat.)}\pm0.06\text{(syst.)}\), again SM-consistent. The differential results are interpreted in SMEFT, setting limits on dimension-6 operators involving the top quark.

Why it matters: This is the day’s one full-dataset collaboration result and extends the “EFT-as-default” reading of top-sector data — every observable is SM-consistent, so the payoff is the constraint on anomalous top couplings via the spin asymmetry and differential shapes rather than any deviation. The caveat is inherent: with everything SM-compatible the sensitivity is entirely in the operator limits, and the polarisation measurement remains statistics-limited (statistical uncertainty on \(A\) more than double the systematic).

arXiv:2608.25705 §

Impact of model-independent higher-order contributions to the Higgs-boson self-couplings

Johannes Braathen et al.

Braathen, Naskar and Weiglein identify a closed, model-independent class of higher-order contributions to \(\lambda_{hhh}\) and \(\lambda_{hhhh}\): SM-like diagrams containing only the observed Higgs \(h\) in the loop but with vertices dressed by loop-corrected coupling modifiers \(\bar\kappa_\lambda\), \(\bar\kappa_4\). Using an effective-potential setup inspired by leading-order HEFT, they compute these up to two loops in an on-shell tadpole/mass scheme (cross-checked against a FeynArts/FormCalc one-loop diagrammatic calculation), yielding analytic \(\kappa_\lambda(\bar\kappa_\lambda,\bar\kappa_4)\) and \(\kappa_4(\bar\kappa_\lambda,\bar\kappa_4)\). Confronting these with the current experimental \(\kappa_\lambda\in[-0.71,6.1]\), perturbative unitarity, and \(\kappa_4\) bounds turns the one-dimensional interval into a non-trivial two-dimensional allowed region. For \(\bar\kappa_4=1\) the inferred upper bound tightens from the experimental 6.1 to \(\bar\kappa_\lambda<4.9\); blind directions open up at large positive \(\bar\kappa_4\) (reaching \(\bar\kappa_\lambda\gtrsim8\) before unitarity caps it near 7). A public code, check_kappas, implements the mapping.

Why it matters: This is a genuine correction to how self-coupling limits should be translated into BSM parameter space, continuing the 08-19 self-coupling thread. The effect is largest precisely where current bounds sit — far from the SM — and would shrink if future measurements land near \(\kappa_\lambda\simeq1\). Honest caveats: the results carry residual \(\overline{\text{MS}}\) renormalisation-scale dependence (bounds shift noticeably between \(Q=m_h\) and \(Q=v\), especially at large \(|\bar\kappa_4|\)), and the extracted \(\bar\kappa\)’s are themselves scale-dependent quantities requiring careful matching to a UV model.

arXiv:2608.25963 §

A High Performance Partial Wave Analysis Framework for Hadron Spectroscopy

Benhou Xiang et al.

Xiang, Fang and Liu present CTPWA, a covariant-tensor PWA framework that exploits the factorisation of the amplitude: kinematic vertex tensors depend only on event four-momenta and decay topology, not fit parameters, so they are precomputed once and cached in GPU memory. The intensity becomes a per-event quadratic form \(I=\boldsymbol\theta_a^\dagger\mathbf{B}\boldsymbol\theta_a\), letting the negative log-likelihood, its analytic gradient, and full analytic Hessian be evaluated as batched dense linear algebra entirely on-device, with L-BFGS minimisation also on the GPU — eliminating graph re-tracing, host-device transfers, and per-iteration allocation. Benchmarks (10 channels, 3 polarisations) show per-iteration cost flat at 0.4–2.8 ms versus linear growth to ~400 ms for conventional pipelines; at \(10^6\) events NLL+gradient takes ~2 ms (vs 23 ms GPU autograd, 2.3 s CPU autograd) and the analytic Hessian ~6 ms (vs 0.75 s and 75 s), while conventional pipelines exhaust device memory. A realistic \(J/\psi\to K^+K^-\eta\) fit with 16 resonances, 33 couplings and \(10^6\) events converges in ~7 s (fixed resonance parameters) or ~100 s (floating), after a one-time ~94 s precompute.

Why it matters: This is exactly the infrastructure high-statistics BESIII/LHCb analyses need, part of the day’s amplitude-analysis upgrade cycle alongside the neural \(\pi\pi\) work. The analytic Hessian makes uncertainty estimation practical at high statistics. Caveat: gains depend on the likelihood being quadratic in couplings; floating resonance parameters break the phase-space pre-averaging and raise per-iteration cost roughly sevenfold, and memory reaches ~32 GB in that mode.

arXiv:2608.23685 §

Direct lattice QCD calculation of the θ-induced CP-violating pion-nucleon coupling

Chuan-Yang Li et al.

The χQCD collaboration reports the first direct lattice evaluation of the θ-induced CP-odd pion-nucleon coupling, extracting the forward proton matrix element of the isovector pseudoscalar density weighted by the topological charge to first order in \(\bar\theta\). Working with overlap valence fermions on three RBC/UKQCD \(2+1\)-flavor domain-wall ensembles at a single spacing (\(a=0.1105\) fm), they use the index-theorem-consistent overlap definition of \(Q\) and cluster-decomposition error reduction (CDER) to tame the volume-enhanced noise, while explicitly including the \(\bar\theta\)-induced parity mixing of the external nucleon (the \(2\alpha_1 g_P\) term). A key simplification over their earlier nEDM work: the ratio at \(q^2=0\) directly isolates the full physical \(\tilde g_P\) with no zero-recoil extrapolation or separate \(\alpha_1\)/\(g_P\) calculation, and \(Z_mZ_P=1\) for overlap removes renormalization. An AIC-weighted average over 7 partially-quenched extrapolation forms yields \(\tilde g_{\pi NN}=0.0306(56)(124)\,\bar\theta\); the full-volume cross-check gives \(0.0305(87)(140)\,\bar\theta\).

This supplies the hadronic-coupling rung above the nEDM, complementing yesterday’s Blum et al. extraction and testing the chiral matching relations that underpin EDM interpretations of light nuclei and diamagnetic atoms. The result is consistent with the indirect \(n\)–\(p\) mass-splitting estimate \(0.0155(20)(16)\,\bar\theta\), though the central value sits somewhat higher. The honest caveats are stark: the dominant second error is the extrapolation-form spread from heavy sea pions (no dedicated partially-quenched χPT expression exists), and the single lattice spacing means discretization effects are not quantified. Lighter-pion ensembles are underway.

arXiv:2608.24332 §

Exclusive Determination of |V_cb| from Semileptonic Decays B→D⁽*)ℓν_ℓ

Xue-Wen Chen et al.

Chen et al. perform a frequentist BGL global fit combining the latest Belle II \(B\to D\ell\nu\) and \(B\to D^*\ell\nu\) spectra, updated lattice form factors (FNAL/MILC, HPQCD, JLQCD), and correlated large-recoil SCET/LCSR predictions with NLO and next-to-leading-power corrections, plus strong unitarity bounds from four heavy-to-heavy channels with HQET power corrections to \(\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_c^2)\). Three nested scenarios show LCSR constraints dramatically shrink high-order form-factor uncertainties. The full global fit yields \(|V_{cb}|=(39.18\pm0.47)\times10^{-3}\), with the SM predictions \(R(D)=0.3069\pm0.0080\) and \(R(D^*)=0.2548\pm0.0043\) from the lattice\(\oplus\)LCSR theory fit. The correlated 68% theory ellipse in the \((R(D),R(D^*))\) plane shows “only modest overlap” with individual experimental measurements and a substantial deviation from the experimental average.

The takeaway for today’s flavor theme: even a state-of-the-art SM benchmark leaves the LFU tension intact, against a month where flavor has otherwise consolidated on nulls. The paper is candid about internal frictions — visible tension between the HPQCD \(B\to D^*\) dataset and other lattice results, and mild lattice-vs-LCSR discord, though points overlap within errors. Their \(|V_{cb}|\) sits \(1.7\times10^{-3}\) below a recent Bayesian angular analysis, attributed to methodological choices (correlated LCSR inputs, dropping electron-mode data with known \(e\)/\(\mu\) tension, frequentist vs. MCMC). Local angular deviations from Belle II exist but carry no significance. Whether the residual \(R(D^{(*)})\) gap is theory systematics or new physics remains open pending better lattice/LCSR control and Belle II statistics.

arXiv:2608.23750 §

S-matrix informed neural networks for amplitude analysis

Wyatt A. Smith et al.

The Joint Physics Analysis Center introduces SINNs — neural representations of partial-wave amplitudes on the real axis that enforce S-matrix principles by construction. A shared trunk feeds channel-specific heads, with threshold barrier factors (\(q^{2\ell+1}\)) and the inelasticity map imposed algebraically in postprocessing (guaranteeing \(0\le\eta\le1\) and elastic unitarity below thresholds), while analyticity and crossing enter numerically through a twice-subtracted Roy-equation loss evaluated over \(2m_\pi<\sqrt{s}<1.1\) GeV. Training uses a six-phase curriculum with UPGrad multi-objective gradient handling to prevent the dispersive integrals from collapsing the fit to trivial solutions. Applied to \(\pi\pi\) scattering, the framework recovers the expected \(\sigma/f_0(500)\), \(\rho(770)\), and \(f_0(980)\) features and yields scattering lengths \(a_0^0=0.222^{+0.014}_{-0.014}\), \(a_0^2=-0.041^{+0.005}_{-0.006}\,m_\pi^{-1}\), consistent with Colangelo et al. and García-Martín et al. (with honestly larger bands, reflecting genuine amplitude-space sampling).

The methodological headline is “spectral response clustering”: each experiment is upweighted inside constrained ensembles, a symmetrized \(\chi^2\)-response matrix is spectrally embedded and Ward-clustered, and groups in tension are pruned automatically — replacing heuristic data selection. Two rounds cut the historically inconsistent \(\pi\pi\) database to 18 experiments/621 points, dropping the deep-dip Protopopescu and several Grayer/Hyams solutions, lowering median loss from 7.2 to 3.1. Robustness is well-documented: a 100-architecture sweep shifts no central value by more than \(0.42\delta x\); closure tests recover a known CFD amplitude (\(|z|<0.4\)); and an ablation shows removing Roy constraints leaves data description untouched but degrades scattering lengths outside the universal band. Caveats acknowledged: the selected dataset implicitly depends on the selection architecture, unknown experimental systematics likely dominate, and the \(K\bar K\) cusp in the \(S_0\) phase is deliberately not imposed. The workflow is presented as transferable to \(\pi K\), \(\pi N\), and beyond.

arXiv:2608.23009 §

Search for the lepton-flavor-violating decay τ→μγ at Belle II

Belle II Collaboration et al.

Belle II sets \(\mathcal{B}(\tau^\pm\to\mu^\pm\gamma)<9.5\,(12.2)\times10^{-8}\) at 90% (95%) CL on 428 fb⁻¹ (393×10⁶ \(\tau\tau\) pairs), from an unbinned 2D extended maximum-likelihood fit in \(M_{\rm bc}\) and \(\Delta E/\sqrt{s}\) after a GBDT selection (final signal efficiency 5.97%, τ⁺τ⁻ rejection 99.8%). Eight events are observed in the signal region against a sideband-extrapolated background of \(5.7\pm1.2\); the fitted branching fraction \((2.8^{+4.4}_{-2.6})\times10^{-8}\) is consistent with zero. The observed limit is slightly weaker than the expected \(7.6\,(9.2)\times10^{-8}\), attributed to a statistical upward fluctuation. Systematics are dominated by the 5.7% GBDT data/MC efficiency uncertainty (total 6.0% on the efficiency×luminosity×cross-section product); the dominant physical background is \(\tau\to\mu\nu\bar\nu\) plus an ISR/beam photon.

Why it matters: this is the flagship charged-LFV channel, where SM rates are \(\sim10^{-54}\) and many BSM scenarios (SUSY, seesaw, Littlest Higgs) predict \(10^{-10}\)–\(10^{-8}\). The result does not yet beat Belle’s \(4.2\times10^{-8}\) (988 fb⁻¹) or BaBar’s \(4.4\times10^{-8}\), but on less than half the luminosity it achieves 41% higher signal efficiency and 81% lower background than the Belle analysis, and introduces a unified likelihood with profiled nuisance parameters — establishing the machinery for the far larger datasets to come. The honest caveat is scale: 428 fb⁻¹ is a fraction of Belle II’s design target, so this is a foundation-laying benchmark rather than a world-best number.

arXiv:2608.22587 §

Calculation of neutron electric dipole moment from Lattice QCD

Thomas Blum et al.

Blum et al. extract the \(\bar\theta\)-induced nEDM via a new Feynman–Hellmann route: rather than the noisy forward limit of the \(F_3\) form factor, they compute the matrix element of the local (single-time-slice) topological charge density between nucleon ground states spatially polarized by a uniform background electric field. Because the dipole interaction is anti-Hermitian, these states have mixed parity and are constructed variationally through a non-Hermitian GEVP over a four-operator basis. On three 2+1-flavor domain-wall ensembles (\(a\approx0.11\) fm, \(m_\pi=340,420,576\) MeV, electro-quenched), they obtain a statistically significant signal and extrapolate to \(d_n=-0.0050(4)(8)\,\bar\theta\) e·fm. Comparison with the experimental bound gives \(|\bar\theta|\lesssim10^{-11}\), confirming the strong-CP problem.

Why it matters: previous \(F_3\)-based calculations at the physical point (Alexandrou et al., Bhattacharya et al.) yield no significant signal because the global topological-charge summation is too noisy; truncated-sum methods (Dragos et al., Liang et al.) introduce their own systematics. Sampling the topological charge locally sidesteps both global summation and the \(Q^2\to0\) extrapolation, and the authors show the conventional positive-parity projection suffers large excited-state contamination that the GEVP removes — plausibly explaining a real discrepancy with the lighter-mass points of Liang et al. The method extends directly to Weinberg three-gluon and four-quark CP-odd operators. Caveats are explicit: single lattice spacing, electro-quenched approximation, and a physical-point extrapolation anchored at \(m_\pi=340\) MeV — finite-volume and discretization effects remain to be quantified.

arXiv:2608.21510 §

Electroweak and Single Top-Quark Conspiracy in Current LHC Data

Christoph Englert et al.

Englert, Kogler, and Spannowsky ask whether recent above-SM central values in \(tWZ\) (\(\mu=1.77\pm0.32\)) and \(tq\gamma\) (\(1.34^{+0.19}_{-0.17}\)), plus a large ATLAS \(tHq\) (\(7.2^{+4.6}_{-4.0}\)) alongside below-SM \(t\bar tH\) (\(0.59^{+0.22}_{-0.20}\)), form a coherent SMEFT pattern while VBS/triboson channels stay SM-like. A linearized (\(\mathcal{O}(\Lambda^{-2})\)) five-coefficient Warsaw-basis fit to eight LHC rates plus five \(Z\)-pole observables gives \(\chi^2_{\min}=11.2\) (8 d.o.f., \(p=0.19\)), improving on the SM by \(\Delta\chi^2=13.4\) (local \(\sim2.1\sigma\)). The largest pulls are a triple-gauge \(C_W\) (\(\sim1.9\sigma\)) and the weak top dipole \(C_{tW}\) (\(\sim1.7\sigma\)), with positive \(C_{Hq}^{(3)}\); \(C_{HW}\) is poorly constrained. Fixing \(C_W=0\) still fits (\(\chi^2=14.9\), 9 d.o.f.). Notably, the fit absorbs the \(A_{\rm FB}^{0,b}\) anomaly — but only by shifting \(A_e\) ~4σ below LEP/SLD, a subset artifact flagged by the authors.

Why it matters: this is the EFT-as-diagnostic thread — using SMEFT not for limits but to test UV consistency. Under weakly-coupled renormalizable matching, no single scalar, vector, or vector-like-quark multiplet reproduces the pattern: currents need a charge-2/3 singlet, the dipole’s opposite chirality needs a hypercharge-1/6 doublet, and positive \(C_{Hq}^{(3)}\) demands triplet custodial partners protecting \(Zb_L\bar b_L\). A benchmark multi-threshold VLQ sector (1.6–2.0 TeV) reproduces the current alignment with \(\kappa_t\simeq0.91\), but the fit-preferred dipole must come from unspecified loop dynamics. Caveats: linearized truncation with no EFT uncertainty, no experimental covariances, and a preselected measurement set — the “\(2\sigma\)” is explicitly local and not a global SM improvement.

arXiv:2608.20464 §

First Search for Ultraheavy Dark Matter Using a Magnetically Levitated Particle

Dennis G. Uitenbroek et al.

(abstract only) POLONAISE uses a milligram-scale ferromagnet levitated in a superconducting trap as an impulse sensor, reaching a force sensitivity of 0.07 fN Hz⁻¹ᐟ² and resolving momentum kicks as small as 1 TeV/c. Treating every candidate impulse as a possible DM scatter, the collaboration sets optimum-interval upper limits on the neutron coupling for DM interacting via a light mediator: for masses 10⁶–10¹⁵ GeV/c² and mediators below 30 meV/c², they exclude couplings down to α_n = 3.2×10⁻⁹ at 95% CL, giving leading constraints on the DM–neutron cross section for composite DM.

The headline is the apparatus, not a signal. Magnetic levitation extends the mass reach of levitated-sensor DM searches seven orders of magnitude beyond optical levitation, opening the ultraheavy frontier where composite/dark-nugget candidates deposit resolvable single-scatter impulses. This is the mechanical-sensor counterpart to the day’s other hidden-sector hardware routes (qubit sensing, the HFIR mirror-neutron beam). With only the abstract available, the exposure, background model, and impulse-candidate treatment can’t be assessed here — but the quoted sensitivity and mass span are the concrete takeaways.

arXiv:2608.21082 §

Angular analysis of the decay Λ_b⁰ → Λ(1520)μ⁺μ⁻

LHCb collaboration et al.

First angular analysis of this baryonic b→sℓℓ mode, using LHCb’s full Run 1+2 9 fb⁻¹ sample. The leptonic forward-backward asymmetry A^ℓ_FB,3/2 and the CP-averaged S_1cc are extracted in four narrow q² bins (0.1–12.5 GeV²/c⁴) plus the wide [1.1,6.0] bin, with the Λ(1520) reconstructed in pK⁻ and spin-1/2 Λ* contributions plus their interferences modelled explicitly. All measured values agree with SM predictions built on lattice-QCD and dispersive form factors — e.g. A^ℓ_FB,3/2 = 0.16±0.15±0.06 and S_1cc = 0.19±0.30±0.18 in [1.1,6.0]. The single mild outlier is S_1cc = 1.35±0.54±0.17 in [6.0,8.0], but statistics dominate throughout.

This is the baryonic complement to the mesonic b→sℓℓ program, probing the same FCNC transition with different hadronic structure and spin content — a useful cross-check as the mesonic picture has consolidated rather than sharpened since the mid-month C₉ discussion. Caveats are candid: the measurement is statistics-limited, the fit sits near physical boundaries (biases up to ~18% of the statistical error, corrected via pseudoexperiments), and systematics are dominated by combinatorial and Λ_c⁺-feeddown backgrounds. Discriminating among form-factor predictions awaits larger samples.

arXiv:2608.21120 §

New Limits on n→n′ Transformation from HFIR Cold Neutron Beam

James M. Rogers et al.

A neutron-regeneration search at HFIR’s CG-2/GP-SANS cold beam pushes the mirror-neutron transformation limit an order of magnitude below the prior SNS results. A 3.5 mm cadmium wafer at the center of a 6.6 T split-pair magnet absorbs all beam neutrons (to <10⁻²⁰); any n→n′ conversion in the first magnet half lets sterile n′ pass the cadmium and regenerate to detectable neutrons in the second half, caught by the low-background ³He detector 20 m downstream. No signal appears above background: 95% CL regeneration-probability limits reach ~1.25×10⁻¹¹ per neutron (B=0) up to a few×10⁻¹¹ at nonzero field, translating to an order-of-magnitude improvement in 2θ₀² across Δm = 0.1–1000 neV.

The gains come from HFIR’s ~400× higher average intensity, colder/wider spectrum, and the facility ³He detector. This is the same mirror-neutron channel flagged on 08-13, and one of four hidden-sector hardware routes in the day’s digest. Honest caveats: the 2021 run (~40 h) suffered detector activation of ⁵⁵Mn→⁵⁶Mn, raising and time-varying the background, so it carries reduced weight; the 2024 run (~90 h) is cleaner but background still crept up ~8% over the run. Beam-intensity normalization carries ~4–6% systematics reconciled across multiple monitors. Further reach demands higher intensity, longer beam time, and hard background reduction.

arXiv:2608.19546 §

Observation of an excess at the top quark pair production threshold in the single-lepton channel

CMS Collaboration

(Abstract only available.) CMS reports a \(6.1\sigma\) excess near the \(t\bar t\) production threshold in single-lepton (\(e/\mu\) + jets) final states using the full Run 2 dataset (138 fb⁻¹ at 13 TeV). The analysis exploits the relative top–antitop velocity plus two angular observables sensitive to the parity and spin of the \(t\bar t\) system, comparing to an NNLO QCD baseline. The excess maps onto an observed total cross section of \(5.1\pm0.9\) pb and is consistent with a simplified NRQCD-motivated color-singlet pseudoscalar toponium state.

The significance here matters as much as the central value: the single-lepton channel is statistically independent of the dilepton measurement that first flagged the threshold enhancement, so this constitutes a genuine confirmation rather than a re-analysis of the same events. With two channels now converging on the same \(\sim\)5 pb, pseudoscalar-consistent signal, the “toponium” reading moves from a single anomaly toward a coherent picture — and sets up the competing elementary-scalar interpretation (Matsuoka’s top-philic 2HDM CP-odd Higgs) as the live alternative to test. The caveat is intrinsic to the modeling: the “toponium” is extracted against a fixed NNLO \(t\bar t\) prediction and fit to a simplified single-state NRQCD template, so the inferred cross section and its pseudoscalar identification are only as robust as that threshold-region theory baseline.

arXiv:2608.19547 §

Search for top quark FCNC in multilepton final states using an EFT approach

CMS Collaboration

CMS performs the first global top-FCNC search in which all 24 relevant dimension-six SMEFT Wilson coefficients (12 for \(tu\), 12 for \(tc\)) are constrained simultaneously in one likelihood, using 138 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV data in same-sign dilepton and trilepton final states. Signal is probed in both single-top production and \(t\bar t\) decay via Higgs-, \(Z\)-, photon-, gluon-, and directly lepton-pair-mediated FCNC vertices; BDT discriminants (binary for \(2\ell\)ss, multiclass jigsaw-reconstructed for \(3\ell\)) feed a maximum-likelihood fit where signal yields are quadratic polynomials in the WCs. No excess is seen; 68% and 95% intervals are set on all 24 coefficients and translated to branching-fraction limits.

The headline is methodological and archival: this is the first simultaneous treatment of the complete FCNC operator set — including the first-ever direct constraints on two-quark–two-lepton FCNC operators — rather than the usual single-mediator-at-a-time approach that ignores interference. The resulting \(\mathcal{B}(t\to Hq)\) and \(\mathcal{B}(t\to Zq)\) bounds are quoted as similar to or more restrictive than prior anomalous-coupling results, and the framework is explicitly pitched as the reference for future LHC combinations. This fits the digest’s EFT-as-default-language theme. Caveats are candid: chiral partners are assumed equal-strength and lepton flavors share common WCs to keep the fit tractable; gluon (\(c_{tG}\)) and photon (\(c_{tA}\)) operators are included only “for completeness” given the multilepton channel’s weak sensitivity; and a 50% uncertainty on \(t\bar tW\) reflects a known modeling tension.

arXiv:2608.19708 §

Tracing Vacuum Hadronization with Conserved Currents

Weiyao Ke et al.

Ke, Li, Li, Liu, and Shao argue that color triality — the requirement that an anti-fundamental Wilson-line endpoint be screened by matter of conjugate triality — forces the net electric-charge, baryon-number, and strangeness flows into a jet (or celestial region) to satisfy a jet-level Gell-Mann–Nishijima relation, \(\langle Q_{\rm flow}\rangle_f \simeq I_{3,f} + \tfrac12(\langle S_{\rm flow}\rangle_f + \langle B_{\rm flow}\rangle_f)\). Because these are moments of conserved currents, they are IR-safe and renormalization-scale independent at leading power: any shift from the initiating parton’s charge is purely nonperturbative “defect-charge” screening. The net jet baryon number directly reads out the diquark-to-quark vacuum ratio, \(\langle B_{\rm flow}\rangle_f \simeq r_{qq}/(1+r_{qq})\), and the strangeness flow encodes the strange-to-light ratio \(r_s\).

Pythia 8.3 (Lund string) and Herwig 7.3 (cluster) validate the relations in \(e^+e^-\) dijets across \(E_{\rm CM}=50\)–1000 GeV: parton-level flows recover \(Q_f\); hadron-level flows shift but obey the Gell-Mann–Nishijima diagonal in both generators. Scanning probStoUD recovers \(r_s\approx\) the input value across three independent slope projections; scanning probQQtoQ gives \(\langle B_{\rm flow}\rangle\) largely independent of the strange parameter, as predicted. This is the conserved-current-observable program (cf. the DELPHI one-point charge correlator) applied to hadronization microphysics, offering a flavor-resolved probe complementary to inclusive yield fits. Honest caveats: the extraction rests on a minimal one-quark/anti-diquark screening truncation (validated only to \(\sim\)9% by the event-level \(r_{topology}\) check), assumes light-flavor symmetry and independent flavor sampling, and the extracted \(r_{qq}\) deliberately does not equal probQQtoQ because of the state-dependent fragmentation history. Everything so far is generator-level; experimental tests are left to future work.

Observation of enhanced baryon production using strange hadrons in oxygen-oxygen collisions

CMS Collaboration

CMS measured \(p_T\)-differential \(K^0_S\) and \(\Lambda\) cross sections in OO and pp collisions at \(\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5.36\) TeV (6.8 nb\(^{-1}\) and 1.06 pb\(^{-1}\)), reconstructing the neutral strange hadrons via displaced two-track vertices. The nuclear modification factor \(R_{OO}\) for \(K^0_S\) tracks that of inclusive charged hadrons, but \(\Lambda\) baryons are enhanced over both in the intermediate range \(2<p_T<8\) GeV; above 10 GeV all three converge and sit well below unity, consistent with nPDF effects plus parton energy loss. The headline is the \(\Lambda/K^0_S\) cross-section ratio, which shows a pronounced enhancement in OO versus pp for \(2.3<p_T<5.2\) GeV, with a significance of 5.0–6.3σ across bins after accounting for correlated systematics. The ratio’s peak also shifts from \(p_T\approx2.3\) GeV in pp to \(\approx2.8\) GeV in OO.

This is the first observation of a baryon-to-meson enhancement in OO — the quark-coalescence/radial-flow signature of a deconfined medium — in a genuinely small system, without the event-activity selections that complicate high-multiplicity pp/pPb interpretations. It caps the season’s OO program (CMS charged-hadron suppression, ALICE/ATLAS flow, Υ suppression) with a clean coalescence handle. The qualitative trends mirror PbPb and AuAu, though “less pronounced in the smaller OO system.” Caveats are honest: the \(\Lambda\) feed-down correction (∼18% at 5 GeV) relies on scaling ALICE \(\Xi/\Omega\) ratios to the OO multiplicity, and the pp comparison inherits pileup and secondary-track corrections.

Search for anomalous couplings in WW and WZ production with single-lepton final states at √s = 13 TeV

CMS Collaboration

Using the full Run 2 dataset (138 fb\(^{-1}\)), CMS searched for dimension-six EFT effects in boosted \(WV\) (\(WW\)/\(WZ\)) production where one \(W\) decays leptonically and the hadronic boson merges into a single large-\(R\) jet, tagged with mass-decorrelated ParticleNet (43% \(V\)-tag efficiency, ∼1% mistag). In SMEFT, \(c_W\) and \(c_{\tilde W}\) reach \([-0.08,0.08]\) TeV\(^{-2}\) — the tightest triple-gauge bounds available — while the light-quark operators are competitive with, but not surpassing, inclusive-jet limits. This entrenches EFT with merged-jet substructure as the default diboson language, matching the digest’s theme. arXiv:2608.18506 §

Axion-like particle at the 10 GeV scale: Higgs decays to wide jets and photons

Bogdan A. Dobrescu et al.

Dobrescu and Roy build a renormalizable model where an ALP \(A_h\) couples to the SM only through the Higgs portal (\(-\tfrac{\mu_{H\phi}}{2v_\phi}H^\dagger H A_h A_h\)), with TeV-scale charge-2/3 colored scalars generating loop-induced \(A_h\to gg\) (BR ∼99%) and \(A_h\to\gamma\gamma\) (BR ∼0.15%, fixed by \(8\alpha^2/9\alpha_s^2\)). An effective portal coupling \(\mu_{H\phi}/v_\phi\approx10^{-2}\) yields \(B(h^0\to A_hA_h)\approx10\%\), below current undetected-width limits (\(<12\%\) from ATLAS under \(|\kappa_V|\le1\)). The key claim: \(h^0\to A_hA_h\to(gg)(\gamma\gamma)\) produces an automatically boosted diphoton at \(M_A\approx13.6\) GeV that satisfies existing low-mass diphoton selections. Delphes-level cutflows give acceptance×efficiency of 12.7% (CMS) and 11.9% (ATLAS), and the model’s \(1\sigma\) bands overlap both the CMS 3.5σ excess and the milder ATLAS ∼1.8σ at the same mass — reconciling the two, which under a singly-produced-ALP hypothesis appeared inconsistent.

If it holds, the CMS excess is not a lone diphoton resonance but a Higgs cascade with distinctive hadronic activity: the second \(A_h\) yields a wide jet \(J_{gg}\) with 2-prong substructure, and combining it with the diphoton reconstructs a 125-GeV peak (broad, from their simulation) — a background-suppression handle CMS/ATLAS could exploit. The companion \(h^0\to A_hA_h\to4g\) mode (two wide jets, or (2+2)-prong when boosted) is unsearched. Caveats: the fit relies on order-of-magnitude kinematic estimates and simplified isolation proxies (large PTRatioMax to bypass rejection), not a full experimental reproduction, and the ALP mass formula requires tuned but “natural” cutoffs.

arXiv:2608.16998 §

Quadruple-Higgs boson production at the high-luminosity LHC

Andreas Papaefstathiou et al.

First phenomenological study of \(gg\to hhhh\) as a probe of the Higgs self-couplings, targeting the eight-\(b\)-jet final state at \(\sqrt{s}=14\) TeV, \(3000\,\mathrm{fb}^{-1}\). The SM LO rate is tiny — \(\sigma(gg\to hhhh)\simeq1.24\times10^{-4}\) fb, a factor \(\sim350\) below triple-Higgs — but the four-Higgs amplitude carries more quartic-coupling structure. An XGBoost classifier trained on 52 reconstructed observables, combined with a five-bin Poisson likelihood over the \((c_3,d_4)\) plane (where \(c_3=\kappa_3-1\), \(d_4=\kappa_4-1\)), yields simultaneous 95% bounds \(-10.3<c_3<11.2\) and \(-222<d_4<234\). The signal hypothesis actually combines \(hhhh\), \(hhh+b\bar b\), and \(hh+4b\); at the SM point it expects only 0.39 signal vs 21.9 background events. They also show \(hhhh\to8b\) events with \(\geq6\) tags feed the ATLAS \(hhh\to6b\) region — with \(\sim89.5\%\) tag probability the four-Higgs contribution can reach 10–100% of the \(hhh\) rate in parts of the plane.

Why it matters: the quartic coupling \(\kappa_4\) is essentially unconstrained by phenomenology; this opens a channel and argues for jointly treating \(hhh\) and \(hhhh\) in inclusive multi-\(b\) analyses, complementing the ATLAS Higgs-combination push into loose corners. Caveats are heavy and honest: statistics-only limits, no detector/PDF/scale systematics, the \(hh+4b\) piece computed in \(m_t\to\infty\) with only \(c_3\) dependence, forced-splitting approximations, and sub-unit expected counts making asymptotic contours approximate. On a like-for-like basis the reach is comparable to ATLAS \(hhh\) in \(c_3\) but weaker in \(d_4\).

arXiv:2608.17685 §

Entanglement of back-to-back gluon pair

Lei Yang et al.

Full polarization density matrices for back-to-back gluon pairs in unpolarized \(pp\) collisions, computed at tree level for the \(q\bar q\to gg\) and \(gg\to gg\) channels. For \(q\bar q\to gg\), chirality conservation plus the MHV structure restrict the state to the \(|\Psi^\pm\rangle\) Bell sector; at perpendicular emission (\(\theta=\pi/2\)) the pair becomes the pure Bell state \(|\Psi^-\rangle\) — maximally entangled, concurrence \(\mathcal{C}=-c_q(\theta)\) reaching unity and \(S_{\max}>2\) (CHSH-violating) across \(0<\theta<\pi\). In contrast, \(gg\to gg\) is separable at all angles (\(\mathcal{C}=0\), \(S_{\max}\leq10\sqrt2/9<2\)) but retains an appreciable linear-polarization correlation, \(c_g(\pi/2)=1/9\approx11\%\). The observable link is the four-point energy correlator (E4C): contracting the pair density matrix with collinear splitting kernels maps the correlation onto a \(\cos2\Delta\Phi\) azimuthal modulation between the two branching planes, with asymmetry \(\mathscr{A}^{\bm\kappa}=\tfrac12(C_{xx'}+C_{yy'})a^{\bm\kappa}(n)\).

Why it matters: it extends the collider quantum-information program into the gluon sector, showing entanglement is generated purely by QCD helicity structure rather than a color-singlet vertex as in \(H\to gg\). The distinction between an entangled \(q\bar q\)-initiated state and a merely correlated \(gg\)-initiated one is physically sharp. Caveat: the inclusive E4C signal suffers strong cancellations among \(q\bar q q\bar q\), \(q\bar q gg\), \(gggg\) splitting channels; heavy-flavor tagging in both jets is needed to isolate \(Q\bar Q Q\bar Q\) and recover sensitivity. Extraction relies on the one-step collinear-splitting approximation, and the tagged sample is expected to be \(gg\to gg\)-dominated with \(q\bar q\to gg\) as irreducible Born contamination.

arXiv:2608.17015 §

Astrophysical Graviton Squeezing Can Be Hidden in the Far-Field

Cheng-Jun Fang et al.

Using spatial quantum-optics methods extended to gravity, the authors compute the second-order coherence \(g^{(2)}\) accessible to a single far-field detector for gravitons emitted in a macroscopically squeezed state from a localized astrophysical source. An EFT description of squeezed-graviton generation yields Bogoliubov coefficients that inherit the source’s momentum envelope; the far-field projection (retarded pole extraction, angular stationary phase) shows a local observer effectively measures the asymptotic out-state along a single line of sight. The key result is a “same-cone” kinematic conflict: for a non-relativistic source the squeezing matrix peaks on the anti-diagonal (\(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}\approx0\), back-to-back pairs), so the anomalous coherence \(M_D\) requires one internal momentum simultaneously near \(\mathbf{p}\) and \(-\mathbf{p}'\) — incompatible for same-direction detection. The result is \(g^{(2)}-2\sim\mathcal{O}[\exp(-2d\,\mathcal{E}/k_*)]+\mathcal{O}(L_D/r)\), so a globally squeezed state appears classically thermal (\(g^{(2)}=2\)) locally.

Why it matters: this is the third graviton-quantumness no-go the memory has tracked (after Bao et al. and Miyauchi et al.), attacking macroscopic squeezing from the far-field-observability angle rather than production. Global squeezing does not guarantee local detectability. The authors frame it as a conditional no-go requiring three conditions: non-relativistic source, single localized detector, near-flat propagation. Each suggests an escape: relativistic/collinear sources (BH binaries, jets) could restore \(\mathcal{E}/k_*\sim1\); multi-detector networks probing both partner cones recover cross-correlations (feasible for a nearby solar axion halo); and strong-curvature lensing near compact objects can redirect back-to-back pairs into one cone. A concurrent independent preprint (ref. 51) reached consistent conclusions via a different framework.

Tuesday, August 18, 2026

arXiv:2608.15357 §

First measurement of the one-point charge correlator in e⁺e⁻ collisions at √(s)=91.2 GeV with DELPHI Open Data

Jingyu Zhang et al.

Zhang et al. deliver the first-ever measurement of the one-point charge correlator — the net electric-charge flux \(\mathcal{Q}(\theta)\) into the detector as a function of polar angle relative to the \(e^-\) beam — from 61 pb⁻¹ of archival DELPHI Open Data (46 pb⁻¹ in 1994, 15 pb⁻¹ in 1995). This is a jet-free, hemisphere-free, IRC-safe reformulation of the hadronic forward-backward asymmetry: the parity-odd \(\cos\theta\) coupling structure shows up directly as a \(\sin(2\theta)\) modulation in the charge-weighted track density (tracks with \(p_T>2\) GeV, \(20°\le\theta\le160°\)). Detector effects are handled in two stages — a PYTHIA 8.3-based correction for track-finding, fakes and charge misID, then a data-driven tag-and-probe measurement of the residual data–MC charge-misreconstruction bias in \(Z\to\tau^+\tau^-\) events, where charge conservation fixes the probe’s true sign. The fully corrected result exhibits the expected \(\sin(2\theta)\) shape, negative in the forward hemisphere, crossing zero at 90°, and agrees with PYTHIA 8.3 within uncertainties.

Why it matters: this establishes experimental viability of a genuinely new precision-EW handle, one whose relation to the \(Z\) couplings is not mediated by jet-charge model dependence — the very ambiguity blamed for the persistent \(A_{FB}^b\) tension. The construction extends to heavy-flavor currents where tensions are largest, and prefigures Tera-\(Z\) programs at FCC-ee/CEPC. The honest caveat is prominent: this is a proof-of-principle, not a competitive extraction. Systematics (27.6%/35.6% at \(\theta=55°/125°\)) dominate over statistics and are set by the τ control-sample size; the parity-odd bias component is bounded, not measured to vanish. Sagitta and photon-conversion calibrations are the named next steps.

arXiv:2608.14703 §

Evidence for parity violation in gravitational fields

V. Gharibyan et al.

Gharibyan et al. analyze 314,896 Compton spectra from the HERA transverse polarimeter (2004–2007), tagged by 514.5 nm laser helicity, and report a laser-helicity-dependent asymmetry in the Compton-edge energy (\(\omega_{max}=13.9\) GeV) of \(A=(4.89\pm0.52)\times10^{-5}\), claimed at \(9\sigma\). Within a phenomenological framework where the gravitational coupling acquires a helicity-dependent term \(G_s=G+(\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{P}/P)\Delta G_{\mathcal P}\), the modified energy-momentum relation shifts \(\omega_{max}\) oppositely for the two helicities, and the \(\gamma^2\) amplification factor sets the sensitivity. They translate the asymmetry into a helicity-dependent gravitational-potential difference \(\Delta U_{\mathcal P}=(1.71\pm0.036\pm0.187)\times10^{-14}\), i.e. \(\Delta G_{\mathcal P}/G=(5.7\pm0.6)\times10^{-10}\) using summed local potentials (dominated by the Virgo Supercluster). The signal is isotropic in right ascension (constant fit checked against 64 bins), which they argue excludes Lorentz-violating vector theories, and they estimate instrumental false asymmetries from transverse/longitudinal beam polarization and linear-light contamination to be sub-dominant.

Why it matters: if real, this would be the first high-energy test of a discrete symmetry in gravity showing violation, with speculative implications for galactic rotation curves via a chiral-gravity shift of the 21 cm line — potentially bearing on dark matter. But this is an extraordinary claim resting on a \(5\times10^{-5}\) edge shift in a polarimeter never built to measure absolute energy asymmetries. The authors themselves flag “hidden systematic factors”: helicity-dependent detector gain (estimated opposite-sign from bremsstrahlung fits, but uncorrected due to unknown beam offsets on an inhomogeneous calorimeter) and helicity-correlated laser spatial jitter convolved with the lepton phase space. The digest’s “read skeptically” is well-earned.

arXiv:2608.16875 §

Truncation of the Radial Ladder in Heavy Quarkonia

Wen-Xuan Zhang et al.

Zhang, Luo and Liu ask whether the radial excitation ladder of quarkonia terminates, solving a screened relativized Godfrey–Isgur Hamiltonian for \(c\bar c\) and \(b\bar b\) via the Gaussian expansion method (\(N_{max}=20\), \(r_{max}=30\) fm) up to \(n=14\). Screened confinement replaces the linear term’s unbounded rise with a plateau \(M_\infty=m_1+m_2+b/\mu+c\), giving limiting masses of 4.74 GeV (\(c\bar c\)) and 11.67 GeV (\(b\bar b\)). Crucially, while masses saturate — adjacent gaps dropping below 10 MeV — RMS radii keep diverging to \(\sim\)10–50 fm, an order of magnitude past the confinement scale. This “mass–radius decoupling” defines a breakdown of the \(q\bar q\) picture. A threshold criterion (min gap \(<\)10 MeV with radius increments \(>\)3/2 fm), corroborated by CUSUM/two-segment changepoint analysis of \(\log(\Delta R/\Delta M)\) and a resolvability index \(\chi_n=\Delta M_n R_n\) collapsing below unity, locates operational upper limits at \(n\approx8\)–10 for charmonium (\(D\)-waves first, then \(P\), then \(S\)) and \(n\approx12\)–13 for bottomonium. Staged \(\chi^2\) fits give RMS mass residuals of 34/24 MeV (10/4 MeV for low-lying states), with the charmonium screening length \(\mu^{-1}\approx1.4\) fm inside the lattice string-breaking range.

Why it matters: this is the first quantitative answer to a long-standing spectroscopy question, and it is directly testable — no new charmonia should appear above \(\sim\)4.7 GeV, and BESIII/Belle II/LHCb can probe this. Caveats are candidly stated: high-\(n\) bottomonium shows round-off artifacts; the thresholds are empirical; and the truncation value is model-input-dependent (alternative fits give 4.61–4.83 GeV). Formal parameter errors are not quoted. The manuscript was substantially AI-drafted (Grok, GPT-5.4), per the authors’ declaration.
